-> $\theta$ <-
<note>This central mark is an ink stamp in the shape of the Greek letter **Theta**. In the context of library science and archival history, such symbols were often used as "ownership marks" or "processing stamps." A Theta mark specifically was sometimes used to indicate that a volume had been "deaccessioned" (officially removed from a library's collection) or to categorize it within a specific thematic section, such as Theology.</note>

# PROTESTATION

In whatever I may say in this book, I submit to what our Mother, the Holy Roman Church <note>Teresa emphasizes her loyalty to the Catholic Church to protect herself and her work from the Spanish Inquisition, which was highly suspicious of mystical writings during the 16th century.</note>, teaches; if I write anything contrary to this, it will be unintentionally. Therefore, I beg of the <term>theologians</term> <note>Scholars who specialize in the study of God and religious doctrine.</note> who are to read it, for the love of our Lord, to examine it carefully and to correct any such faults as well as any other defects it may possess. If it has any good in it, may it be for the honour and glory of God and the service of His most holy Mother, our patron and our Lady <note>Refers to the Virgin Mary, the patroness of the Carmelite Order to which Teresa belonged.</note>, whose <term>habit</term> <note>The distinctive clothing worn by members of a religious order; for Teresa, this was the brown and white dress of the Carmelites.</note> I wear, although most unworthy of it.

<note>This formal declaration of faith, while not found in Teresa’s original handwritten manuscripts, was written by her for the specific purpose of the printed edition published in Evora, Portugal, after her death in 1582.</note>

<summary>This page presents Saint Teresa of Avila’s formal "Protestation," a standard 16th-century declaration where she submits her writing to the judgment and correction of the Catholic Church.</summary>

<keywords>Protestation, Holy Roman Church, theologians, Saint Teresa of Avila, Evora edition, Carmelite habit, orthodoxy, Virgin Mary</keywords>

# PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.

->THEOTONIO DE BRAGANZA, ARCHBISHOP OF EVORA<-
->IN PORTUGAL (HOWEVER UNWORTHY), TO THE<-
->PIOUS AND DEVOUT MOTHERS, THE NUNS OF<-
->THE PRIMITIVE RULE OF OUR LADY OF MOUNT<-
->CARMEL.<-
<note>Theotonio de Braganza (1530–1602) was an influential Portuguese prelate who was a close friend and supporter of St. Teresa of Avila.</note>

->HEALTH IN THE LORD JESUS CHRIST.<-

AMONG many favours I have received from our Lord, not the least was my intimate acquaintance with the very Reverend Mother Teresa of Jesus, now in Glory <note>A pious reference to the fact that Teresa had already died (in 1582) and was believed to be in heaven.</note>, because in her I have witnessed the splendour of the gifts of our Lord and of His divine grace. These are evidenced by the convents founded by her according to the <term>primitive Rule</term> <note>The original, stricter set of regulations for the Carmelite Order, which emphasized extreme silence, fasting, and poverty before later "mitigations" or relaxations were introduced.</note> of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, without any mitigation <note>In a monastic context, a "mitigation" is a formal easing or lessening of the strictness of the religious rule.</note>, but with as much religious observance and recollection, with such austerity, such unceasing prayer and as much manual labour as our weak human nature is able to bear. She herself was a living example of that manner of life, and she fully trusted that our Lord would grant to His servants spiritual and bodily strength to

<summary>The Archbishop of Evora introduces the first edition of St. Teresa’s writings, praising her holy life and her successful restoration of the strict, original Carmelite Rule.</summary>
<keywords>Theotonio de Braganza, Archbishop of Evora, St. Teresa of Avila, Discalced Carmelites, Primitive Rule, monastic reform, 16th century Portugal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface by Archbishop Theotonio de Braganza. On the previous page, he described St. Teresa’s dedication to the primitive Carmelite rule and her hope that her followers would find the strength to continue her work.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>

# PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.

persevere to the end. So great were the charity <note>In this context, charity refers to the theological concept of "caritas"—a selfless, divine love for God and others.</note> and fervour of this Mother, such her solicitude for the perfection of her daughters <note>The nuns in the convents St. Teresa founded were often referred to as her "spiritual daughters."</note>, that she did not content herself with the good example and the instructions she gave while alive, but wished that, even after her death, her words might remain and continue the work she had begun on earth. As one truly hungering after our Lord, and greatly experienced in all that concerns the <term>religious life</term> <note>A technical term for the life led by those who have taken formal vows in a religious order, such as poverty, chastity, and obedience.</note>, she wrote the advice and the explanations contained in this book, so that the sadness caused to the nuns by her bodily absence might be counterbalanced by her spiritual presence ; for, indeed, she seems living even in the dead letters <note>This is a metaphor suggesting that her vibrant spirit is so well-captured in her writing that she feels alive to the reader, despite the "dead" or inanimate nature of ink on paper.</note>. This, then, is one of the consolations with which her spiritual daughters may alleviate the sorrow caused by her death ; another being the certainty that, where she now is, she will not abandon those whom she so ardently loved, because, so far from being smaller, charity is much greater in heaven than on earth.

It is no small consolation to see, albeit after her death, her spirit still alive in the doctrine of this book, which she composed through zeal for the spiritual improvement of her daughters, and which she earnestly requested me to get printed <note>The first official collection of St. Teresa's works was printed in 1588, six years after her death.</note>.

There being various manuscript copies, it was unavoidable that there should be many passages at variance <note>Before the printing press was widely used for her works, her writings were copied by hand. This led to many "variations" or errors where different scribes accidentally changed her words.</note> with what she had written ; this could only be obviated by printing the whole

<summary>Archbishop Theotonio explains that St. Teresa’s writings serve as a "spiritual presence" to comfort her nuns after her death. He also notes the necessity of printing her works to ensure accuracy, as handwritten copies had begun to differ from her original words.</summary>

# PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.

work, and therefore I willingly complied with her request.

In this book, then, she first recommends the practice of **<term>prayer</term>** <note>The act of lifting the mind and heart to God in communication.</note> and **<term>meditation</term>** <note>A form of mental prayer where one reflects deeply on sacred truths or the life of Christ.</note> which give a taste of that sweetness reserved by God for those who fear Him, rendering them prompt and ready for all the works of virtue. For, just as Satan, with the allurement of pleasure, leads men to vice, so the **Holy Ghost** <note>The Holy Spirit.</note> opposes to the sensual a spiritual pleasure whereby He inclines them to the practice of virtue.

She further strongly recommends the **<term>mortification</term>** <note>From the Latin word meaning "to put to death"; the practice of self-denial or physical discipline used to overcome selfish desires and strengthen the spirit.</note> of our unruly appetites and our self-will, which is brought about by prayer which softens the heart, and by its sweetness compensates for the bitterness inseparable from mortification. These two virtues might be termed frankincense and myrrh, so often mentioned in the **Canticle of Canticles** <note>Also known as the Song of Solomon, a book of the Bible often interpreted by mystics as a poem describing the love between God and the human soul.</note>; incense, having the property of rising into the air, represents prayer, and myrrh, which has a bitter taste, mortification.

Moreover, she recommends in this book **<term>interior recollection</term>** <note>A state of focused inner prayer where the soul withdraws from external distractions to find God within.</note> and the withdrawal from conversation with worldly people, were they even our own parents, according to the words of the prophet:

> “Hearken, O daughter, and see, and incline thy ear; and forget thy people and thy father’s house” (Psalm xliv. 2). <note>In modern Bibles, this is typically cited as Psalm 45:10.</note>

She recommends manual labour with a view to lessen such conversations and to enable the nuns, who ought to be lovers of the

<summary>Archbishop Theotonio outlines St. Teresa’s spiritual program, emphasizing how the "sweetness" of prayer balances the "bitterness" of self-discipline (mortification). He uses the biblical metaphors of incense and myrrh to describe the necessary balance between a soul's ascent to God and its detachment from the world.</summary>

<keywords>prayer, meditation, mortification, interior recollection, manual labor, Canticle of Canticles, spiritual discipline, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface by Archbishop Theotonio de Braganza. He transitions from discussing prayer and mortification to the practical and communal aspects of St. Teresa’s Reform, specifically manual labor and the strictness of the rule.</meta>

<page-num>x</page-num>

->**PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.**<-

**<term>poverty of Christ</term>** <note>The voluntary renunciation of all personal property to live in imitation of Jesus, who owned nothing.</note>, to provide for their own needs without having recourse to their families for assistance. **<term>St. Paul</term>** <note>The Apostle Paul, who famously supported his ministry by working as a tentmaker so as not to be a burden to the early Christian communities.</note> himself, notwithstanding his care of all the churches, provided by the labour of his hands for his own requirements and those of his companions; how, then, could persons who have no such charge dispense themselves, with a good conscience, from this duty?

<margin>v</margin> She likewise recommends the rigour and the austerity of the monastic life, and this rigour has ever been maintained. For the first obligation of religious persons, who have consecrated their body and soul to Christ their Spouse, is to **follow the Lamb whithersoever He goeth** <note>original: "sequi Agnum quocumque ierit"; a quote from Revelation 14:4, symbolizing total devotion and the imitation of Christ's suffering.</note>, which means to imitate and follow Him, and we know that her whole life was a perpetual bearing of the cross, and that she was exceedingly zealous for the rigour and strictness of the **<term>Order</term>** <note>The Discalced Carmelites, the reformed branch of the Carmelite Order founded by St. Teresa.</note>, labouring hard that it might ever remain firm and never become weakened, for, if the least **<term>mitigation</term>** <note>A formal relaxation or softening of the strictness of a religious rule.</note> were allowed to creep in, **<term>relaxation</term>** <note>The decline of discipline or the abandonment of original strict observances within a monastery.</note> would inevitably follow until the whole edifice came to grief; for our nature has a tendency towards ease, and drags us down. This austerity has a further advantage, inasmuch as those who might choose the religious life, not for the sake of God, but for earthly considerations, could never select a manner of life so entirely at variance with the inclinations of human nature. Just as the sea casts out dead bodies, so a strict Order frightens away those who, without being...

<summary>Archbishop de Braganza explains St. Teresa's insistence on manual labor for self-sufficiency and the vital importance of maintaining strict monastic discipline to prevent the Order from losing its spiritual purpose.</summary>
<keywords>poverty, St. Paul, manual labor, monastic rigour, Discalced Carmelites, mitigation, spiritual discipline, following the Lamb</keywords>

<page-num>xi</page-num>

->**PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.**<-

...called by God, are only guided by human considerations. It follows that none will choose it but those ready to give up the world for the sake of Christ, and these, so far from chafing under the **<term>recollection</term>** <note>A state of inward prayer and spiritual focus, withdrawing from external distractions.</note> and **<term>austerity</term>** <note>The practice of self-denial and rigorous discipline within the religious life.</note> of this kind of life, cherish it, and these are precisely the class of persons who maintain the **<term>Order</term>** <note>The Discalced Carmelites, the reformed branch of the Carmelite Order founded by St. Teresa.</note> in its integrity. This Mother <note>St. Teresa of Avila, the foundress.</note> also wished her **<term>nuns</term>** to be few in number, because small means are large enough for a few, and thus will be warded off the greatest danger that could befall a **<term>religious community</term>** <note>A group of people living together under a specific religious rule, such as a convent or monastery.</note>, namely, that of paying more attention to the **<term>dowry</term>** <note>A sum of money or property brought by a woman to her convent to help support the community; Teresa feared that accepting women just for their money would ruin the spiritual quality of the house.</note> than to the spirit and devotion of **<term>aspirants</term>** <note>Candidates or those seeking to enter a religious order.</note>; otherwise some persons unfit for the **<term>religious life</term>** might be admitted. And as they must needs be strict in the choice of those whom they receive, they must be prompt in dismissing those who have not the required qualities. For this reason she thought it imprudent to receive nuns coming from a great distance, as it might not be convenient to send them back to their homes if the necessity arose.

These are the points, very reverend Mothers, that you will learn from this book, and which I have learnt from the life and the example of your Mother, together with many other particulars about the gifts and virtues with which our Lord had enriched her. One of these was her wonderful obedience to her **<term>spiritual fathers</term>** <note>Priests or confessors who provided guidance on matters of the soul and religious practice; obedience to them was a cornerstone of Teresa's discipline.</note>, which was such that sometimes, when she knew the will of God

<summary>This page outlines St. Teresa’s philosophy on convent management, emphasizing small community sizes and the importance of choosing candidates based on spiritual merit rather than the size of their dowry. It also introduces the theme of Teresa's profound obedience to her spiritual directors.</summary>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface by Archbishop Theotonio de Braganza. Having previously discussed the discernment of vocations and the management of the Carmelite houses, he now highlights St. Teresa’s specific spiritual gifts—particularly her obedience and her ability to guide others in prayer—before concluding with a request for the nuns' prayers for his own soul.</meta>

<page-num>xii</page-num>

->**PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.**<-

...to be different from theirs, she obeyed them all the same, our Lord approving her manner of acting and testifying that He preferred obedience to **<term>confessors</term>** <note>Priests authorized to hear confessions and provide spiritual guidance, a role essential in the life of a Catholic nun.</note> and **<term>superiors</term>** <note>The appointed leaders within the religious order or the Church hierarchy.</note>.

She possessed another special gift of our Lord, namely this, that all the persons dealing with her changed their lives and advanced in virtue, as has been clearly seen in some **<term>religious</term>** <note>Members of religious orders, such as monks, friars, or nuns.</note> deficient in gravity and learning, and also in many other persons. Not less remarkable was another gift she held from God, which enabled her to lead her visitors to the exercise of prayer and meditation, so that in a very short time and with great facility they even became masters in that art, provided they had the necessary disposition.

Owing to the great desire I have that your Reverences should in all things imitate her and faithfully guard the treasure entrusted to you, I wished to remind you of these matters, trusting that our Lord, Who hath given you so rich a share of His spirit, will preserve it within you. Thus you will ever advance from virtue to virtue until you attain perfection, and will not be far from the glorious sight of your most sweet **<term>Bridegroom</term>** <note>A traditional mystical image of Jesus Christ, representing the soul's intimate union with God.</note>, our Lord. For myself I desire no other reward than that the nuns into whose hands this book may fall should commend me to our Lord, asking Him that, since **<term>His Majesty</term>** <note>A respectful title used frequently by St. Teresa and her contemporaries to refer to God or Christ.</note> has placed me in this high position, He may also give me the grace so to fill it that after this mortal life I may come...

<summary>This page concludes the preface, praising St. Teresa's radical obedience and her miraculous ability to teach prayer to others. The author, Archbishop Theotonio, exhorts the nuns to follow her example and requests their prayers for his own spiritual welfare.</summary>

<keywords>St. Teresa of Avila, obedience, confessors, spiritual direction, meditation, Bridegroom, His Majesty, Archbishop Theotonio de Braganza, religious life</keywords>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>

# PREFACE OF THE FIRST EDITION.

...to the enjoyment of His glory, which we believe this **<term>blessed Mother</term>** <note>A title of deep respect for St. Teresa of Avila, the foundress of the Discalced Carmelites, who had died a few years before this preface was written.</note> already to possess. I trust she will not forget those that loved her during life, nor those devoted to her since she has gone.

May Christ ever dwell in the souls of your **<term>Reverences</term>** <note>A formal honorific used here to address the community of Carmelite nuns to whom the book is dedicated.</note> with the abundance of His grace.

->THEOTONIO, ARCHBISHOP OF EVORA.<-
<note>Theotonio de Braganza (1530–1602) was a significant ecclesiastical figure in Portugal and a supporter of the Carmelite reform. He was instrumental in publishing the first collected works of St. Teresa in 1588.</note>

<summary>This final page of the preface concludes with Archbishop Theotonio's prayerful wish for St. Teresa's intercession and a blessing for the Carmelite sisters. It marks the formal opening to the first published collection of her mystical writings.</summary>

<keywords>Theotonio de Braganza, Archbishop of Evora, St. Teresa of Avila, intercession, grace, Carmelite nuns, preface, first edition</keywords>

## CHAPTER VIII
Treats of the great advantage of <term>detaching</term> ourselves from all <term>created things</term>, whether interior or exterior. Of the great benefits gained by those who have left the world by severing themselves from their relatives, and how they will meet with far truer friendship instead of that which they have given up . . . . . . 56
<note>"Detaching" is the spiritual practice of releasing one's emotional or physical dependency on anything that might distract from God. "Created things" refers to anything in the material or emotional world—such as wealth, family, or personal reputation—that is not God Himself.</note>

<note>"Abnegation" refers to self-denial or the renouncing of one's own will and ego in order to better serve God's will.</note>

# CONTENTS.

## CHAPTER XI

->PAGE<-

Continues to treat of <term>mortification</term> and explains how to gain it during illness. How little this life should be valued by one who truly loves God . . . . . . . 69
<note>"Mortification" refers to the spiritual practice of "dying to oneself." It involves disciplining the body’s desires and the ego’s demands—such as by staying patient and cheerful during a painful illness—to focus entirely on God rather than one's own comfort.</note>

## CHAPTER XII

That one who truly loves God must care little for life or honour. That one who seeks after <term>perfection</term> must despise honours . . . . . . . . . 73
<note>In this context, "perfection" refers to "Christian perfection"—a state of spiritual maturity where a person is fully surrendered to God’s will and motivated by pure love.</note>

## CHAPTER XIII

Continues the subject of mortification: that a nun must avoid the touchiness and wisdom of the world, in order to attain true wisdom . . . . . . . 80
<note>The "wisdom of the world" refers to being overly concerned with one's social status, reputation, or "common sense" that prioritizes self-interest over the radical humility required in the convent.</note>

## CHAPTER XIV

Treats of the great importance of not <term>professing</term> any one of a spirit contrary to the qualifications here described . . . . 87
<note>To "profess" is the formal act of a sister taking her final, permanent vows to join the religious order. The author warns that admitting someone who lacks the proper temperament or spiritual focus can harm the entire community.</note>

## CHAPTER XV

The great advantage of not excusing ourselves even when unjustly blamed . . . . . . . . 90
<note>This refers to the practice of "silent humility," where a person accepts a correction or even a false accusation without arguing, as a way to imitate the humility of Christ.</note>

## CHAPTER XVI

The game of <term>Chess</term> . . . . . . . . 91
<note>The author uses chess as a famous metaphor for the spiritual life. She explains that just as a player uses strategy to "checkmate" the King, the soul uses virtues and prayer to "win over" the Lord and achieve union with Him.</note>

<note>The "Our Father" is the central prayer taught by Jesus. Teresa emphasizes that because God is "Father" to all, social status or "noble birth"—which was very important in 16th-century Spain—has no place in a religious community.</note>

xxiii

# INTRODUCTION.

THE <term>convent of St. Joseph at Avila</term> <note>This was the first house of the Discalced (or "barefoot") Carmelite reform, established by Teresa to return to a simpler, more prayerful way of life.</note> having been inaugurated on August 24, 1562, and the storms occasioned by its foundation having somewhat subsided, St. Teresa received permission from the <term>Provincial</term> <note>A high-ranking superior responsible for a geographic district (province) of a religious order.</note>, <term>Fray</term> <note>A Spanish title for a friar or monk.</note> Angel de Salazar, to leave the monastery of the Incarnation and join her new community; she crossed the threshold of that “Paradise,” as our Lord had vouchsafed to call it, about Mid-Lent 1563 <note>The midpoint of the forty-day period of fasting and penance before Easter.</note>, never to leave the <term>enclosure</term> <note>The strict rule of remaining within the convent walls to maintain a life of secluded prayer.</note> again—as she fervently hoped. She did not know then that God had destined her to more arduous work which would compel her to sally forth and establish convent after convent in distant parts of Spain. Her sojourn at St. Joseph’s only lasted four and a half years, but, as she says, it was the happiest time of her life. The convent was small and poor, the observance as strict as human nature, strengthened by grace, can bear, but she enjoyed to the full the peace which, after the many struggles graphically described in the *Life* <note>original: "Vida"; This refers to St. Teresa’s celebrated autobiography.</note>, had at length been granted her.

The visitor who has the privilege of penetrating

<summary>This page introduces the history of the Convent of St. Joseph in Avila, marking St. Teresa's move from the Monastery of the Incarnation to her new foundation in 1563.</summary>

xxiv

# INTRODUCTION.

into the hallowed enclosure will have to reconstruct in his mind the convent as it was in St. Teresa’s time. The handsome church was not yet begun, and what is now called the primitive chapel was in reality built at a later period, though undoubtedly on the original lines. For even now it is only about twelve paces long and eight paces wide, and the <term>sanctuary</term> <note>The sacred space around the altar.</note>, the <term>sacristy</term> <note>The room where vestments and sacred vessels are kept.</note>, and the <term>nuns’ choir</term> <note>The private area where the sisters gathered to sing the liturgy, usually separated from the public by a grille.</note> are of diminutive proportions.

The main building of the convent, in the shape of a <term>quadrangle</term> <note>A square courtyard enclosed by buildings on all four sides.</note>, is likewise a later addition: in the Saint’s time a few old and small houses served for a convent, and the kitchen, the <term>refectory</term> <note>The communal dining hall of the convent.</note>, and other dependencies being on a lower level than the surrounding land, were both dark and damp. 

There were then no <term>lay sisters</term> <note>Members of the order who traditionally performed manual labor so that the choir nuns could focus entirely on prayer.</note> to do the housework. The few choir nuns took it in turns to see to the washing, the scrubbing, the service in kitchen and <term>scullery</term> <note>A small room next to the kitchen used for washing dishes and dirty household work.</note>, and Teresa, who had been nominated <term>Prioress</term> <note>The female superior or head of the convent.</note> by the Bishop, and retained that office until her death (employing a <term>Vicaress</term> <note>A deputy who acts as the head of the house when the Prioress is absent.</note> during her prolonged absences), took her share, and more than her share, in the common work. 

Never was the convent so scrupulously clean as when it was her turn to do the scrubbing. Never was the food so tasty as when she did the kitchen, though she might have been seen in an ecstasy, saucepan in hand. The <term>Divine Office</term> <note>The daily cycle of prayers, psalms, and hymns that the nuns were required to pray at specific hours.</note> was performed with a devotion and a refinement which were at once a source of edification <note>Moral or spiritual improvement.</note> for the faithful

<page-num>XXV</page-num>
-># INTRODUCTION. XXV<-

and a revelation to the clerics who came to assist at it.

The convent was unendowed <note>It had no permanent financial endowment or fixed income, relying entirely on charity.</note>, and voluntary alms were anything but abundant; so the nuns spent long hours in spinning in order to earn their livelihood, and we know that Teresa herself was busy with the **distaff** <note>A tool used in spinning to hold the unspun wool or flax.</note>, not only when alone in her cell but also when in the parlour with visitors. How, after all that, there remained any time for anything else is a wonder; but the fact is that during these four and a half years she did find the time to write two works which have brought light and peace into the hearts of thousands. She had been advised by Don Francisco Soto y Salazar, the <term>Grand Inquisitor</term> <note>The lead official of the Inquisition, the office responsible for maintaining Catholic orthodoxy.</note>, to write a full history of her life, and to send it to **Blessed Juan d’Avila** <note>A celebrated Spanish priest and mystic; his approval was vital for validating Teresa's spiritual visions.</note>, the man who, since the death of **St. Peter of Alcantara** <note>A Franciscan friar known for his extreme asceticism and for being a close spiritual advisor to Teresa.</note>, was better able than anybody in Spain to judge about spiritual experiences. 

She began it probably soon after having taken up her residence at St. Joseph’s, and completed it in May or June, or, at the latest, in the course of the summer of 1565. The place where she wrote this marvellous work is still shown to the visitor. The **cell** <note>A nun's small, private living quarters.</note> inhabited by her has been left exactly as she left it herself when last she bade farewell to the convent. With the exception of a bed it contains no furniture: no table to put her books on, no chair to sit on. When writing she knelt or sat on the floor, the paper lying on a small

<summary>This page describes the extreme poverty and manual labor of St. Teresa and her nuns at St. Joseph’s, noting that despite these hardships, she wrote her autobiography (The Book of Her Life) while sitting on the floor of her unfurnished cell.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s description of the physical environment where St. Teresa wrote her works, transitioning from the description of her cell to the specific conditions of her writing desk and the motivations behind her compositions.</meta>

<page-num>xxvi</page-num>

-># INTRODUCTION.<-

projection of the wall in the window recess. Glass windows would have been too great a luxury for St. Joseph’s convent <note>The first convent of the Discalced Carmelite reform, established by Teresa to return to a life of strict poverty and prayer.</note>; instead of these the nuns used canvas fixed in a frame and fastened in the open window. Had it not been for the fire burning within her, her fingers must have been numbed during the long evenings in cold Avila <note>Avila is located on a high plateau in central Spain; its winters are notoriously long, windy, and freezing.</note> when she was writing, at a prodigious speed, at a window practically open.

The sisters, though perhaps free from inquisitiveness, could not have helped noticing how much their saintly <term>Prioress</term> <note>The woman in charge of a priory or convent; the local superior.</note> was writing, and no doubt were told she was composing a work on prayer. Prayer being the very object for which they had joined her, and, at the same time, a subject on which careful and detailed instruction is required, no less than on the most recondite science <note>In this context, "science" refers to a systematic body of knowledge; "recondite" means obscure or deeply hidden.</note>, they were naturally anxious to read that work. There is so much in it that concerns any one desirous of doing more than merely repeat a set form of prayers. They lived a <term>contemplative life</term> <note>A form of religious life focused on internal prayer, silence, and solitude to achieve union with God.</note>, and they required a guiding hand. However, besides the incomparable treatise on the various phases of the contemplative life, it contains much that is intensely personal, and if Teresa was always anxious to lay bare her innermost soul before those who held the place of God in her respect <note>This refers to her confessors and spiritual directors, whom she viewed as representatives of divine authority.</note>, she was by no means willing to make known to the world, at least during her life-time, the wonderful graces <note>In mystical theology, "graces" refers to supernatural gifts or favors, such as visions, ecstasies, or divine locutions.</note> showered on her. As to her failings and short-

-># INTRODUCTION.<-

comings, she was more than ready to proclaim them, even in exaggerated terms; but Divine favours—no! least of all to those over whom she exercised superiority: before these she only delighted in humbling herself.

However, the nuns would not be put off; they appealed to the <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest who hears a person's confession and provides spiritual guidance; in this case, a high-ranking theological advisor.</note>, **Fray Domingo Bañez**, who commanded the **Prioress** to write one more book, embodying the homely instructions she was wont to give her daughters. An order coming from God’s representative always found ready compliance on her part. Thus, no sooner was the *Life* <note>Referring to her autobiography, "The Life of Saint Teresa of Jesus."</note> completed than she took up her pen once more.

Not much reflection was required about the title of the new work. “**The Way of Perfection**” was a favourite expression of the Saint’s. She found it in the *Imitation of Christ* <note>original: "Imitatio Christi"; a 15th-century spiritual classic by Thomas à Kempis.</note>¹: “Hence it comes to pass that I recognise the way of perfection”; and also in one of the Meditations of **St. Peter of Alcantara** <note>A Franciscan mystic and close friend of Teresa who encouraged her reform movements.</note>²: “But the servant of God that expecteth merit and comfort in the way of perfection,” while she herself had repeatedly used it in the *Life*: “Some of them began to walk in the way of perfection³”; “it requires greater courage in one not yet perfect to walk in the way of perfection”; “I cannot understand what it is that makes men afraid of walking in the way of perfection.”

¹ *Imitation of Christ*, book 3, chapter 55, section 3.
² *Meditations of St. Peter of Alcantara*, translated by Giles Willoughby, page 199 (Liverpool, 1843).
³ *Life*, chapter 21, section 9; chapter 31, section 19; chapter 35, section 14.

It is not the "way of the perfect" <note>Referring to those who have already achieved a high state of spiritual maturity, as opposed to beginners.</note> of which the *Imitation of Christ* <note>A famous 15th-century devotional book by Thomas à Kempis.</note> speaks in another place,<gloss>1 *The Imitation of Christ*, book iii, chapter xxxii, section 3.</gloss> but the road that leads to perfection. Although she never grew tired of praising the perfection of the few nuns gathered around her,<gloss>2 *The Book of Foundations*, chapter i, section iv and following.</gloss> she could not but foresee that others, less advanced, might come in the future, and she desired to address herself to these no less than to the former. This was all the more necessary as there appears to have been a slight misunderstanding about the title of the newly established **Reform**. **St. Teresa** called herself and her nuns "<term>Discalced</term> <note>Literally "barefooted." This term was used by reformed religious orders to signal a return to a more austere, original way of life.</note>," following in this the example of some other Orders which had been lately reformed and had made barefootedness the distinctive sign of the reform, so much so that "barefooted" and "**Reformed**" came to be synonymous terms. But in **Rome** the new Reform of the **Carmelite Order** received a different title. The General, **John Baptist Rubeo** <note>Also known as Giovanni Battista Rossi, the head of the entire Carmelite Order at the time.</note>, called the Reformed members of his Order **Contemplatives**. Thus, in a patent <note>An official document or decree granting a right or title.</note> of 1567 he speaks of "houses and monasteries of contemplative Carmelites,"<gloss>3 Patent of August 16, 1567: <note>original Spanish: "casas y monasterios de Carmelitas contemplativos."</note></gloss> and again in 1570 he addresses "the contemplative Carmelites of the province of **Castile**."<gloss>4 Patent of August 8, 1570: <note>original Latin: "ad Carmelitas contemplativos provinciæ Castellæ."</note></gloss> Both these documents as well as some others, are of a later date than

In fact, both he and **Fray Garcia of Toledo** read and approved it, though neither has left a written approbation, but there is reason to believe that some passages were deleted by **Bañez**. The original manuscript appears to have remained in the convent of **St. Joseph at Avila** <note>The first convent of the Discalced Carmelite reform, founded by Teresa in 1562.</note> until, in 1586, four years after the death of **St. Teresa**, it was lent to **Fray Luis de Leon** <note>An influential Spanish poet and academic who prepared the first definitive edition of Teresa's writings.</note>, who had been entrusted with the editing of her works; when he had done with it, it was presented to **Philip II** <note>The King of Spain, who was a great admirer of Teresa and collected her original manuscripts.</note> for the royal monastery of the **Escorial** <note>A massive palace and monastery complex near Madrid; its library holds many of the most important documents of Spanish history.</note>, where it has remained ever since, and where the present writer was privileged to see it. It is not in the library, but in a small room where some other precious manuscripts, as well as certain relics of saints, are preserved.

...del Campo <note>Medina del Campo, the site of the second convent founded by St. Teresa in 1567.</note>, until just a few months before her death, she was busy establishing many new convents based on the model of her first foundation. More than a hundred devout women joined the order of **Our Lady of Mount Carmel** <note>The Carmelite Order, which St. Teresa sought to return to its original, more rigorous "discalced" or barefoot roots.</note> and committed themselves to a life of prayer and spiritual sacrifice on behalf of others. These women needed the guidance found in the ***Way of Perfection*** <note>original: *Camino de Perfección*, a guidebook for prayer and communal living written by Teresa for her nuns.</note> just as much as they needed the official regulations found in the **Rule** and **Constitutions** <note>The "Rule" refers to the ancient laws of the Carmelite Order, while the "Constitutions" were the specific daily regulations Teresa wrote for her reformed convents.</note>.

Because of this, **St. Teresa** <note>Teresa of Avila (1515–1582), a Spanish mystic, writer, and reformer of the Carmelite Order.</note> decided to rewrite the entire book. She paid close attention to the corrections made by her reviser <note>Likely the Dominican theologian Dominic Bañez, who censored the first draft to ensure it met strict religious standards.</note>. Along with these corrections, she introduced many other changes, aiming for more precise language, reordering entire chapters, and expanding on some of her thoughts. We no longer know the exact date or place where she wrote this second version.

The original manuscript—except for a few pages that were lost or stolen—is preserved at the convent in **Valladolid**. It is kept in a solid silver binding and is one of that convent's most important treasures. This manuscript was copied many times while the Saint was still alive. One copy is kept in **Toledo**, another at the convent of **El Pardo** in **Madrid**, and a third in **Salamanca**. All of these contain small variations written in the Saint’s own hand, but only the second and third copies bear the signature of **St. Teresa**, where she confirms they are faithful copies. One more copy can be found in the library of the **Escorial** <note>The Royal Library at the Monastery of San Lorenzo de El Escorial, founded by King Philip II of Spain.</note>, which matches the text word for word...

In a letter to her brother, **Don Lorenzo de Cepeda** <note>Teresa’s younger brother who had returned from the Spanish Americas and became a close spiritual student of his sister.</note>, dated January 2, 1577, **St. Teresa** says: “The book which treats of the matter of which I have told you is the one where I explained the ***Pater Noster*** <note>Latin for "Our Father." This refers to the *Way of Perfection*, which Teresa structured as a commentary on the Lord's Prayer.</note>. There you will find considerations on the degree of prayer which you have reached, although the subject is not so fully developed as in the other book (the ***Life***) <note>Teresa’s autobiography, *The Book of Her Life*, which contains deep theological reflections on mystical prayer.</note>. I think it is in the explanation of these words: ***Adveniat regnum tuum*** <note>Latin: "Thy kingdom come."</note>. Read it again, at least the section on the ***Pater Noster*** <note>The Our Father.</note>; perhaps you will find something to satisfy you.” Thus, Don Lorenzo must have had access to the work.

But copying a book with the pen was a long process, and there was always a danger of omissions and changes, which could only be obviated by having the whole work printed. To this end, she sent a copy, the one now at **Toledo** <note>Another of the major surviving manuscripts, known as the "Toledo Codex."</note>, to **Don Teutonio de Braganza**, **Archbishop of Evora** <note>A Portuguese prelate and friend of Teresa who helped facilitate the printing of her works.</note>, requesting him to make the necessary arrangements. The letter is unfortunately not preserved, but in one of July 22, 1579, she says: “Last week I wrote a long letter to your Lordship sending you my little book; I will therefore only write a few words to-day, as I forgot to ask your...

Taking the text of Valladolid <note>The "Valladolid manuscript" is one of the two main original versions of *The Way of Perfection*; it is a more polished, less "raw" version than the Escorial manuscript.</note> for his basis, he incorporated with it many of the variants to be found in the other sources, so that his edition, which appeared in 1588, so far from being a reproduction of any one of the authentic texts, was rather a combination of all of them. It has been reproduced, times without number, until recent years ; the two English translations, by **Abraham Woodhead** <note>An English academic and Catholic convert (1608–1678) who published several of Teresa's works during a period when Catholic books were largely suppressed in England.</note> and his companion, in 1675, and by **Canon Dalton** in 1852, have followed it, though the latter allowed himself to be influenced by the French translation of **Robert Arnauld d’Andilly** <note>A significant French writer and translator (1589–1674) associated with the Port-Royal monastery.</note>, one (though, perhaps, the least dangerous) of the **Jansenist** <term>Jansenism: A controversial 17th-century theological movement within the Catholic Church that emphasized a very strict moral code and a specific view of divine grace and predestination.</term> leaders.

-># INTRODUCTION.<-

The merit of having returned to one of the originals, the manuscript of Valladolid <note>The Valladolid manuscript is one of the two primary versions of *The Way of Perfection* written in St. Teresa’s own hand; it is generally considered more polished than the earlier draft.</note>, belongs to Father <note>original: "P." (Pater)</note> Marcel Bouix, whose French translation appeared in 1856. In 1883, on the occasion of the third centenary of the death of the Saint, Don Francisco Herrera Bayona, treasurer of the Metropolitan church of Valladolid, undertook the publication of a photographic reproduction of St. Teresa’s autograph <note>An "autograph" in this context refers to a manuscript written in the author's own handwriting.</note>, as Don Vicente de la Fuente had already done for the *Life* and the *Book of the Foundations*. He was only allowed to photograph the manuscript of the Escorial <note>The Escorial is a historical residence of the King of Spain; its library holds the other primary original manuscript of this work.</note>, but every facility was accorded him to prepare a literal transcript of the one at Valladolid, which he printed in parallel columns with the transcript of the former version. Moreover, he confronted with these texts the three early copies of Toledo, Madrid, and Salamanca, as well as Don Teutonio’s edition <note>Teutonio de Braganza (1530–1602) was the Archbishop of Évora who published the first edition of the book in 1583.</note>, adding to the volume a complete <term>apparatus of variants</term> <note>A scholarly list showing all the differences in wording between various versions and manuscripts of a text.</note>. This edition has been utilised for the present translation, which was ready for the press four years ago, but the publication of which has been delayed owing to a prolonged absence of the present writer.

The question which text to follow in this translation has been carefully discussed by those who were able to form an opinion. The first idea was to choose one of the two original versions, and to supplement it by the variants which would have been added as foot-notes, or at least printed in

# INTRODUCTION.

a different style; but, after various essays <note>In this context, "essays" refers to experimental drafts or attempts at arranging the text.</note>, it was found that such an arrangement would prove bewildering for the generality of readers, while the student, who alone can be interested in the gradual evolution of the text, finds the whole material in Don Francisco’s <note>Don Francisco Herrera Bayona, the scholar mentioned on the previous page who published the parallel-column edition.</note> edition. It was therefore decided, though not without reluctance, to follow the precedent (but not the text) of Luis de Leon <note>Fray Luis de León (1527–1591) was a famous Spanish poet and theologian who published the first collected edition of St. Teresa's works in 1588.</note>, and to combine once more the various versions, so that this edition should contain everything written by St. Teresa. There are some chapters where the divergence between the versions of the Escorial and of Valladolid <note>The two primary surviving handwritten versions of *The Way of Perfection*.</note> are so great that the translation resembles a mosaic composed of a large number of small bits, skilfully combined. But the work has been done most conscientiously, and while nothing has been added to the text of the Saint, nothing has been omitted, except, of course, what would have been mere repetition. No doubt the plan adopted in this translation will not meet with the approval of scholars, but as the translator desired to benefit the souls of the faithful rather than the intellect of the student, no other course could have been chosen. The present writer has repeatedly compared every word with the originals and can vouch for the accuracy of the translation.

<meta>This page concludes the editorial introduction, moving from the technical discussion of combining manuscript versions on the previous page to a specific disclaimer regarding the authenticity of a work commonly attributed to St. Teresa.</meta>

<page-num>xxxviii</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION.

*Days of the Week.* This short work appeared for the first time at Antwerp <note>Antwerp, in modern-day Belgium, was a major center for Catholic printing in the 17th century.</note> in 1630 and has been frequently reprinted and translated into many languages, including English. But the book is not by St. Teresa. Not one of her biographers knew anything about it; it is never alluded to either in her correspondence or in the very numerous and minute depositions <note>In this context, "depositions" refers to formal written testimonies or statements used as evidence.</note> made by all sorts and conditions of persons during the process of <term>canonisation</term> <note>canonisation: The official process by which the Catholic Church declares a deceased person to be a saint.</note>, while the <term>Chronicler of the Order</term> <note>Chronicler of the Order: The official historian of the Discalced Carmelite religious order, tasked with maintaining accurate records of the group's history.</note> distinctly denies its authenticity, giving good reasons for his judgment. It is probably the work of some friar or nun, and deserves respect owing to the pious sentiments it contains. But no one acquainted with the style of St. Teresa could admit its authenticity.

->BENEDICT ZIMMERMAN,<-
->*Prior, O.C.D.* <note>original: "O.C.D." stands for "Ordo Carmelitarum Discalceatorum," the Order of Discalced Carmelites founded by St. Teresa.</note><-

ST. LUKE’S PRIORY,
WINCANTON.
October 15, 1910.

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION.

### JESUS. <note>original: "I H S." A Christogram representing the name of Jesus, traditionally used as a heading in Christian manuscripts.</note>

## INTRODUCTION.

**->Which contains the reasons for writing this book.<-**

The sisters of this convent of St. Joseph <note>St. Joseph's in Avila was the first convent of the Discalced Carmelite Reform, founded by Teresa in 1562.</note>, having learnt that my confessor, Father Master Dominic Bañes <note>Dominic Bañes (1528–1604) was a famous Dominican theologian and a significant influence on St. Teresa’s writings and spiritual direction.</note>, of the glorious <term>Order of St. Dominic</term> <note>Order of St. Dominic: Often called the Dominicans; a religious order known for its focus on study, preaching, and defending the faith.</note>, had given me leave to write about prayer,¹ <note>original: "Rel. vii. 9. Way of Perf. ch. xlii." These are scholarly citations to Teresa's other "Relations" (spiritual reports) and the 42nd chapter of this book.</note> of which I appeared capable, because of having spoken with so many spiritual and devout persons, have so importuned <note>importuned: Persistently urged or begged.</note> me that I have decided on complying with their wishes. Faulty and imperfect as my style may be, I know that the sisters’ great love for me will give my words more influence with them than that of books far better written by those who have taught me all I know. Therefore I have determined to yield to the nuns’ wishes and persuasions. I rely upon their prayers and on humility; perhaps by these means God may give me grace to say something useful concerning the life that ought to be led in this house, and

If I fail, Father Master <note>A title of respect for her confessor, Dominic Bañes, a prominent Dominican theologian.</note>, who is to read these writings first, will either correct them or throw them into the fire: thus I shall have lost nothing by obeying the wishes of these servants of God, and they will discover what I really am, when His Majesty <note>original: "Su Majestad." St. Teresa’s favorite title for Jesus Christ, reflecting her view of Him as both a sovereign King and a close friend.</note> does not assist me.

<margin>² See *The Life*, ch. 32, sections 13, 14; ch. 35, sections 13, 14; ch. 36, sections 27, 28.</margin>
I intend suggesting remedies for certain minor temptations of the devil which, because they are slight, are often disregarded; also to explain my object in founding this house, namely, to restore the perfect observance of our Rule <note>The Carmelite Rule. St. Teresa sought to return to the original, stricter "Primitive Rule" of the order.</note> that had been mitigated <note>mitigated: This refers to the "Rule of Mitigation" (1432), which relaxed the original austerities of the Carmelite life, such as fasting and silence.</note> elsewhere.² I will also speak of other matters as our Lord may direct me, and as they occur to my mind. Not knowing of what things I shall treat, I cannot arrange them in proper order. I think, after all, that this is best, as it is quite incongruous <note>incongruous: Unsuitable or out of place. Teresa often used such self-deprecating language to emphasize that her insights came from God, not her own education.</note> for such a person as myself to speak about such subjects. May our Lord guide me in all I do, that it may be pleasing to His holy will, for this has always been my aim, faulty as my deeds may be!

Certainly, on my part, there is no lack of love for my sisters, nor of a desire to do all I can to help their souls to make great progress in God’s service. This affection, my age, and my personal experience in various convents <note>term: convent. A community of religious women (nuns) living under vows.</note>, may assist me to write of such lesser matters better than do learned men, whose more important business and

<summary>St. Teresa expresses her intent to guide her nuns through minor temptations and explains her goal of restoring the strict Carmelite Rule. She humbly suggests that her practical experience as a woman in the cloister may offer unique insights that learned men might overlook.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION.

powerful minds make them overlook things, insignificant in themselves, yet which may do great harm to such weak creatures as we women are. The devil employs his most subtle wiles against strictly cloistered nuns <note>cloistered nuns: Religious sisters who live within the "enclosure" of a convent, separated from the outside world to devote themselves entirely to prayer and contemplation.</note>, for he sees that he requires some new sort of weapon to injure them. Wicked as I am, I have been able to defend myself but ill against him, and I wish my sisters to take warning by me. I shall only speak of what I have learnt by my own experience, have witnessed in others, or that God has shown to me during prayer.

A short time ago, I was told to write a history of my life <note>original: "libro de su vida." St. Teresa refers to her autobiography, which she wrote under obedience to her confessors. May God direct my work, as I have begged of Him, and may He order it all for His greater glory ! Amen.

## CHAPTER I.

### The reason why I founded this convent in such austere observance.

*1. Why the Convent of St. Joseph at Avila was founded. 2. Reasons for the corporal austerities <note>physical acts of penance, such as fasting or wearing hairshirts, meant to discipline the body</note> of this convent. 3. St. Teresa's grief at sin and the eternal loss of sinners. 4. She begs the nuns to intercede against these evils. 5. This, and not worldly matters, should be the object of our petitions.*

1. THIS convent <note>St. Joseph’s in Avila, the first house of the "Discalced" or reformed Carmelites, established in 1562</note> was founded for the reasons already given in the work above mentioned,¹ and also on account of certain favours that God showed me, in which He revealed that He would be served with great fervour in this house.² I did not at first intend that such rigorous bodily austerities should be practised in it, nor that it should possess no income; on the contrary, I wished it to have sufficient means to prevent the possibility of want; which shows how weak and wicked I am, although I meant rather to do what was right than to seek for self-indulgence.³

2. Just at this time I heard of the miseries France was suffering, of the havoc the Lutherans <note>In 16th-century Spain, "Lutheran" was a general term used for all Protestant reformers. Teresa is referring to the French Wars of Religion</note> were making there,

³ *The Life*, chapter 33, section 15; chapter 35, sections 2-7.

<summary>St. Teresa explains that her original plan for the Convent of St. Joseph did not include such extreme poverty or discipline, but she was moved to increase the community's rigour after learning about the religious conflicts in France.</summary>

and how this wretched sect was increasing.⁴ It grieved me bitterly, and as if I could have done anything, or had been of any consequence, I cried to God and begged Him to cure this terrible evil. I felt that I would have laid down a thousand lives to save one of the many souls perishing there. Yet, as I am but a woman, feeble and faulty, it was impossible for me to serve God in the way I wished—indeed, all I cared for then, as I do now, was that, as the enemies of God are so many and His friends so few, these latter might at least be good ones. 3. O my Redeemer! How it wearies my heart <note>The superscript 6 refers to the marginal quote from the Book of Psalms provided above.</note> to think of this! To what a state have Christians come! That must be their fate, though it breaks my heart to see so many...

<summary>St. Teresa expresses deep grief over the disloyalty of Christians who have turned against the Church, comparing their actions to a second crucifixion of Christ. 4. O my sisters in Christ ! help me to pray to our Lord for this ! ## CHAPTER II.

### That the necessities of the body should be disregarded.
### Of the advantages of poverty.

*1. Nuns should leave the care of their health to Christ. 2. Perpetual poverty to be maintained in the Order. 3. Safeguards against loss of poverty of spirit. 4. Advantages of poverty. 5. Honours and riches are opposed to poverty. 6. Poverty always the badge of the Carmelite Order. 7. The convents, though poor, may possess hermitages. 8. Intercession to be made for benefactors.*

1. Do not suppose, my sisters, that because you do not seek favour with the world, you will be left to starve; I can reassure you about that. Never try to sustain yourselves by any human artifice, or you will perish of famine, as you would deserve. Look to your Spouse <note>Teresa refers to Jesus Christ as the "Spouse" or "Bridegroom" of the nuns, a central image in her mystical theology.</note>; He must maintain you: if He is pleased with you, those who like you least will give you food, even against their will, as you have learnt by experience.<gloss>1</gloss> If, when you

# CHAPTER II. POVERTY OF THE ORDER.

...have done this, you should die of hunger, happy the nuns of St. Joseph! <note>The Convent of Saint Joseph in Ávila, founded by Teresa in 1562, was the first house of the Discalced Carmelite Reform. Unlike traditional convents of the time, it was established to survive solely on alms without any guaranteed income or "rents."</note> Thus our prayers will be pleasing to God and we shall have carried out what we professed. For the love of God, do not forget this: as you have given up your revenues <note>Under the new Reform, the sisters renounced the standard practice of "endowments"—fixed financial gifts or properties that provided a steady income to a religious house.</note>, give up the care of your sustenance as well, or all will be lost. People whom our Lord wishes to possess incomes are quite right in looking after such matters, for that is their vocation <note>A "vocation" is a divine calling. Teresa acknowledges that while laypeople or other religious orders may be called to manage wealth responsibly, her specific reform is called to a different standard.</note>, but it is inconsistent in us. To calculate what we shall receive from others seems to me like reckoning up their riches, and all your care will not change their minds, nor make them wish to give you alms. Leave your case in the hands of Him Who bends all wills, Who is the Lord of riches and of rich men. We came here at His bidding: His words are sure and cannot fail; heaven and earth will fail first; let us not forsake Him, and never fear that He will forsake us. If at any time He did so, it would be for our greater good, as life forsook the saints when they were slain and beheaded for our Lord, that their glory might be increased by their martyrdom. It would be a good exchange to finish this life quickly, so as to enjoy eternal satiety <note>satiety: a state of being completely full or satisfied. In this context, Teresa contrasts the "hunger" of earthly poverty with the spiritual "fullness" of heaven.</note>.

2. Be certain, sisters, that this matter will be most important for you when I am dead, therefore I leave it you in writing. While I live, by the grace of God I will remind you of it, knowing by experience how great are its benefits. When I have least, I am most free from

4. Poverty includes in itself all the world's goods; it is a vast domain. I dare to affirm that he who despises all earthly goods holds sway over them. What are kings and lords to me, if I do not want their money, nor seek to please them, if by so doing I should displease God in the very least? What care I for their honours, if I know that the honour of a poor man consists in true poverty? It seems to me that honours and riches nearly always go together; he who loves honour never hates riches, while he who hates riches seeks no honours.

So much has been written on this matter which I cannot even understand, much less explain, that I will not depreciate it by my praise and will now say no more about it. ### CHAPTER II.
## POVERTY OF THE ORDER.

...our words, and, what is far more, in our hearts. While this is done, there is no fear that <term>religious observance: the strict adherence to the rules and spiritual practices of a monastic life</term> should decay here, by the help of God; for, as St. Clare used to say, **poverty is a strong wall.** 

With this, and with humility, she wished to surround her monasteries. True enough, if poverty is real, it guards purity and all the other virtues better than do fine buildings. Keep to this, I beg of you, by the love of God and by His Blood. If, with a good conscience, I could wish that the day you build a costly dwelling it may fall and kill you all—I say, if I could do so with good conscience—I *would* wish it and beg God to grant it. <note>Teresa’s characteristic humor and intensity are on display here; she would rather see her nuns die in a collapsed building than see them abandon the spiritual safety of poverty for architectural luxury.</note> It looks very ill, my daughters, to build fine houses with needy men’s <term>alms: charitable donations given to the poor or to religious orders</term>! God forbid it! ours should be poor and mean in every way. Let us to this extent at least resemble our King <note>Referring to Jesus Christ, who Christians believe was born in a humble stable.</note>. He had no home except the stable of Bethlehem where He was...

***

<margin>5. “St. Clare often taught the sisters that the Order would be pleasing to God while endowed with poverty, and that it would always prosper as long as it was fortified by the tower of strictest poverty” (<note>original Latin: "Acta SS." referring to the *Acta Sanctorum* or "Acts of the Saints," a multi-volume scholarly text on the lives of saints.</note> *Acts of the Saints*, Aug. 12). While founding the convent of St. Joseph at Avila, St. Teresa tells us: “St. Clare appeared to me in great beauty and bade me take courage and go on with what I had begun; she would help me. I began to have a great devotion to St. Clare; and she has so truly kept her word, that a monastery of nuns of her Order in our neighbourhood helped us to live; and, what is of more importance, by little and little she so perfectly fulfilled my desire, that the poverty which the blessed Saint observes in her own house is observed in this and we are living on alms” (<note>Teresa is referring to her own autobiography,</note> *Life*, ch. xxxiii. 15).</margin>

<summary>In this passage, St. Teresa emphasizes that physical poverty is a "strong wall" that protects a monastery's spiritual integrity, warning her nuns never to build luxurious houses with the donations of the poor.</summary>

If there should be any grounds (as there must be, on account of the <term>enclosure: the restricted area of a monastery where the nuns live and which outsiders may not enter</term> and because they are a help to prayer and devotion), by all means let there be <term>hermitages: small, separate structures within the convent grounds for solitary prayer</term> in which to retire for prayer, for weak human nature requires some indulgence; but let the <term>convents: the community and buildings of a religious order, in this case, the Discalced Carmelites</term> be neither large nor handsome.⁶ God deliver us from such things! It would not look well if the house of thirteen poor women made much noise when it tumbled, for the real poor make no commotion—they must be silent, or none will pity them.

# CHAPTER III.

->This chapter continues the subject spoken of in the first chapter. The writer exhorts the sisters constantly to beg God to protect those who labour for the Church, and concludes with an ardent appeal to God.<-

->1. *Evils of the times.* 2. *Difficulties of religious and ecclesiastics who live in the world.* 3. *The two chief objects for prayer.* 4. *An appeal to God the Father on behalf of His Son.* 5. *Subjects for intercession with God.*<-

1. **L**ET us now return to the reason why our Lord has assembled us in this house, in which I am most desirous that we should please His Majesty. Seeing how great are the disasters of these times $^1$ <note>Teresa is referring to the Protestant Reformation and the religious wars in Europe, which she viewed as a great tragedy for the Church.</note> (although some persons

<meta>This page continues the metaphor introduced in Chapter III, comparing the spiritual life and the defense of the Church to a besieged fortress. It also concludes a footnote containing an excerpt from a letter by St. Teresa to her brother, Lorenzo de Cepeda.</meta>

[Page 16]

THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. III.]

[...some persons] have imagined that force of arms could stop this great evil <note>Referring to the religious conflicts and the spread of Protestantism during the 16th century.</note>), and that no human power can quench the devouring flames of heresy, which spread most rapidly, I think we should act as people do when, in time of war, the enemy has overrun the country and the King finds himself hard pressed. He retires into a strongly fortified town from whence he sometimes makes a sortie <note>A sudden attack launched from a defensive position.</note>. The small company with him in the citadel <note>The core fortress protecting a city.</note>, being picked men, are better than a large army of cowardly soldiers; thus they often come off victors, or at least, if not victors, they are not vanquished, for there is no traitor in their ranks and famine alone can conquer them. No famine can force *us* to surrender—it may kill us—it cannot vanquish us! 

But why have I told you all this? To teach you, my sisters, that we must ask God to grant that, of all the good Christians in this fort, none may desert to the enemy, that no traitor may be found here, and that the captains of this castle, or city—that is, the preachers and theologians—may be proficient in the way of our Lord. Since most of these are <term>religious</term> <note>Members of religious orders, such as monks, friars, or priests, who have taken formal vows.</note>, you must pray that they may advance in <term>perfection</term> <note>In this context, "perfection" refers to the spiritual goal of complete union with God’s will.</note> and may follow their <term>vocation</term> <note>A "vocation" is a person's sense of being called by God to a specific task or way of life.</note> more perfectly. This is very necessary, for, as [one must understand...]

<note>The text below continues the footnote excerpt from the previous page, quoting a letter from St. Teresa to her brother Lorenzo regarding the spiritual state of the world.</note>

“...enlighten them, for there are many miseries both in their country and in our own. I travel to many places and talk to many people, and I can only say that we are often worse than the beasts, for we do not understand the great dignity of our souls and we degrade them with the base things of this world. May God give us light!”

<summary>St. Teresa uses the metaphor of a besieged fortress to explain the role of her nuns: they are the "picked men" defending the Church through prayer, specifically supporting the "captains" (preachers and theologians). She emphasizes the need for spiritual perfection and highlights the tragic neglect of the soul's dignity in the world.</summary>

## CHAPTER III: INTERCESSION FOR THE CHURCH

I said, it is the arm of the Church, and not of the State, which must defend us now. We, being women, can fight for our King <note>In St. Teresa's writing, "the King" is a frequent metaphor for God or Christ.</note> in neither way: let us, then, strive so to live that our prayers may avail to help these servants of God, who have laboured hard to arm themselves with learning and virtue with which to help their Sovereign. You may ask why I insist so much on this, and why I say we must help those who are better than ourselves. I will explain this, as I do not think you realise how much you owe to God for withdrawing you so entirely from all earthly cares, from occasions of sin, and from the society of the world. <note>Referring to the "Interior Castle," Mansion VI, chapter vi, paragraph 14.</note> This is a very special favour, and one not shared in by the men I have been speaking of. Indeed, it would be less fitting for them now than ever, for they have to strengthen the weak and to encourage the timid. What a state soldiers would get into without their captain! These defenders of the Church must live amongst men and associate with them, they must frequent the Court, and even, at times, conform outwardly to its customs.

2. Do you think, my daughters, that it is easy to keep friends with the world, to live in it, to transact worldly business, and, as I said, to conform to its usages, and yet, in one's heart, to remain a stranger and enemy of this same world, like an exile? In short, not to be men but to be angels? Unless they are all this, they do not

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. III.]

...deserve the name of **captains**, and may our Lord prevent <note>In this context, "prevent" carries the older meaning of "to hinder" or "keep from."</note> them ever leaving their **cells** <term>Cells (The small, private rooms of monks or nuns, representing a life of secluded prayer)</term>, for they will do more harm than good. This is no time for defects to appear in the teachers, who, unless they be forearmed by knowing the need of spurning all things earthly beneath their feet, **detached** <term>Detachment (The spiritual discipline of releasing one's emotional and physical dependence on material things)</term> from all things transitory, and wholly devoted to what is eternal, are bound to manifest their imperfections, strive as they may to hide them. For are they not dealing with the world? Do not imagine that it will pardon or fail to observe their shortcomings. Much that is good may pass unnoticed—or even perhaps be considered evil, but no fault or imperfection will ever escape criticism.³

3. I wonder who taught them all about **perfection** <note>Teresa is being slightly sarcastic here, noting how quickly worldly people criticize the behavior of religious figures while failing to practice any virtue themselves.</note> (not for their own practice, for they think it will suffice to content God if they keep the commandments fairly well) but that they may censure others, while sometimes they take **virtue** for self-indulgence? You see that, far from requiring but little help from God during the great struggle in which they are engaged, our defenders need it urgently. I wish you to lead such lives as to merit to obtain these two favours from God. Firstly, that among very learned **theologians** and **religious** <term>Religious (A person who has taken vows in a religious order, such as a monk, nun, or friar)</term> there may be many with the qualifications I describe, and that our Lord may perfect those who are less fitted, for one who is perfect can do more than many who are...

->### CHAP. III.] APPEAL TO GOD THE FATHER.<-

...believe that Thou wilt reject us. Thou art a just Judge, not like earthly judges who, being sons of Adam and stern men, have no faith in woman’s virtue. <note>Teresa is critiquing the patriarchal society of 16th-century Spain, where women were often excluded from theological and public life.</note> The day will come, my King, when all will be known. I speak not for myself, for all men know of my wickedness, and I rejoice that it is made public, but, seeing in what manner of times we are living, it is not right to repulse the good and valiant, though they are but women. <note>The "manner of times" refers to the Protestant Reformation and the internal reforms of the Catholic Church, a period of great religious conflict.</note> When we beg of Thee honours, income, riches, any worldly thing, do not listen to us, but how shouldst Thou not hear us when we ask for what concerns the honour of Thy Son? Why, O Eternal Father, shouldst Thou refuse those who would lose a thousand honours and a thousand lives for Thee? Not for our sakes, O God, for we deserve it not, but for the sake of Thy Son and of His merits. <term>merits (In Catholic theology, these are the spiritual "credits" or rewards earned by Christ through his suffering, which can be applied to others.)</term> Think, should such stripes and insults and such bitter torments be forgotten? <note>original: "stripes" - This refers to the scourging of Jesus at the pillar during His Passion.</note> How can a heart so loving as Thine own endure that what was instituted with such ardent love by Thy Son, for the sake of pleasing Thee (for Thou didst bid Him love us) should be held of so little value as is the **Blessed Sacrament** in these days by heretics? <term>Blessed Sacrament (The Eucharist or Holy Communion. Catholics believe this is the literal body and blood of Christ.)</term> For they drive It from Its dwelling-place when they destroy the churches. <note>Teresa is likely referring to the iconoclasm and the closing of Catholic churches in parts of Europe during the Reformation.</note> It is not as if He had left undone aught that could please Thee. No, He has consummated everything! Was it not enough, O Eternal Father, for Him to have had no place in which to lay His head...

<summary>St. Teresa petitions God to value the prayers of women, arguing that while they may be dismissed by worldly men, their devotion to the Blessed Sacrament is vital during a time of religious conflict and church destruction.</summary>

...while He lived, amidst incessant toils, that now they must deprive Him of the place to which He invites His friends, seeing that they are fainting and knowing that those who labour need to be supported by **this meat**? <term>this meat (In 16th-century English, "meat" meant food or nourishment in general. Here, Teresa refers to the Eucharist as the essential spiritual food for the soul.)</term> Has He not paid, in overwhelming excess, for the sin of Adam? Must this most meek and loving Lamb atone anew for every fresh sin we commit? Do not suffer it, my Sovereign King; let Thy Majesty be appeased; look not on our faults but on Thy most holy Son Who has redeemed us; reflect on His merits, on those of His glorious Mother and on those of the many Saints and Martyrs who have died for Thee! 

But alas, O my God! who am I, who have dared to offer Thee this petition in the name of all? My daughters, what a wretched advocate you have, to gain a hearing and to present your petition for you! <note>Teresa frequently employs a rhetorical style of "holy humility," describing herself as unworthy or "wretched" to emphasize that the power of prayer relies entirely on God's mercy rather than the speaker's status.</note> What if my presumption should anger this supreme Judge, as would be only right and just? But remember, O my Sovereign and my Lord, that Thou art the God of mercy; do Thou prove it to this poor sinner and worm who is thus bold with Thee. Look, O God, upon my desires and on the tears with which I beg this of Thee, and, forgetting my evil actions for Thine own sake, take pity on the many perishing souls and have mercy on Thy Church. Do not permit these evils to increase in Christendom, O Lord, but illuminate its darkness. <note>The "darkness" refers to the theological conflicts of the Protestant Reformation, which Teresa viewed as a spiritual crisis threatening the unity of the Christian world.</note>

## CHAP. III.] INTERCESSION FOR SUPERIORS.

...to intercede for this poor, presumptuous creature <note>Teresa is referring to herself here. She often uses self-deprecating language—calling herself "wretched" or a "creature"—to demonstrate the "holy humility" she believes is necessary when approaching God.</note>, and fulfill your duty by asking Him to give her humility. I do not charge you particularly to pray for kings and prelates <term>prelates (High-ranking members of the clergy, such as bishops or abbots, who have authority over a specific territory or community.)</term> of the Church, especially for our own Bishop <note>Don Alvaro de Mendoza (died 1586) was the Bishop of Avila and a vital supporter of Teresa. He took her first convent under his protection and was eventually buried there.</note>, as I see that you do so diligently; therefore, I need not speak of it. 

But let whoever comes after me be sure of this: if the Superior is holy, the community will be the same. Therefore, always crave this most important favor from God. If your prayers and desires, your disciplines and fasts <term>disciplines and fasts (Physical acts of penance, such as self-flagellation or abstaining from food, practiced to share in the sufferings of Christ and purify the soul.)</term>, are not performed for these intentions, know that you are not doing the work nor carrying out the design for which God placed you here. I beg His Majesty <note>original: "Su Majestad." Teresa’s favorite title for God, reflecting her view of Him as a sovereign yet approachable King.</note>, for the sake of His Godhead, never to let you forget this.

***

<note>Don Alvaro de Mendoza belonged to the family of the Counts of Ribadavia. After serving as the first chaplain of San Juan de los Reyes in Toledo, he was named Bishop of Avila in 1560. He became a close friend of St. Teresa and placed her convent under his jurisdiction. In 1577, he was promoted to the See of Palencia. Later, he was commissioned by the Pope to oversee the interests of the Discalced <term>Discalced (Literally "shoeless"; referring to the reformed branch of the Carmelites who wore sandals as a sign of poverty.)</term> Carmelites. He died on April 19, 1586, and was buried in the chapel of the convent of St. Joseph at Avila. St. Teresa always spoke of him with the highest respect.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa instructs her nuns that their primary mission is to pray for the holiness of Church and state leaders, warning that their spiritual exercises are wasted if not directed toward this goal.</summary>
<keywords>intercession, humility, Bishop Alvaro de Mendoza, prelates, Discalced Carmelites, monastic discipline, prayer for leaders</keywords>
