I. You see upon how great an enterprise you have embarked for the sake of the Father Provincial <note>The regional superior who oversees the various houses of the Carmelite Order within a specific province.</note>, the Bishop of the diocese <note>Don Alvaro de Mendoza, the Bishop of Avila and a key patron of Teresa's reform.</note>, and of your Order, in which all else is included, all being for the good of the Church, for which we are bound to pray as a matter of obligation. As I said, what lives are not those bound to live who have had the courage to engage in this design, if they would not be confounded, before God and man, for their audacity? Clearly we must work hard; it is a great help to have high aspirations: by their means we may cause our actions to become great also, although there are different ways of doing so. If we endeavour to observe our <term>Rule</term><note>The Rule of Saint Albert, the foundational code of conduct for the Carmelite Order.</note> and <term>Constitutions</term><note>The specific regulations and daily statutes that Teresa wrote to govern the lived experience of her reformed "Discalced" (barefoot) nuns.</note> very faithfully, I hope that God will grant our petitions. may fulfil those that our holy Fathers <note>Teresa refers here to the early hermits of Mount Carmel, the spiritual ancestors of her religious order.</note> enjoined and practised in our **Rules and Constitutions**, which include all the virtues and by performing which our predecessors earned the name of **Saints**. I will explain three matters only, which are in our *Constitutions* <note>The "Rules" are the foundational laws of the order, while the "Constitutions" are the specific daily regulations Teresa established for her reformed convents.</note> : it is essential for us to understand how much they help us to preserve that peace, both interior and exterior, which our Lord so strongly enjoined. If this commandment were observed in this world as it ought to be, I believe it would be a great help towards obeying the others, but whether we err by excess or by defect, we only succeed in keeping it imperfectly.

->[CHAP. IV.] PARTICULAR FRIENDSHIPS.<-

here which are hardly detected by those who are content to serve God in a superficial way—indeed, they take such conduct for virtue—those, however, who are bent on **perfection** <note>In Teresa's writings, "perfection" refers to the pursuit of total spiritual maturity and union with God’s will.</note> understand the evil clearly, for, little by little it deprives the will of strength to devote itself entirely to the love of God. I think this injures women even more than men, and does serious damage to the community. It prevents a **nun** from loving all the others equally, makes her resent any injury done to her friend, causes her to wish she had something to give her favourite and to seek for opportunities to talk to her often, and tell her how much she loves her, and other nonsense of the sort, rather than of how much she loves God. These **particular friendships** <term>particular friendships</term> <note>A technical term in monastic life for exclusive, overly emotional attachments between two members of a community, which are seen as a distraction from communal love and divine focus.</note> rarely serve to forward the love of God; in fact, I believe the devil originates them so as to make factions among the **religious** <note>In this context, "the religious" refers to individuals who have taken formal vows in a religious order, such as monks or nuns.</note>. When a friendship has the service of God for its object, it is at once manifest that the will is not only uninfluenced by passion but it rather helps to subdue the other passions.

5. In a large convent I permit such friendships, but in **St. Joseph’s** <note>St. Joseph’s in Avila was the first reformed convent Teresa founded; she intended it to be a small, intimate environment where her new "Discalced" (reformed) rule could be lived strictly.</note>, where there are, and can be, no more than thirteen nuns, all must love and help one another. Keep free of **partialities**, for the love of God, however holy they may be, for even among brothers they are like poison. I can see no advantage in them, and matters are far worse when they exist between relatives, for then they are a perfect pest. Believe me, sisters, though I may...

6. Let us love virtue and holiness and always try to prevent ourselves from being attracted by externals. O my sisters, let us not permit our will to become the slave of any save of Him Who purchased it with His Blood <note>A reference to Jesus Christ's sacrifice, which Teresa uses to remind the nuns that their primary devotion belongs to God alone.</note>, or, without knowing how, we shall find ourselves caught in a trap from which we cannot escape! Lord, have mercy upon us! The childish nonsense that comes from this is untold, and is so petty that no one could credit it who had not witnessed the thing. It is best not to speak of it here, lest women’s **foibles** <note>Weaknesses or character flaws; here Teresa refers to the petty jealousies or gossip that can arise from cliques.</note> should be learnt by those who know nothing about them; so I will give no details, although they astonish even me at times. By the mercy of God, I have never been entangled in such things myself, but perhaps this may be because I have fallen into far graver faults. However, as I have said, I have often seen it, but as I told you, in a **Superior** <term>Superior</term> <note>The head of the convent, responsible for the spiritual guidance and discipline of the community.</note> it would be ruinous. ### [CHAP. IV.] MUTUAL CHARITY.

<margin>
⁵ Rule 5.
</margin>
...by severity. A most useful precaution is for the nuns, according to our present habit, never to be with one another nor talk together except at the appointed times, but as the Rule <note>The Rule of St. Albert, which governs the Carmelite Order, emphasizing silence and individual reflection.</note> enjoins, for the sisters not to be together but each one alone in her cell.⁵ Let there be no common work-room in **St. Joseph’s** <note>San José in Avila, the first convent of Teresa's reform.</note>, for although this is a praiseworthy custom, silence is better kept when one is alone. Solitude is very helpful to persons who practise prayer, and since prayer is the **mortar** <note>Teresa uses this architectural metaphor to show that prayer is the essential binding agent that prevents the religious community from crumbling.</note> which keeps this house together, we must learn to like what promotes it.⁶

⁶ Rule 14. Const. 5.
</margin>

7. To return to speak of our charity for one another. It seems superfluous to insist on this, for who would be so boorish as not to love those with whom they associate and live, cut off as they are from all conversation, intercourse and recreation with any one outside the house, whilst believing that they bear a mutual love for God, as He has for all of them, since for His sake they have left everything? More especially as goodness always attracts love, and, by the blessing of God, I trust that the nuns of this convent will always be good. Therefore, I do not think there is much need for me to persuade you to love each other. But as regards the nature of this love and of the **virtuous love** <note>Teresa distinguishes between "natural" affection (based on personality) and "virtuous" love (based on a shared commitment to God).</note> that I wish you all to feel, and the means of knowing whether we possess this greatest of virtues—for it must be a very great virtue...

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. IV.<-

...since our Lord so often enjoins it on us, as He did most stringently upon His apostles—of this I will speak to you for a short time as well as my inaptitude will allow. If you find the matter explained in any other books, you need not read mine, for I very frequently do not understand what I am talking about, unless our Lord enlightens me.⁷ <note>original: "Castle, M. v. ch. iii. 12." Refers to Teresa's work *The Interior Castle*, the Fifth Mansions, chapter 3. The Escorial edition is one of the primary original manuscripts of this text.</note>

8. I intend treating of two kinds of love: one which is entirely spiritual, free from any sort of affection or natural tenderness which could tarnish its purity, and another which is spiritual but mingled with the frailty and weakness of human nature. The latter is good and seems lawful, being such as is felt between relatives and friends, and is that which I have mentioned before. The first of these two ways of loving, and the one that I will discuss, is unmixed with any kind of passion that would disturb its harmony. This love, exercised with moderation and discretion, is profitable in every way, particularly when borne towards holy people or <term>confessors</term> <note>A priest who hears confessions and offers spiritual guidance; in Teresa's time, the relationship between a nun and her confessor was the most significant intellectual and spiritual bond she possessed.</note>, for that which seems only natural is then changed into virtue.⁸ At times, however, these two kinds of love seem so combined that it is difficult to distinguish them from one another, especially as regards a confessor.

<summary>St. Teresa begins her analysis of two types of love: a purely spiritual love and one that retains elements of human nature. ->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. IV.<-

# CHAP. IV.] LOVE FOR CONFESSORS.

When persons who practise prayer discover that their <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest who hears confessions and offers spiritual guidance; in Teresa's time, the relationship between a nun and her confessor was the most significant intellectual and spiritual bond she possessed.</note> is a holy man who understands their **spiritual state**, they feel a strong affection for him; the devil then opens a "perfect battery" of <term>scruples</term> <note>In a religious context, "scruples" refers to excessive or irrational guilt about small or imagined sins, which can paralyze a person's spiritual progress.</note> on the soul. This is intended to greatly disturb it, especially if the priest is leading his <term>penitent</term> <note>A person who confesses their sins and seeks spiritual guidance.</note> to higher perfection. Then the evil one torments his victim to such a pitch that she leaves her <term>spiritual director</term> <note>A guide who provides ongoing advice on how to grow in holiness and prayer.</note>, so that the temptation gives her no peace either in one way or the other.

9. In such a case it is best not to think about whether you like your confessor or not, or whether you wish to like him. If we feel **friendship** for those who benefit our bodies, why should we not feel as great a friendship for those who strive and labour to benefit our souls? On the contrary, I think a liking for my confessor is a great help to my progress if he is holy and spiritual, and if I see that he endeavours to profit my soul. Human nature is so weak that this feeling is often a help to our undertaking great things in **God’s service**.

<margin>(</margin>
10. If, however, the confessor be a man of indifferent character, we must not let him know of our liking for him. Great prudence and caution are necessary on account of the difficulty of knowing his disposition: it is best, on this account, to conceal our feelings from him. You should believe that your friendship for him is harmless and think no more about it. You may follow this advice when you see that all your confessor says tends...

<summary>St. Teresa discusses the natural affection a person feels for a holy spiritual guide and warns that the devil uses "scruples" to make this affection feel sinful. She argues that friendship with a holy director is a positive aid to spiritual growth, though she advises caution if the director's character is unknown.</summary>

### CHAP. IV.] WORLDLY CONFESSORS.

therefore my advice about changing **confessors** <note>In the context of 16th-century monastic life, the confessor was the primary spiritual authority for nuns. St. Teresa struggled throughout her life with directors who did not understand her mystical experiences.</note> is the best, and I trust in God that you will be able to do so.

12. Be convinced of the importance of this: the thing is dangerous, a hell in itself, and injurious to every one. Do not wait until much harm has come of it, but stop the matter at once in every feasible way: this may be done with a clear conscience. I trust, however, that God will prevent those vowed to a life of prayer from becoming attached to any one who does not serve God fervently, as He certainly will, unless they omit to practise prayer and to strive after <term>perfection</term> <note>For St. Teresa, "perfection" is not a lack of mistakes, but a state of advanced spiritual maturity characterized by total surrender to God's will.</note>, as we profess to do in this house. If the nuns see that the confessor does not understand their <term>language</term> <note>Teresa refers here to the "language of the soul"—the specific vocabulary of interior prayer and mystical states—rather than a literal foreign tongue.</note> nor cares to speak of God, they cannot like him, for he differs from them. If he is of such a character, he will have extremely few chances of doing any harm here, and unless he is very foolish he will neither trouble himself about the servants of God, nor disturb those who have few pleasures and little or no opportunity of following their own way.

## CHAP. V. CONSULT OTHER CONFESSORS.
<page-num>35</page-num>

other. Hence comes the temptation to omit confessing grave sins for fear lest the unfortunate culprit should live in continual disgrace. God help us! What mischief the devil works in this way; how many a soul he may entrap, and how dear their miserable reserve and regard for honour costs people! Having no other director to consult, they imagine that they are preserving religious observance and the reputation of their monastery. Thus Satan schemes to ensnare souls whom he could entrap in no other way. If the poor <term>nuns</term> <note>Women living under religious vows in a cloistered community.</note> ask for another <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest who hears confessions and gives spiritual guidance.</note>, they are told at once that this would destroy all religious discipline, and if he should be a priest of another Order, though as holy as <term>St. Jerome</term> <note>A 4th-century Doctor of the Church known for his great learning and his translation of the Bible into Latin.</note>, to speak to him is said to be an affront to the whole of their own Order. 

Thank God, my daughters, for the liberty you are allowed in this matter, for though you cannot consult a large number of priests, there are some with whom you may confer besides your ordinary confessors, and who can give you the requisite light.² For the love of our Lord, I beg the <term>Superior</term> <note>The nun in charge of the specific convent, often called the Prioress.</note> to maintain this holy liberty, and to get permanent leave from the <term>Bishop</term> <note>The high-ranking official who oversees the local diocese.</note> or <term>Provincial</term> <note>A superior who oversees all the houses of a religious order within a specific geographic province.</note> for the nuns to have, besides their ordinary confessors, learned priests.

---

² <note>The holy Mother <note>St. Teresa herself.</note> often charged her daughters, both by word of mouth and in her books, to endeavour to talk to learned and prudent theologians. She recommended the <term>Dominicans</term> <note>The Order of Preachers, known for their rigorous intellectual training and defense of church doctrine.</note> for this purpose, on account of the sound doctrine taught by this holy Order. You know that the foundation of all must be a good conscience; you ought to make every effort to free yourselves even from <term>venial sin</term> <note>In Catholic teaching, a "lesser" sin that does not result in a complete break with God but weakens the soul's relationship with Him.</note> and to do what is most perfect. Teresa refers to the "letrados" (learned scholars), believing that while piety is good, a director must understand Church law and theology to prevent the sisters from falling into delusions. I dare go further, and say that the sisters should sometimes go to some other priest, even if their confessor possesses all these qualities, for he may be mistaken, and it would not do for all to be misled by him. Nothing, however, must be done against <term>obedience</term> <note>The vow taken by nuns to follow the instructions of their superiors and the rules of their Order.</note>. Matters can always be arranged, and it is worth any trouble to help one soul—how much more to help a number?

3. All this is said to the <term>Prioress</term> <note>The local superior or head of the convent.</note>, whom I again entreat, for the love of God, to give the sisters this consolation, since there are no comforts here except for the soul: God leads people by different ways, and the same confessor may not understand them all. I assure you there will always be holy persons ready to guide and to console you if you live as you ought, however poor you may be. God, Who sustains your bodies and supplies them with food, will incite some one with the good will to enlighten your souls, and to bring a remedy for this evil, which I fear more than any others. When tempted by the <term>devil</term> <note>Specifically, the "enemy of the soul" who Teresa believed sought to trap nuns through false pride or misunderstanding of their spiritual experiences.</note> to levity or to mislead you on any point, the confessor will be more wary and circumspect in his conduct if he knows you will consult some other priest. Therefore, for the love of our Lord, I beg of the <term>Bishop</term> <note>A high-ranking official with authority over the local churches and religious houses in a specific region.</note> for the time being to leave the <term>nuns</term> at liberty on this point, for they will always be his most obedient children, and, when good and learned <term>priests</term> <note>Ordained ministers authorized to administer the sacraments.</note> can be found, not to withdraw the permission. I know this is expedient for many reasons, and that any drawbacks it may possess are as nothing compared with the serious, hidden, and well-nigh irreparable harm that may otherwise be done. In <term>convents</term>, good observance tends to decline quickly if not preserved with great care : evil customs, once begun, are most difficult to eradicate, for imperfections soon grow into habits.

4. I have both seen and heard of what I have been speaking, and have consulted experienced, learned, and holy men, who have considered what is best calculated to advance perfection in this house. Among the many dangers which beset us everywhere in this life, we shall find it safest for no <term>Vicar</term> ⁶ <note>A priest appointed to act as a representative or deputy for a higher authority like a Bishop.</note> to be at liberty to go in or out of the <term>convent</term> or to give such leave to others, nor to give any order : neither shall any <term>confessor</term> hold such power. They are to watch over the <term>religious observance</term> <note>The strict adherence to the rules, vows, and daily duties of the religious life.</note>

...and the piety of the house, as well as its internal and external well-being. If his lordship can trust the <term>Prioress</term> in this matter, he should leave it to her discretion. Since the community is small, this will not require much time. 

This is our current practice, not merely because I advised it, but because it was recommended by the <term>prelate</term> <note>A high-ranking church dignitary, referring here to the Bishop.</note> whom we obey; for many reasons, we are not subject to the <term>Order</term> <note>The larger Carmelite religious organization. He is buried in the convent of St. Joseph in Avila.</note>, comes from a noble family and is a great servant of God. He is highly supportive of all religious observance and holiness. He is a true well-wisher of this house, in both spiritual and practical matters, and he holds it in high regard, desiring that the nuns reach great perfection. 

Therefore, I do not believe our Lord would allow him to be mistaken, as his only goal is the glory of God. He gathered a group of learned, pious, and experienced men who, along with myself—as humble as I am—came to this decision after much prayer. It is only right that future Superiors should, by the grace of God, observe this...

## CHAPTER VI. <note>While this is Chapter 6 in the standard edition, it appears as Chapter 7 in the Valladolid manuscript and Chapter 9 in the Escorial manuscript.</note>

->Returns to the subject of perfect love.<-

*1. **Supernatural love**. 2. Love for others as felt by **perfect souls**. 3. Their **detachment** from seeking the love of others. 4. They realise its worthlessness. 5. **Supernatural friendship**.*

I. I HAVE wandered far from my subject, but no one will blame me who realises the importance of what I have said. Let us return, my sisters, to speak of the love we ought to feel for others, and which is lawful for us: that which I call entirely <term>spiritual love</term> <note>Love that is centered on the soul and its relationship with God, rather than on physical attraction or emotional attachment.</note>. I am not sure whether I understand what I am speaking about: at any rate, I think there is no need to say much about this love, for I fear very few people possess it: if our Lord has granted it to any one amongst you, she should thank and praise Him fervently, for she must have attained great <term>perfection</term> <note>In this context, "perfection" refers to a state of high spiritual maturity and union with God's will.</note>, and perhaps we shall profit by her example. Please God I may even understand it myself, far more that I may succeed in explaining it, for I hardly know when love is <term>spiritual</term> <note>Love directed toward the soul's benefit and God's glory.</note> and when it is partly <term>sensual</term> <note>In this context, love based on emotional feelings or physical attraction rather than spiritual reason.</note>, nor how to speak of it. At times I may talk nonsense : it is most natural to me to do nothing well.

2. Seeing nothing amiable in themselves, they think that others love them for the love of God, and leave <term>His Majesty</term> <note>A title Saint Teresa frequently used to refer to Jesus Christ or God, reflecting the respectful, courtly language of 16th-century Spain.</note> to reward them, as they beg Him to do. 4. Notice, that when we wish for any one’s love, it is always for the sake of self-interest or pleasure: those who are perfect, however, have so trodden under-foot all worldly goods, delights and joys, that, even if they wished, as we might say, they could care for nothing outside God and speaking to others about Him; therefore what gain can human love bring them? They cease to care for it, realising this truth so clearly that they laugh at the thought of how anxious they used to be as to whether their affections were returned or no; for, pure as our love may be, it is natural to wish for it to be repaid. Yet this repayment is but a thing of straw, an airy nothing, blown to and fro by the wind, for after we have been dearly loved, what remains to us? Therefore perfect souls care nothing whether they are cherished or no, except it be by those who can profit them, as I

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. VI.<-

said; for human nature quickly tires of helping those we do not care for. Do you fancy such hearts can love or think of none except God alone? Indeed, they love others far more, with a truer, more generous and intense affection. In a word, this is true love. These souls are ever more ready to give than to receive,⁴ even with their <term>Creator</term> <note>God, as the source of all being. In Teresa's theology, the soul's relationship with the Creator is one of constant exchange, though the soul eventually learns to give itself entirely without seeking reward.</note>. This, I say, merits the name of LOVE, for all other base affections have but usurped the title.

5. You may wonder, if such persons do not love what they see, what it is that they love. They do love what they see, and are drawn by what they hear, but what they see and hear is what is stable. If they care for any one, they do not arrest their eyes on the body, but at once look into the soul to see if it contains aught they can love, or if not, whether it has germs or inclinations which show that, by digging deep enough they will find gold within the mine; loving this soul, no trouble wearies them, no service is too hard for them willingly to render it, for they wish their affection for it to last, which they know is impossible unless their friend possesses <term>virtue</term> <note>Moral excellence and spiritual strength. Teresa argues that a friendship not rooted in virtue is temporary and lacks true substance.</note> and the love of God.⁵ I say it is impossible, though the other should render them immense services and even seek to die for them, and if she should do them every kindness in her power :

->CHAP. VI.] SUPERNATURAL FRIENDSHIP.<-

though she should possess every natural attraction, yet they could not force their **wills** <term>wills</term> <note>In Teresian theology, the "will" is the faculty of the soul that chooses to love. For the advanced soul, the will becomes so aligned with God that it can no longer find rest in purely worldly attachments.</note> to love her nor to remain attached to her. They know, and have learnt by experience, what these are worth, and cannot be cheated with **false coin** <note>A metaphor for superficial qualities like physical beauty, charm, or wealth, which mimic true value but lack spiritual substance.</note>; they see their minds are not in unison, so that they cannot continue to love each other, for if their friend does not love God nor keep His law, they fear their attachment must end with this life and they will go to **different regions** <note>Meaning the spiritual separation in the afterlife, where one soul might ascend toward God while the other does not.</note>. These souls in which our Lord has instilled true **wisdom** hold the affection which ends in this world no higher than its proper value—nay, they hold it cheaper! To those who care for worldly things such as pleasure, honour, and riches, it is of some worth if their friend be wealthy, for the sake of the gaiety and amusement he provides.⁶ Those who have spurned such things beneath their feet care little or nothing for such friendships. If they have any affection for such a one, it is a longing to bring their soul to love God so that they themselves can love her, for, as I said, they realise that no other sort of fondness can last, but will cost them dear. Therefore they make every effort to benefit their friend, and would lose a thousand lives to help her in the least. O priceless love, thus imitating the **Captain of all love, Jesus**, our only good!

# CHAPTER VII. <note>In the Valladolid edition, this is Chapter VIII; in the Escorial edition, it is Chapter XI.</note>

->Continues to treat of spiritual love, and gives advice how to obtain it.<-

1. Unselfishness of supernatural love. 2. Zeal for the loved one’s eternal interests. 3. Benefits conferred by this love on its object. 4. How to gain this love for others. 5. The remembrance of our own defects should make us merciful to others. 6. How to practise perfect charity. 7. A good example is the truest sign of charity. 8. Obstacles to charity in communities.

I. IT is strange to see how passionate is this tenderness! What tears, what **penances** <term>penances</term> <note>Voluntary acts of self-discipline or devotion performed to show sorrow for sins and to strengthen the spirit against worldly desires.</note>, and prayers it costs! How urgent is the care to recommend its object to the **intercession** <term>intercession</term> <note>The act of praying to God, the Virgin Mary, or the Saints on behalf of another person.</note> of all who have power with God! what constant longing for the progress of its friend, and what disquiet if this be wanting! If the loved one seemed to be advancing and then **falls back** <note>Meaning a decline in spiritual fervor or a return to sinful or worldly habits.</note>, the friend who is devoted to her knows no peace in life, and neither eats nor sleeps unhaunted by constant anxiety and dread lest the soul it loves so well should be lost, and they two parted for ever! Such people care nothing for the death of the body—they will not attach themselves to what a breath can deprive us of, beyond our powers of resistance. <note>Teresa argues that physical death is minor compared to the spiritual death of the soul; a true friend fears for the latter, not the former.</note> As I said, there is no thought of self in this affection; its only wish and care is to see its favourite enriched with divine graces. This is love indeed, not like a miserable earthly affection, even though the latter be not faulty—for from an unlawful <note>The text breaks off here as the discussion of the dangers of worldly or "unlawful" affections continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the intense, selfless nature of spiritual love, emphasizing that a true friend's primary concern is the eternal salvation and spiritual progress of their companion. Teresa contrasts this deep, supernatural bond with "miserable earthly affection," noting that spiritual friends are willing to undergo great personal sacrifice for the other's soul.</summary>

<header>CHAP. VII.] SPIRITUAL LOVE. 47</header>

attachment, may God defend us! We need not tire ourselves with inveighing against what is a **perfect hell**, the least of whose ills is beyond exaggeration. We must never mention such a love, sisters, nor remember that it exists, nor must we ever hear it named, either in jest or earnest, nor suffer it to be discussed in our presence. It is utterly worthless, and the very mention of it may harm us. 

But I am speaking of the <term>lawful affection</term> <note>Natural, healthy love and concern for family and friends, which is good but still rooted in human emotion rather than purely divine purpose.</note> we feel for one another, such as is felt between relations and friends. Our one anxiety is that they may not die; if their heads ache, our souls ache too; if they suffer, we lose all patience, and it is the same in other matters. It is not so with **spiritual love**, for, though through human weakness those who feel it grieve at their neighbour's trouble for the moment, yet reason resumes its sway; they reflect whether this **cross** <term>cross</term> <note>A historical and religious metaphor referring to a trial or suffering that a person must endure, seen as an opportunity for spiritual growth in imitation of Jesus.</note> is good for their friend's soul, and whether it increases her virtue; they watch how she bears it; begging God to give her patience, that she may merit by her trials. If they see she is resigned, they are no longer disturbed, but feel happy and consoled.² Though they would rather suffer the pain themselves than let her bear it, if their merit and benefit could all be transferred to her, yet they feel neither distress nor disturbance.

---

² <note>St. Teresa always looked cheerful, however much the trials of her friends may have grieved her. This is noted by the historian Vicente de la Fuente (vol. 6, 310, n. 9) and comes from the legal testimony (deposition) of Mary of St. Francis, one of the nuns who knew her.</note>

<summary>Teresa distinguishes between worldly affection, which suffers emotionally when a friend is in pain, and spiritual love, which prioritizes the soul's growth and remains peaceful if the suffering leads to greater virtue.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual love, lawful affection, suffering, patience, virtue, cross, merit, St. Teresa, Mary of St. Francis, Fuente</keywords>

<page-num>48</page-num>
->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. VII.<-

2. This affection seems exactly like that borne for us by the good <term>Lover, Jesus</term> <note>Teresa often describes Jesus as the "Divine Lover" or "Spouse," emphasizing an intimate, personal relationship with God.</note>: this is why it is so beneficial, for it embraces all the suffering for itself, that others, without suffering, may reap the reward. Even if such souls do not actually perform this, still they strive to lead their friends aright, more by actions than by words. I say, “If they do not actually perform this”; that is, when the thing itself is impossible, for they strive incessantly to labour for, and to benefit, the one they love. Such an affection is a great blessing to its object, for either the friendship must come to an end, or the lover will obtain grace from our Lord that the beloved may travel on the same road as herself, as did **St. Monica** for **St. Augustine**. <note>Saint Monica is the patron saint of mothers; she is famous for her persistent prayers over many years for the conversion of her son, Augustine, who eventually became one of the most influential theologians in Christianity.</note> 

Such a soul will never deal falsely with those it loves, nor allow them to err while believing it can hinder them by <term>reproof</term> <note>A formal or kind correction of a person's faults.</note>, which it will never neglect to give them, because of its ardent desire to see them rich in virtue. What <term>artifices</term> <note>In this context, holy "schemes" or clever strategies used to encourage someone toward a better life.</note> does it not use in order to gain this end, although it cares for no earthly thing! It cannot resist doing this, nor can it flatter its friends, nor dissemble their faults. Either they must amend, or their mutual bond must come to an end, for this soul cannot otherwise endure it, nor ought it to do so, for there can be nothing but war between persons with such different aims. Though such a one may be indifferent to everything else, and take no heed whether they serve God or not, looking

<summary>Teresa describes spiritual love as a selfless, Christ-like devotion that prioritizes a friend's soul over their comfort. She uses the example of St. Monica to show how true friendship requires honesty and constant prayer for the other's spiritual progress.</summary>

only to herself, yet she cannot act thus with her friends ; no fault in them is hidden from her ; she sees the smallest <term>mote</term> <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 7:3; a "mote" is a tiny speck of dust or wood, used here to mean even the most microscopic character flaw.</note>. I call this indeed a heavy cross to bear <note>Teresa suggests that truly loving someone spiritually is painful because you are acutely aware of their failings and feel a deep responsibility for their soul's progress.</note>. Happy the souls thus loved, and blest the day on which they found such a friend !

3. O my God ! of Thy mercy grant that many may feel such love for me. I would far rather win this, O Lord, than the liking of all the kings and princes of the earth, and rightly so, for such affection labours with all its might to make us such that we may spurn the world under our feet, and may hold beneath our sway all that it contains. If you ever meet persons of this kind, sisters, the <term>Mother Prioress</term> <note>The nun in charge of the convent; the local superior.</note> should make every effort to bring you together. Love them as much as you will, there are not many such souls, but our Lord always wishes their perfection to be known : people will tell you that there is no need of such help—God is enough. The company of God’s friends is a good way of keeping near Him : it is of the greatest advantage, as I know from experience ; for, after God Himself, I owe it to such persons that I am not in hell <note>Teresa often speaks of her own perceived unworthiness and "wickedness" with great intensity, crediting the prayers and guidance of holy friends for her spiritual "rescue."</note>. I was most anxious that they should intercede for me with God, and used to beg them to do so. But let us return to our subject.

4. This is the kind of affection I wish we all possessed. <insert>^3</insert>

Although in the beginning our love may be defective, yet our Lord will perfect it. Let us consider the way to obtain perfect love. Although at first we mingle some tenderness with it, no harm will be done, as long as it does not amount to <term>particular friendship</term> <note>In a monastic context, this refers to an exclusive, often emotional or "cliquish" bond between two people that can exclude others and distract from spiritual focus.</note>. It is necessary at times to show some tenderness in our love, $^4$ <note>original: "Castle, M. v. ch. iii. 11." This refers to Teresa’s other masterpiece, *The Interior Castle*, specifically the Fifth Mansion, chapter 3, paragraph 11.</note> and even to feel it; we must sympathise with many of our sisters' <term>trials</term> <note>The difficulties, spiritual or physical, that a person faces as a test of their faith.</note> and weaknesses, insignificant as they may be. $^5$ Sometimes a trifling matter gives as much pain to one person as a heavy cross would cause another. Sensitive natures feel very keenly slight troubles which others would laugh at. If you are stronger-minded, still, pity your neighbours, and do not be astonished at them. Perhaps the devil has taken more trouble to wound their feelings thus than he has to grieve you by severe trials and <term>crosses</term> <note>A common Christian metaphor for a heavy burden or suffering borne for the sake of Christ.</note>. It may be that our Lord spares us these sufferings to give us trials of other kinds, when perhaps what seems, and is, indeed, a heavy cross to us, will appear but a light one to our sisters.

$^4$ *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 5, chapter 3, paragraph 11.

$^5$ "No one was in the company of this glorious Mother without receiving some consolation. <summary>Teresa explains that while spiritual love should be pure, it is necessary to show human tenderness and empathy toward the varying sensitivities and burdens of others. <page-num>51</page-num>
## CHAPTER VII. INDULGENCE FOR OTHERS.

5. We must not judge others by ourselves in such cases, nor compare ourselves to them at a time when, perhaps, through no effort of our own, our Lord has given us greater <term>fortitude</term> <note>The spiritual and emotional strength to endure hardship or temptation with courage.</note>; rather let us estimate ourselves by our weakest moments. There is need for this if we would know how to condole with our neighbours' griefs. Especially is this necessary for more courageous souls, who, as they long for crosses, make little of their troubles. These should remember what they used to feel while they were still weak, and should reflect that, if they have improved, it is not their own doing. Otherwise, the devil may gradually cool our <term>charity</term> <note>The active, selfless love and kindness practiced toward one's community members.</note> for others, and lead us into mistaking an error for perfection. Constant care and vigilance are needed: <term>Satan</term> <note>In the Christian tradition, the personification of evil who seeks to lead souls astray through pride or discouragement.</note> never sleeps, and is always especially wide awake when he watches more perfect souls; then his temptations are more subtle than ever, for he dare use no others; and unless we are very cautious, the mischief is done before we know it.

6. In short, we must always watch and pray,⁶ <note>Teresa emphasizes that spiritual safety requires both alertness and a constant internal dialogue with the Divine.</note> for prayer is the surest way to discover the devil’s snares, and make him reveal himself. Be cordial with your sisters when they take their needful <term>recreation</term> <note>A specific time in the daily monastic schedule for sisters to converse and relax together, intended to maintain mental health and sisterly bonds.</note>,⁷ and

<summary>St. Teresa advises readers to remain humble by remembering their own past weaknesses, preventing spiritual pride from eroding their love for others. She stresses the importance of communal recreation and prayer as defenses against the subtle influences of the devil.</summary>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.] GOOD EXAMPLE.</header>

...God for your sister <note>This text completes the sentence from the previous page: "you must always feel sorrow before God for your sister."</note>, and endeavour to practise perfectly the <term>virtue</term> <note>A specific moral excellence or "good habit" that counteracts a specific vice or fault.</note> contrary to her fault. Force yourself to do this, for you may thus teach her by deed, what by words, or even by punishment, she could never attain to: whereas the sight of this virtue in another would make a great impression on her. This is good advice: do not forget it.

<margin>✓ ✓ ✓</margin>
7. Oh what an excellent and sincere love does that nun show, who sacrifices her own interests to that of her sisters; who makes great progress in all the virtues and in the perfect observance of the <term>Rule</term> <note>The "Rule of St. Albert," the set of laws and religious obligations that govern the daily life of the Carmelite order.</note>! ¹⁰ There is more true friendship in this than in all the tender speeches that can be uttered, and which are not, and must not, be used in this house; such as, “My life, my soul, my darling!” and other expressions people use to one another. Keep endearing words for your <term>Spouse</term> <note>Jesus Christ, referred to in mystical theology as the "Bridegroom" of the soul.</note>; you will often be alone with Him, and will need them all, since <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Teresa’s characteristic way of referring to God or Jesus, acknowledging His royal authority and divine presence.</note> permits their use. However much you may repeat them to Him, they will not make you sentimental, and there is no need to address them to any one else. Such expressions are very womanish, and I do not wish you to be so, nor appear so in any way, but rather, valiant and manly <note>In Teresa’s 16th-century context, "manly" (*varonil*) was a term of praise meaning "courageous," "steady," or "resolute," suggesting a soul that is strong and disciplined in its faith rather than emotionally fragile.</note>. If you do your best, God will make you so strong that men will wonder at you. How easy this is to His Majesty, Who created us out of nothing!

---

¹⁰ *The Interior Castle*, Mansion vii. chapter iv. 22. <note>original: "Castle, M. vii. ch. iv. 22." This refers to Teresa's other foundational work on the stages of prayer and the soul's progress toward God.</note>

<summary>Teresa teaches that the most effective way to correct a sister's fault is by modelling the opposite virtue herself. She also warns against using sentimental or overly affectionate language with one another, urging the sisters to reserve such intimacy for God and to cultivate a courageous, "manly" spiritual strength.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAPTER VII.]

8. It is a great sign of love to relieve others of their work in the duties of the <term>convent</term> <note>The religious house where the nuns live and pray together.</note> and to take those tasks upon ourselves. <margin>✓</margin> <note>original: *Life*, ch. xxxi. 27. Saint Teresa was known for performing humble tasks, such as folding the heavy cloaks (mantles) of the sisters at the Convent of the Incarnation.</note> We should also rejoice and thank God for the <term>spiritual progress</term> <note>The growth of a person's soul in virtue and closeness to God.</note> of our sisters as if it were our own. <note>original: *Life*, ch. xxxiv. 9. This refers to Teresa’s joy at the spiritual growth of Father Garcia de Toledo, a Dominican priest and her confessor.</note> All these actions (besides the great personal benefit they bring) greatly contribute to peace and <term>concord</term> <note>Harmony and unity of heart among the community.</note> among the nuns; by God’s grace, we have learned this through our own experience. May <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Teresa’s common way of referring to God or Jesus, acknowledging His royal and divine authority.</note> be pleased to increase this good feeling among you. 

It would be a terrible thing if it were otherwise, and it would be unbearable for such a small number of sisters to disagree with one another—God forbid! Unless all the good that has been established here by the grace of God were first destroyed, such a misfortune could never happen. If one of you should take offense at some hastily spoken words, let her at once make amends for it and pray fervently about the matter. You must also pray against any habitual fault, or grudge, or "particular friendship," <note>An exclusive, overly emotional attachment between two sisters that could cause jealousy or distract from their devotion to God.</note> or any desire for status or concern for personal honor. <note>original: *The Interior Castle*, Mansions v. ch. iii. 11.</note> 

My blood seems to freeze in my veins while I write this, at the very thought that such a feeling could be held by any nun, for this is the specific poison of <term>convents</term>. If this should ever truly occur, consider yourselves lost. Know that you have driven your <term>Spouse</term> <note>Jesus Christ, often described in mystical tradition as the "Bridegroom" of the religious soul.</note> from the house, and that, in a way, you have forced Him to look for another home, since you have turned...

<summary>Teresa explains that true sisterly love is shown through taking on others' work and rejoicing in their spiritual success. She warns that pride, grudges, and "particular friendships" are a "poison" that drives Christ away from the community.</summary>

<page-num>55</page-num>

->**CHAPTER VII. OBSTACLES TO CHARITY.**<-

...Him out of His own.<insert>✓</insert> Cry for aid to <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Saint Teresa’s respectful and frequent way of referring to God, acknowledging His supreme authority.</note>, for, if your frequent <term>confessions</term> <note>The religious act of admitting sins to a priest to receive absolution.</note> and <term>communions</term> <note>The sacrament of receiving the Eucharist (the Body of Christ).</note> do not prevent such ills, take heed lest there be some <term>Judas</term> <note>A reference to the apostle who betrayed Jesus; here meaning a member of the community who works against its peace and unity.</note> among you. Let the <term>Prioress</term> <note>The mother superior or woman in charge of the convent.</note> be most watchful on this point; let her prevent all chance of its occurring, and stop it from the very first, for here lies all the mischief and its remedy. 

If gentleness will not suffice, let her inflict severe punishment. Should any of the nuns be found to be <term>seditious</term> <note>Promoting rebellion, division, or constant trouble-making within the house.</note>, let her be sent to some other convent—God will provide her <term>dowry</term> <note>The sum of money or property a woman brought with her to the convent for her maintenance. Teresa implies the community should let the money go rather than keep a disruptive person.</note>. Drive away this pestilence, cut off the branches as best you can, or, if this is not enough, pull up the roots. If this cannot be done it were far better that such a person were imprisoned than that the whole community should be infected with such an <term>incurable plague</term> <note>Metaphorical language for the "contagious" nature of bad influence or division in a small group.</note>. 

What a terrible evil it is! God deliver us from a convent where this enters! I would rather it caught fire and burnt us all alive. But as I intend speaking more of this later on, on account of its great importance, I will only say I would rather the sisters should love one another tenderly, with a demonstrative affection, if it be for all alike, although not with the perfect love I have described, than that they should disagree. May our Lord, being Who He is, never permit this. Amen.

<summary>Saint Teresa warns that discord is a spiritual plague and instructs the Prioress to use discipline or even expulsion to preserve the peace of the convent. She emphasizes that communal harmony is more important than even financial stability or personal comfort.</summary>

<header>THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAPTER VIII.]</header>

## CHAPTER VIII. <note>In the version of this text kept at Valladolid, this is Chapter IX; in the Escorial manuscript, it is Chapter XIII.</note>

Treats of the great advantage of <term>detaching</term> <note>The practice of letting go of emotional or physical clinging to worldly things—including people and one's own desires—to make room for God.</note> ourselves from all <term>created things</term> <note>Anything that is not God, such as material goods, social status, or even one's family.</note>, whether interior or exterior. Of the great benefits gained by those who have left the world by severing themselves from their relatives, and how they will meet with far truer friendship instead of that which they have given up.

->1. *Detachment*. 2. *Human affection for our relatives.*<-

1. Now we will consider what is the detachment which we are bound to feel: if this be perfect, it will include everything else. I say “it will include everything else” because, if we cling to our <term>Creator</term> <note>God, the maker of all.</note> alone and care nothing for created things, <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Saint Teresa’s respectful and frequent way of referring to God, acknowledging His supreme authority.</note> will <term>infuse</term> <note>To "pour into"; Teresa suggests that God places these qualities directly into the soul of a person who has made room for Him.</note> the <term>virtues</term> <note>Holy qualities or habits, such as patience, humility, and love.</note> into us, so that, doing by degrees all that is in our power, we shall have little left with which to struggle, for our Lord will defend us against the <term>devils</term> <note>The spiritual forces of temptation or evil.</note> and the whole world as well. Do you think that it is a small gain to give ourselves entirely to Him, keeping nothing for ourselves, since in His goodness all is contained, as I told you? <note>Teresa previously discussed this in Chapter II, section 8 of this book, and in her work *The Interior Castle*, Mansion VI, chapter VI, section 14.</note> Be very grateful to Him, sisters, for bringing us here together, where this is all that we care about. I cannot tell why I am speaking about it to you, all of whom are capable of teaching me on the subject, for I own that, in this respect, I am not as perfect as I wish, and as I know that I ought to be;

<summary>Saint Teresa begins a lesson on detachment, arguing that by letting go of all created things and family ties, a person allows God to protect them and fill them with virtue.</summary>
<keywords>detachment, created things, His Majesty, divine protection, virtues, family ties, spiritual friendship</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter VIII, transitioning from the definition of detachment to Saint Teresa’s personal reflection on her own shortcomings and the immense privilege of the religious vocation. She emphasizes that the isolation of the convent is a gift from God intended to remove worldly distractions.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] DETACHMENT.</header>

indeed, I am the most imperfect of you all.³ I have to say the same with regard to all the <term>virtues</term> <note>Holy qualities or habits, such as patience, humility, and love.</note> and other matters which I treat of here, for it is easier to write of them than to practise them. However, I may often fail even to write well of them, for sometimes one can only speak correctly of such things by experience, so that if I do succeed it must be by describing them as the opposite of my own qualities.⁴ However, at your request, I will mention some matters which occur to me. It is clear that, here, our lives are cut off from all outward things: our Lord seems to wish to deprive us of all that would hold us captive to this world, so that He may, without impediment, draw us to Himself. O my <term>Creator</term> <note>God, the maker of all.</note> and my <term>Sovereign</term>! <note>A title for God emphasizing His supreme kingship and authority.</note> how have I deserved this great honour? Thou seemest to have sought for means whereby Thou mightest come nearer to us. By Thy mercy, permit us not to lose Thee. O my sisters! for the love of God, consider what a signal <term>grace</term> <note>A free and undeserved gift from God.</note> our Lord has shown to those whom He has brought here. Let each of you realise it for herself, since of the twelve <term>nuns</term>, <note>Teresa initially limited her reformed convents to thirteen members (one prioress and twelve sisters) to maintain a small, intimate community modeled after Christ and His apostles.</note> <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Saint Teresa’s respectful and frequent way of referring to God, acknowledging His supreme authority.</note> has chosen her for one. And how many, what a multitude, better than myself do I know of, who would joyfully accept my place! Yet our Lord has bestowed this favour on me, who so ill deserve it. 2. It astonishes me to see what harm the society <note>In this context, the company or frequent interaction with relatives.</note> of our kindred does us. <page-num>61</page-num>
->### CHAP. IX.] DETACHMENT FROM RELATIVES.<-

...daughters, we must pray a good deal for our relatives, after having interceded for the Church <note>To intercede is to offer prayers on behalf of others; Teresa suggests prioritizing the universal Church over personal family needs.</note>, as I told you: this is only right; but having done this, we must blot them out of our memory as far as possible, because it is natural to us to fix our affection on them in preference to others. My relations were extremely fond of me, people say, and I loved them so much that I would not allow them to forget me. Yet I have learnt from experience, both in my own case and that of others, that (with the exception of parents, who only in very rare cases refuse succour <note>Help, aid, or assistance given in a time of difficulty.</note> to their children), when I have been in need, my own kith and kin <note>An old-fashioned term for one's friends, acquaintances, and family relations.</note> have helped me least of all, and it has been the servants of God who have come to my aid. It is right, however, when our father and mother require comfort from us, that we should not hold ourselves aloof from them: this is consistent with perfect <term>detachment</term> <note>In a religious context, detachment is the spiritual practice of being free from emotional or material dependency on anything other than God.</note>. The same applies to our brothers and sisters.

3. Believe me, sisters, if you serve God as you ought, you will find no better kindred than those servants of His whom He will send you. I am convinced of the truth of this, and if you do what you know to be right (for to take any other course would be to fail in your duty to your true Friend and Spouse <note>This refers to Jesus Christ; in the Catholic tradition, nuns are spiritually "married" to Christ upon taking their vows.</note>), you may be sure that in a very short time you will gain this liberty of spirit <note>An internal state of freedom where a person is no longer governed by their own desires or the expectations of the world.</note>. Those who love you for His sake alone, who are more to be relied on than all your relatives, will

If, in spite of all my imperfections, I understand this so well, how far better more spiritual persons must realise it! As I said, much has been written elsewhere on this matter; many books hardly treat of anything else. There is no doubt that the saints do right in advising us to fly from the world <note>To "fly from the world" means to withdraw from secular society and its distractions to focus on spiritual life.</note>. ### **That this <term>abnegation</term> <note>The act of renouncing or surrendering one's own interests and desires.</note> is not enough unless we also are detached from ourselves: how this virtue and humility go together.**

->*1. Detachment from self-will. 2. Humility and <term>mortification</term> <note>The practice of self-discipline or overcoming physical or worldly desires for spiritual growth.</note>. 3. I. SEPARATED from the world and our own <term>kindred</term> <note>Family or relatives.</note>, in a state of absolute poverty, and enclosed in this convent, under the conditions I have described, it would seem that we have done all, and that there is nothing left to contend with. You would be like a man who goes peacefully to rest, after having bolted his doors securely against the robbers who are already inside them. -># CHAP. X.] EFFECTS OF HUMILITY.<-

are not the kindred I advise you to forsake: embrace and love them, and never be seen without them. <meta>pencil check mark</meta>

3. O sovereign virtues! <note>Teresa refers here specifically to the "kindred" of humility and detachment.</note> rulers of all created things; queens of the world; our deliverers from all the snares and traps of the devil, dearly loved as you were by our Teacher, Jesus Christ, Who never for a moment lived without you! Those who possess you may sally forth and fight with all hell and the whole world and its temptations! Let them not be afraid, for “theirs is the kingdom of heaven”: <gloss>6</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Quoniam ipsorum est regnum cœlorum"</note> they have no cause for dread, for they care not if all be lost—nor do they count it loss—their sole fear is lest they should offend their God; they implore Him to preserve these virtues in their souls, lest by their own fault they should lose them. It is true that these virtues have the property of hiding themselves from their owner's sight, so that he never believes he possesses them, whatever he may be told to the contrary. Yet he esteems them so highly that he is always striving to gain them, and thus grows more perfect in them. The possessor of these virtues soon unwittingly reveals them to those who talk to him.

4. But what presumption for me to praise humility and **mortification** <note>The practice of self-denial or "dying" to one's own desires to better follow God.</note>, which have been so extolled by the King of Glory, and exemplified by all His toils and sufferings! These are the virtues that you must labour to obtain in order to escape from the **Land of Egypt** <note>A biblical metaphor common in mystical writing representing the state of spiritual bondage, sin, or worldly distraction.</note>.

My daughters, when you possess them <note>The virtues of humility and detachment.</note>, you will find the **manna** <gloss>7</gloss> <note>A biblical reference to the "hidden manna" (Revelation 2:17) promised to those who overcome; it represents the sweet, spiritual nourishment provided by God.</note>: then all things will taste sweet to you: however bitter the world may find them, to you they will be delicious. Our first effort must be to cease loving our bodies <gloss>8</gloss>; some of us are naturally self-indulgent, therefore this is no easy work. Considering that we are nuns, it is surprising what a struggle these two things cost us, just as they do other people. Some of us seem to think we only entered the convent in order to keep ourselves alive, and to nurse and pamper our bodies as well as we can, and this constitutes our principal pleasure. To tell the truth, there is very little opportunity of practising this here, but I do not want you even to wish for such a thing. Be convinced, sisters, that you came here to die for Christ, not to indulge yourselves for Him. The devil suggests that we need to take care of ourselves in order to observe the **Rule** <note>The "Primitive Rule" of the Carmelites, which Teresa restored, emphasizing austerity, silence, and prayer.</note>. Such nuns are so exceedingly anxious to preserve their health so that they may be able to obey the Rule, that they die without ever having observed it for a month, or perhaps even for a single day. If good health is our object in life, I do not know why we came here. There is no fear lest we should fail in **discretion** <note>The virtue of moderation; Teresa is being slightly sarcastic here, suggesting that people are usually too careful of their health rather than not careful enough.</note> on this point, for our confessors at once

<page-num>67</page-num>

### CHAP. X.] ANXIETY ABOUT HEALTH.

take fright lest we should kill ourselves with **penances** <term>voluntary acts of self-discipline or physical hardship performed to show sorrow for sin</term>; and I wish our other faults of observance were as odious to us as is such want of **prudence** <term>the quality of being cautious or sensible, which Teresa argues is here being used as an excuse for laziness</term>.

5. I know that those who practise the contrary will pay no attention to what I am writing, nor do I care if they say that I judge others by myself, which is true enough. I am sure that there are more who behave as I do, than there are nuns who are offended at my words, because they never, themselves, act in such a way. I believe that this is why our Lord permits us to have delicate constitutions; at least, He has shown me great mercy in doing so in my case, for, as I was sure to pamper myself, He wished me to have some excuse for it.⁹ <note>Teresa refers here to her own lifelong struggles with illness, which she discusses in her autobiography (Life, chapter 24) and her "Spiritual Relations" (account 11).</note> It is amusing to see how some worry themselves about this. At one time they have a mania for doing penances, without either **moderation** or **discretion** ¹⁰ <note>Teresa emphasizes that spiritual exercises must be balanced; she references similar advice in her books "The Interior Castle" (Mansions 1, chapter 2) and "The Way of Perfection" (chapter 39).</note>: this lasts for a day or so, and then the devil puts it into their heads that penance makes them ill, so henceforth they never perform any more, even when the **Rule** <note>The "Primitive Rule" of the Carmelites, the foundational set of laws for the order.</note> enjoins it, as they have found it injures them.¹¹ <note>Citing "The Interior Castle" (Mansions 3, chapter 2) and the "Constitutions" (the specific administrative laws of the convent).</note> We do not obey even the least points of the Rule, such as **silence** <term>the practice of refraining from speaking to foster internal prayer</term>, which could do us no harm. Directly we fancy our head aches, we stop away from **choir** <note>The communal gathering for the Divine Office, the formal sung prayers of the Church.</note>, which would not kill us either. <page-num>68</page-num>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. X.<-

absent because it aches, the next because it has ached, and three more, lest it should ache again; but we love to invent **penances** <term>acts of self-discipline or physical hardship performed to show sorrow for sin</term> for ourselves, so that we end by doing neither the one nor the other. Sometimes we feel stronger, yet we think we are not obliged to **mortify** <term>to subdue bodily desires or passions through self-denial</term> ourselves, but that by having obtained leave we are henceforth dispensed from everything.

6. You may ask why the **Prioress** <term>the woman in charge of the convent</term> gives these **dispensations** <term>official exemptions from a religious rule or obligation</term>? If she could see into the state of your body, perhaps she would refuse, but you tell her there is need of them, and the doctor supports your cause, while a friend or relation stands by weeping, and though the poor Prioress sometimes sees that too much is asked for, yet what is she to do? She feels a **scruple** <term>a feeling of doubt or hesitation regarding the morality of an action</term> lest she should be wanting in **charity** <term>the virtue of kindness and divine love toward others</term>, and would rather you erred than that she should, while it seems unjust to judge you harshly. Oh these complaints! God help us, among nuns! May He forgive me for saying so, but I believe it has already become the custom. I once saw a case of this kind: a nun said she had a headache, and complained of it a great deal. When it came to be inquired into, she felt no pain at all in her head, but suffered in some other part of her body. Such things may occur here at times, so I warn you against them now, for if the **devil** <note>In Teresa’s writings, the devil is often depicted as a psychological manipulator who uses our natural fears—like the fear of illness—to pull us away from our spiritual commitments.</note> once begins to daunt us with the fear of losing our health, we shall never do anything. May God give us light to accomplish all our **duties**. Amen.

<header>CHAPTER XI. ILLNESS.</header>

# CHAPTER XI.<gloss>The Valladolid edition lists this as chapter XII; the Escorial edition as chapter XVII.</gloss>

->Continues to discuss **mortification** <term>the practice of subduing bodily desires or passions through self-denial</term> and explains how to practice it during illness. How little this life should be valued by one who truly loves God.<-

->*1. How a **religious** <term>a person bound by monastic vows, such as a nun or monk</term> should behave during illness. 2. Sufferings endured by those living in the world. 3. How to master our bodies. 4. Strong resolution needed.*<-

1. To be continually complaining about our slight ailments appears to me, sisters, to be a very great imperfection. Say nothing about them if you can help it. When the illness is severe, it is self-evident—this is quite another sort of complaining, which at once makes itself known. Remember, you are few in number, and if any of you has this habit, she will weary all the rest, provided that love and **charity** <term>the virtue of selfless, divine love for others</term> reign among you. If the illness is real, speak about it and take the necessary remedies<gloss>Referring to the Constitutions, section 22, and the book of Visitations, section 10.</gloss>: if you have lost **self-love** <term>the tendency to prioritize one's own comfort and ego over spiritual growth</term>, you will so dislike indulgence that there will be no fear of your wanting more than necessary, nor of your complaining without good cause. When there is a genuine reason, you do right in mentioning it: this is far better than taking anything extra without leave, and it would be very wrong of your sisters not to pity you. I am confident that, wherever prayer and charity prevail, in however small a degree, the

<summary>Saint Teresa advises the sisters to avoid complaining about minor illnesses to prevent burdening the community, emphasizing that true illness is obvious and should be treated with common sense and transparency.</summary>

The body possesses this defect—the more you give it, the more it requires. It is wonderful how fond it is of comfort, and what pretexts it will offer to obtain it, however little needed; it deceives the unfortunate soul, and prevents it making progress. Remember how many poor people are ill and have no one to complain to—poverty and ease do not go together. Think, too, what a number of married women there are, many of them, as I know, of good position in life, who, lest they should annoy their husbands, dare not speak of the serious maladies and

# SUFFERINGS.
## CHAPTER XI

...poignant trials from which they are suffering. Sinner as I am, no, we did not come here to be better treated than they are! <note>Teresa refers to the poor and those in the world who suffer without the spiritual support of a community.</note> How free you are from the great troubles of the world; learn, then, to suffer some little thing for the love of God, without every one knowing it. When a woman has made an unhappy marriage, she does not say so, nor lament about it, for fear her husband should know: she is very wretched, but confides in no one: shall not we, then, keep secret between God and ourselves some of the ills He sends us for our sins? all the more, because speaking of it does not lighten our load.

3. What I have said does not apply to serious illness, attended with high fever, although even then, I beg of you to be reasonable and patient <gloss>5</gloss> <note>original: "Rel. iii. 2." This refers to Saint Teresa’s *Spiritual Relations*, a collection of reports she wrote for her confessors regarding her internal state.</note>; but I allude to slight ailments with which we can go about without troubling other people concerning them. But what will happen if this is read by any one outside the **convent** <term>a community of nuns living under religious vows</term>? What will all the **nuns** <term>women who have taken vows to live a life of prayer and service, typically in a cloistered environment</term> say of me? How willingly I would bear this, if I could help any one to improve! If one nun constantly complains, at last her sisters will not believe any one else who says she is really ill, even though the doctor may confirm her words. However, as I am only speaking to my daughters they will forgive me for what I have written. Let us remember our holy Fathers, the ancient **hermits** <term>early Christian monastics, such as the Desert Fathers or the early Carmelites, who lived in solitude and extreme austerity</term>, whose...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XI regarding physical discipline and the suppression of minor complaints. It transitions from comparing the nuns' lives to those in the secular world to comparing them to the early ascetic saints.</meta>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XI.<-

...lives we profess to imitate: what sufferings they bore, what solitude, what cold and hunger, what scorching sunshine and heat, with no one to complain to but God! do you think they were made of iron?⁶ <note>Teresa is referring to the "Desert Fathers," early Christian hermits who lived in extreme austerity in the Egyptian wilderness.</note> They were flesh and blood like ourselves. Believe me, daughters, when once we begin to subdue our wretched bodies, they do not trouble us so much. It is enough for you to see to what is needful. Do not desire anything extra, unless it is absolutely necessary.

4. Unless we resolve, once for all, to resign ourselves to death and ill-health, we shall never do anything. Endeavour to lose all fear of them, and to leave everything in God's hands, come what may. What does it matter if we die? How many times have not our bodies mocked us? Let us mock them for once. Trust me, this resolution is more important than can be realised, for by keeping faithfully to it, and practising it little by little, until it becomes a habit, with the grace of God we shall **vanquish the flesh** <term>the spiritual practice of overcoming physical desires, discomforts, and the instinct for self-preservation to focus entirely on the divine</term>, and you will experience the truth of what I say. Victory over such an enemy goes far to carry us through the battle of life. May God grant it to us, since He has the power, and we need His grace in all things: may He bring it to pass for

[PAGE 73]
### CHAP. XII.] SELF-ABNEGATION.

His own sake. I am certain that only those who rejoice in this conquest can estimate its value, which is so great that I believe no one will regret any sufferings that have been endured in order to gain such peace and self-command.

# CHAPTER XII <gloss>Valladolid edition, chapter 13; Escorial edition, chapter 18.</gloss>

->That one who truly loves God must care little for life or honour. That one who seeks after perfection must despise honours.<-

1. *Having renounced all else, those in religious life must renounce themselves.* 2. *The religious life as a martyrdom.* 3. *Joy brought by mortification* <term>mortification: the practice of self-denial and spiritual discipline to overcome worldly desires</term>. 4. *Contemplation is incompatible with a desire for honour or riches.* 5. *Forsaking all things.* 6. *Humility.* 7. *Proud thoughts corrected by outward acts of humility.* 8. *Evil arising in convents from a regard for honour.* 9. *Wrong done by taking offence.*

I. LET us now speak of other small matters which are very important, insignificant as they may appear. All this seems an enormous work, as indeed it is, being a warfare carried on against ourselves; yet, when once we begin it, God so works in our souls, bestowing on them numerous graces, that all we can do in this life seems but a trifle. We nuns have done the greater part of our task; we have given up our liberty for the love of God, and placed it in the power of another. We endure so much in our labours, <gloss>Refers to Rule 13, Constitutions 23, and Visitation II <note>These are the governing documents and regulations of the Discalced Carmelite Order founded by Teresa.</note></gloss> fasts, <gloss>Refers to Rule 10 and Constitutions 11.</gloss> silence, <gloss>Refers to Rule 14 and Constitutions 5.</gloss>

<summary>Saint Teresa introduces Chapter XII, framing the religious life as a spiritual "warfare" against the self. She argues that having already surrendered their physical liberty, nuns must now focus on the harder task of surrendering their desire for honor and social status.</summary>
<keywords>self-abnegation, religious life, mortification, humility, monastic rules, Carmelites, spiritual warfare, honor</keywords>

enclosure <note>The practice of remaining within the walls of the convent to foster a life of prayer.</note>,<sup>5</sup> attendance at choir <note>The communal gathering of nuns to sing the Divine Office at set times throughout the day.</note>,<sup>6</sup> that, however strongly we may wish for our ease, we can rarely enjoy it,<sup>7</sup> and perhaps, among the many convents I have visited, I may be the only nun who tries to obtain it! 2. This interior mortification, as I said, is acquired little by little, through never following our own wills or liking, even in the most trifling matters, until we have subdued the body to the spirit. I repeat, that this is entirely, or at least, mainly accomplished by renouncing all care for ourselves and our own pleasure. If we have really begun to serve our Lord, the least we can offer Him is our life, after having yielded our will to Him. What is there to fear in this? Whoever is a true religious <note>In this context, a "religious" refers to a person who has taken formal vows in a religious order, like a nun or monk.</note>, or is genuinely devoted to prayer and aspires to enjoy divine consolations, should be convinced that she must not recoil from wishing either to die,<sup>8</sup>

->### CHAPTER XII. MARTYRDOM<-

...or to suffer martyrdom for His sake. <note>The text concludes a thought from the previous page: a person truly devoted to prayer must be willing to face death or suffering for the sake of God.</note> <note>One of the sisters once asked Mother Teresa how to become a saint. She replied: "Daughter, we are soon going to make a foundation <note>foundation: the establishment of a new convent</note>; then you will learn the way." During the difficult process of establishing the new house, the sister faced many trials and complained to Teresa. The Saint replied: "Daughter, did you not ask me to teach you how to become a saint? This is the way"—implying that holiness is found in enduring suffering for God’s sake. (Source: Vicente de la Fuente, volume 6, page 318, numbers 20, 21. From the testimony of Isabel of Jesus.)</note> Do you not know, sisters, that the life of a good religious <term>religious: a person who has taken formal vows in a religious order, such as a nun or monk</term>, and of one who wishes to be among the most intimate friends of God, is one long martyrdom? "Long," because, compared to decapitation, which lasts but an instant, it may be termed *long*; but a whole life-time is short, and, sometimes, very short indeed. How do we know but that ours may not be so short as to end in an hour, or even in a moment, after we have determined to give ourselves entirely to God? It is quite possible, for we cannot depend on anything that passes away, much less on life, on which we must not reckon for a single day. Who, thinking each hour to be his last, would not spend it in labour?

3. Trust me, this is the safest view to take, therefore we must learn to cross our wills in everything; although we may not succeed at once, yet little by little, by the help of prayer, as I said, without knowing how, we shall reach the summit. But how rigorous it sounds to say that we must never please ourselves, unless we are told of the consolation this self-denial brings with—

<summary>Teresa describes the life of a dedicated nun as a "long martyrdom" of the self, emphasizing that because life is fragile and uncertain, one should live every hour as if it were their last. She argues that while denying one's own will is difficult, it eventually leads to spiritual peace and the "summit" of prayer.</summary>
<keywords>martyrdom, religious life, self-denial, will, sanctity, St. Teresa of Avila, Isabel of Jesus, Vicente de la Fuente, monastic discipline</keywords>
