->### THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XII.]<-

...in it, and of the security it obtains during this life! But, as you all practise it here, the principal part is already done: each one is urging her sisters on, and strives to excel the rest. Be most watchful over your secret feelings, especially such as concern **precedence**. <note>precedence: the priority in rank, seniority, or importance given to one person over another.</note> <margin>Cross-references to Teresa's other works: *The Life of St. Teresa of Avila*, chapters 21 and 27; *Spiritual Relations*, 1.28; *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 5, chapter 3; *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 2.</margin> God deliver us, for the sake of His **Passion**, <note>Passion: The suffering and death of Jesus Christ.</note> from saying, or from deliberately thinking, "I am her senior in the Order": "I am older than she": "I have done more work": "She is better treated than I am."

4. When such thoughts arise in your minds, you must suppress them at once: if you dwell upon them or give them utterance, they will prove contagious, and will give rise to great evils in a **religious house**. <term>religious house: a community where people live under religious vows, such as a convent or monastery.</term> Should your **Prioress** <term>Prioress: the nun in charge of the convent.</term> allow anything of this kind, however slight it may be, you must believe that God has permitted her to hold that office in punishment for your sins, and that it is the beginning of your ruin. Cry to Him for aid, and let all your prayers be directed to obtain a remedy, for you are in danger. A **religious**, or a person who practises prayer with the genuine resolution of obtaining the graces and joys God grants to souls, is bound to this **detachment** from everything. <note>In the Escorial edition <note>Escorial edition: one of the original manuscript versions of this book kept at the Royal Library of San Lorenzo de El Escorial.</note>, chapter 19, this is explained further: "That care for honor and for the wisdom of this world must be avoided, in order to arrive at true wisdom."</note>

5. You may think I insist too much on this, and that...

<summary>Teresa warns the sisters against the spiritual danger of seeking status or seniority (precedence) within the convent, calling it a "contagion" that leads to ruin. She emphasizes that true prayer requires total detachment from worldly concerns of honor and rank.</summary>

->### CHAPTER XII. FORSAKING ALL THINGS.<-

<margin>
$^{12}$ Matthew 19:27, Mark 10:28, Luke 18:28: “We have left all things, and followed You.” <note>original: "Reliquimus omnia, et secuti sumus Te."</note>
$^{13}$ *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 2, 30, 32, 33; *The Way of Perfection*, chapter 36, 2–7.
</margin>

...I treat it with excessive severity, as God bestows His consolations on souls wanting in this detachment. Doubtless this is the case, for by His infinite wisdom He knows they can be thus drawn to **forsake all things** for His sake. By “forsaking all things” $^{12}$ I do not necessarily mean **entering religion**, <term>entering religion: the act of joining a religious order, such as becoming a nun or monk, and taking formal vows.</term> for there may be obstacles preventing this, and in every state perfect souls may be **detached** and humble; however, this is more difficult whilst living in the world, for our surroundings influence us strongly. But rest assured of this—people may desire honours or possessions in monasteries as well as outside them (yet the sin is greater as the temptation is less),$^{13}$ but such souls, although they may have spent years in prayer, or rather in **speculations** <note>speculations: intellectual or abstract thoughts about God that lack the transformative power and humility of true, heart-centered prayer.</note> (for perfect prayer eventually destroys these vices), will never make great progress nor enjoy the real fruit of prayer.

6. Ascertain, sisters, whether you care for these **trifles**, <note>trifles: here referring to petty concerns about social status, seniority, or personal recognition within the convent.</note> for you came here that you might spurn them. They give you no increase of honour, and you miss the advantages which would have brought you more honour in the end; so discredit and loss are here combined. Let each one examine whether she is truly humble, and she will learn what progress she has made.

<summary>Teresa explains that while God may comfort souls who are still attached to the world to draw them toward Him, true spiritual progress and the "fruit of prayer" require total detachment from honors. She warns her nuns that seeking status within the convent is a self-defeating "trifle" that prevents genuine humility.</summary>
<keywords>detachment, religious life, monastery, prayer, humility, St. Teresa of Avila, Way of Perfection, Conceptions of the Love of God, spiritual progress, monastic discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 12 of "The Way of Perfection." It follows a discussion on how souls who cling to desires for rank or honor—even within a convent—cannot reach the heights of perfect prayer. Saint Teresa is now explaining how humility serves as a defense against the devil's temptations regarding social status.</meta>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XII.<-

I do not think the devil would dare to tempt a lowly heart with even the suggestions of a wish for precedence, <note>precedence: the desire to be ranked higher than others or to receive special honor because of one’s seniority or status.</note> for he is sagacious enough to fear the wound he would receive.

Such a temptation of the evil one can only strengthen and increase this virtue in a truly humble heart, which will reflect upon its former life, the little service it has rendered to our Lord, compared with what it owed Him, and the wonders He performed in **abasing Himself** to give us an example of lowliness: it will recall its sins, and remember that it has deserved hell in return for them. These reflections so benefit the mind that Satan dares not return next day, for fear of getting a **broken head**. <note>broken head: a vivid, colloquial expression used by Teresa to describe the devil being soundly defeated and humiliated by the soul's humility.</note>

7. Take this advice from me, and do not forget it—that not only should you gain the victory within your own heart (where it would be very wrong not to benefit by the temptation), but, even outwardly, you ought to let your sisters be the gainers by its means, if you would be avenged on the devil and escape from the repetition of such thoughts. As soon as they arise, tell the **Prioress** <term>Prioress: the nun in charge of the convent, serving as its spiritual and administrative head.</term> of them, and ask her to give you some very **mean employment**, <note>mean employment: lowly or menial tasks, such as cleaning or kitchen work, intended to help the nun practice humility.</note> or else of your own accord do any sort of work of the kind. Meanwhile, study how to subdue your will in the things you are most averse to (our Lord will show you many ways of doing this): and perform some **public penances** <note>public penances: acts of self-discipline or expressions of repentance performed in front of the community to counteract pride.</note> such as are usual in the house. <margin>Constitutions, rule 25. <note>original: "Const. 25."</note></margin>

<summary>Saint Teresa explains how the devil's attempts to stir up pride actually strengthen a humble soul, and she provides practical advice on using menial labor and confession to the Prioress to defeat such temptations.</summary>
<keywords>humility, temptation, Satan, Prioress, penance, lowliness, abasement, Way of Perfection, menial labor, spiritual discipline</keywords>

->### CHAP. XII.<-
->### POINTS OF HONOUR.<-

Thus the temptation will quickly vanish. God deliver us from people who try to serve Him, yet who care for their **honour** or fear **disgrace**. What we gain by this only does us harm: as I said, honour is lost by those who seek it; above all, by <term>religious</term>, <note>religious: individuals, such as monks or nuns, who have taken formal vows to live according to a specific spiritual rule.</note> especially in the matter of **rank**, <note>rank: in the 16th century, the order of seniority and status within a convent was strictly observed, often causing spiritual pride or resentment.</note> for no poison in the world is so fatal to perfection.

8. You may say that this is a trifling fault, and only human nature; that it is of no importance. Do not hold it lightly; it spreads in monasteries as quickly as **foam gathers on the sea**; <note>A metaphor for how small, seemingly insignificant resentments can multiply rapidly and cloud one's spiritual vision if they are not addressed.</note> nothing can be called trifling in so great a danger as these **points of honour** and sensitiveness about affronts. Would you like to know the chief reason—not to speak of other causes? Some slight annoyance, hardly worth mentioning, is offered you, and the devil instigates one of your sisters to consider it a grave insult: she even thinks it a charity to tell you of it and to ask you how you could bear such an injury: she says she begs God to grant you patience; she should offer it up to Him, for a saint could bear no more.

9. In short, the evil one makes mischief through another’s tongue: so that although you are resolved to suffer meekly, you are tempted to **vainglory** <note>vainglory: excessive pride in one’s own achievements or spiritual standing; it is "vain" because it seeks the praise of people rather than God.</note> on account of what you have not borne as well as you ought to have done. Human nature is so weak, that though we overcome the temptation by telling ourselves there 

<summary>Saint Teresa warns that the pursuit of honor and social rank is a fatal poison to spiritual perfection, often fueled by the misplaced "charity" of others who stir up feelings of resentment. She argues that even small concerns about status can quickly grow into major spiritual obstacles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "points of honor" from Chapter 12. St. Teresa warns that when sisters sympathize with each other's perceived slights, they actually strengthen the devil's influence and weaken their own spiritual progress.</meta>

->### THE WAY OF PERFECTION [CHAPTER 13]<-

...is no cause for annoyance, yet we think we have done something praiseworthy and feel we have been injured—how much more so when others agree with us? This makes the trouble worse; we believe we are in the right. Our souls lose an opportunity to gain merit and are left weaker than before, while a door is opened to Satan through which he can return another time to tempt us more severely. 

It may even happen that, when you are willing to bear an injury, someone may come and ask you: “Are you to be treated like a dog? Surely everyone ought to have some self-respect!” Oh, for the love of God, sisters, never show such indiscreet sympathy for one another regarding mere fancied injuries. This is like the "comfort" shown to holy Job by his wife and friends. <note>In the biblical Book of Job, his companions and wife famously offered poor advice that encouraged bitterness rather than spiritual endurance.</note>

## CHAPTER XIII <margin>Valladolid edition, chapter 14; Escorial manuscript, continuation of chapter 19.</margin> <note>These refer to the two primary surviving manuscript versions of St. Teresa’s text.</note>

### Continues the subject of mortification: that a nun must avoid the touchiness and "wisdom" of the world in order to attain true wisdom. <term>mortification: the spiritual practice of self-discipline and "dying to oneself" to overcome worldly desires.</term>

-># CHAP. XIII.] POINTS OF HONOUR.<-

...leagues away from saying, “I was in the right: it was not right for me to suffer this, they had no right to do such a thing to me!” ² <note>original: "Const. 30." This refers to the thirtieth rule of the *Constitutiones* (Constitutions), the governing regulations St. Teresa wrote for her reformed convents.</note> Now God deliver us from such wrong rights! Do you think that there was any question of rights when our good Jesus suffered the injuries which were so unrighteously inflicted on Him? I do not know what any person is doing in a <term>monastery: a secluded residence where religious sisters live under vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience.</term> who will only bear a cross which people have a perfect right to lay upon her—let her go back to the world where people care nothing for such rights. Is it possible for you to suffer so much that you ought not to suffer any more? What rights have you in this? I do not know of them. When we receive **honours** or affection or kind treatment let us think what right have we to them, for certainly we have no right to them in this life; but when wrong is done to us we call it so, though it does us no wrong—I do not know why we should ever speak of it. Either we are <term>Brides of this great King: a mystical title for nuns, suggesting that their primary relationship and loyalty is to Jesus Christ.</term> or we are not. If we are, what faithful wife does not share her **husband’s disgrace**, even against her will? In short, they share both honour and shame together. To seek to share in His Kingdom and to enjoy His presence, and yet to shun all parts in His <term>ignominy: public shame, humiliation, or loss of reputation.</term> and His <term>toils: strenuous physical or spiritual labors and struggles.</term>, is incompatible. God preserve us from such a wish! Let her who believes that her sisters hold her the last of all, think herself the most fortunate, as indeed she is, if she bears it as she ought,

2. But what presumption for me to write “You may trust my words!” when He Who is true Wisdom <note>Jesus Christ</note> tells us so, as does the Queen of Angels <note>The Virgin Mary</note>! Let us, my daughters, imitate, however feebly, the most holy Virgin, whose <term>habit: the distinctive clothing or uniform worn by members of a religious order.</term> we wear. Indeed, we feel ashamed to number ourselves among her nuns, because, however deeply we seem to humble ourselves, we are most unworthy to be called the daughters of such a mother, and the brides of such a <term>Spouse: a mystical reference to Jesus Christ, to whom the nuns are spiritually married.</term>, even if we copy her humility in any way—“in any way,” I say. However, though we may all abase and humble ourselves, no one is so bound to do this as myself, who, for my sins, deserve to be insulted and despised by the devils themselves. Yet, although others may not have committed so many faults, it will be wonderful if they have done nothing that deserves hell. Therefore, I repeat, you must not consider the defects I have spoken of as insignificant, for, if you do not check them carefully, what seems a trifle to-day will become a <term>venial sin: in Catholic theology, a "lesser" sin that does not result in complete separation from God but weakens the soul’s grace.</term> to-morrow; this is a thing of such evil growth that,

### [CHAP. XIII.] PUNCTILIOUSNESS. <note>The original Spanish concept is "puntos de honra" (points of honor), referring to an obsessive concern with social status, reputation, and perceived slights.</note>

if left alone, it will spread, and is most injurious in communities. We must be very watchful in such matters, lest we injure those who are trying to help us and to give us a good example. If we only realised the immense harm that is done by introducing the bad habit of touchiness about honour, we should die a thousand times rather than be the means of doing so. That would be only the death of the body, but the loss of souls is a terrible one, and seems never-ending, for when one generation dies, others succeed it, and perhaps they will all be influenced more by the one bad custom we began,⁴ than by the many virtues they see practised, for the devil takes care the evil habit is kept up, while the infirmity of human nature destroys our virtues.⁵

3. I do not mean one who cannot bear <term>penances: voluntary acts of self-discipline or devotion performed to show sorrow for sin.</term> and fasting, for, though this be a fault, it does not cause so much harm, but I am speaking of those who wish to be esteemed and respected; who see others' faults but never know their own, and who

# CHAP. XIII. INTERIOR MORTIFICATION.

...if they were not <term>professed: the formal act of taking permanent religious vows to join the religious Order</term> for ten years, because a humble nun would care little for the delay, knowing that if she were good she would not be sent away, and if she were not, why should she wish to stay and damage Christ’s community? 

By not being “good,” I do not mean an attachment to worldly vanities—for, by the mercy of God, I believe that those in this house are far from anything of the sort—but I mean a want of <term>mortification: the practice of self-discipline and "dying" to one's own selfish desires and physical comforts for spiritual growth</term>, self-esteem, or a care for human respect and self, of the kind I have spoken of. Let her who knows that she is not very mortified believe what I say, and not make her profession, unless she wishes to live in a very hell in this life—and God grant she may not find herself in another hell in the next world—for there is much in such dispositions to make one fear such a fate for her, though perhaps neither she herself nor the nuns understand her case as I do. 

Trust my words, otherwise you will learn this truth in the future. The spirit of our Order bids us not only to be nuns, but <term>hermits: individuals who live in seclusion for religious devotion; St. Teresa refers here to the origins of the Carmelites on Mount Carmel</term>, like our <note>The "Holy Fathers" refers to the early desert hermits and the founding figures of the Carmelite tradition.</note> holy Fathers in times past; therefore we must be detached from all created things. This grace of detachment is to be found in any one to whom God has given our vocation, and although at first she may not possess it in perfection, yet we know that she has it by her joy at seeing she is cut... <note>The text likely continues to describe the novice's joy at being "cut off" or "separated" from the world.</note>

<margin>Maria de San José <note>original: "Maria Davila"</note>, one of the first four <term>novices: those who have entered the convent but have not yet taken permanent vows</term>, was only professed on July 2, 1566, nearly four years after her entrance.</margin>

<summary>St. Teresa explains that novices should not hurry to take permanent vows if they lack true humility and self-discipline, warning that living as a nun without "mortification" is like living in hell. She reminds her sisters that their Order is rooted in the tradition of ancient hermits who were completely detached from worldly concerns.</summary>

## CHAPTER XIV.<gloss>This chapter is numbered as Chapter 15 in the edition from Valladolid and Chapter 21 in the Escorial manuscript.</gloss>

->Treats of the great importance of not <term>professing: the formal act of taking permanent religious vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience</term> any one<-
->of a spirit contrary to the qualifications here described.<-

->*1. Qualifications requisite for a **Carmelite nun**: a member of the Order of Our Lady of Mount Carmel, known for a life of prayer and simplicity. 2. On admitting **novices**: individuals who have entered the convent for a period of trial and training but have not yet taken final vows to **profession**. 3. Responsibility of admitting novices.*<-

I. I BELIEVE that God gives great grace to any one who is resolved to do right, therefore you must examine into what is the intention of a person entering the convent. She must not come here to find a home, as often happens nowadays, although if she has good sense our Lord may perfect even this intention. <note>Teresa acknowledges that while some might seek the convent for social or economic security, those with sound judgment can eventually develop a true spiritual calling.</note> If she is wanting in sense, on no account receive her, for she will not know why she enters, nor will her Superiors ever be able to teach her afterwards. As a rule, those who are defective in this way think they know, better than the wisest, what is good for them. I believe this ill is incurable, for it is seldom without some <term>malice: in this context, a stubborn or willful resistance to being corrected or guided by others</term>. Among a number of religious and in a large convent, it may be borne with,

but in our small community it would be intolerable. Good sense, when it once begins to see what is right, clings firmly to it, convinced that this is the safest course, and although this may not lead to great spirituality, still such a person is useful when practical advice is required, and in many other ways, and is not a burden to any one, but rather a help by her cheerfulness. However, I cannot see of what service in a <term>community: the group of nuns living together under a religious rule</term> a person wanting in sense can be, though she may do much harm. <note>original: "²" Ribera says in his life of Saint Teresa: “She was singularly fond of intelligent people. Next to their having a <term>vocation: a divine call to the religious life</term>, what she cared for most in those she received as <term>novices: individuals in a period of trial before taking permanent vows</term>, even if only <term>lay sisters: nuns who focused on manual labor and domestic duties rather than the full choir duties</term>, was a good understanding. People who knew her holiness and love of prayer were careful to praise the fervour and prayerfulness of the candidates they brought her, thinking this would make her accept them. But she only seemed to care to know whether they were sensible and apt. I myself was among their number, and, being greatly surprised, I asked her the reason. She answered: ‘Father, our Lord will give her devotion when she enters, and we will teach her prayer. As for those who have practised prayer outside, we very often have to teach them to forget all they have learnt—but as for intelligence, we cannot give it to them. Besides, a devout, good nun, if she has no brains, is only of use to herself.’ But I can put a sensible nun at the head of the house and trust her with any of the offices” (Ribera, book 4, chapter 24).</note> This defect, and the others I spoke of, <note>original: "³" Referring to Chapter 13, section 3 and Chapter 5, section 24 of this work, as well as the *Constitutions* (the governing rules of the order) sections 13, 17, and 41.</note> are not known at first, for many people speak well, and understand ill, while others' speech is short and unpolished, but they are at home with God. ->### CHAPTER XIV. (CONTINUED) PROFESSION<-

but are suited for prayer.⁴ Therefore, inquiries must be made before receiving a <term>novice: a person in a period of trial and preparation before taking permanent vows</term>, and a long probation should be given to her before her <term>profession: the formal act of entering a religious order and taking permanent vows</term>. If the world once understands that you are free to send away new-comers (as there is often good reason for doing in a <term>convent: a community of nuns</term> where <term>austerities: strict spiritual disciplines and physical self-denial</term> are practiced), no one will feel offended by your doing so.

2. I say this because these times are so disastrous <note>Teresa likely refers to the social and religious upheavals of the 16th century, including the Protestant Reformation</note>, and our human weakness is so great, that the rules of past generations do not suffice to make us disregard all pretenses about social status. That is to say, we must not—simply to spare the feelings of a novice's relatives or for fear of giving some slight offense—allow the established good customs of the convent to be forgotten. God grant that those who receive unsuitable candidates may not suffer for it in the next world; there is always some slight pretext for thinking we may admit them, though in a case of such importance, no excuse is valid.

3. I believe that when the <term>Prioress: the nun in charge of the convent</term> is uninfluenced by personal affection or prejudice and only seeks the welfare of the house, God will not permit her to fall into error.

---

⁴ When Saint Teresa set out for Soria, she chose Catherine of Christ, of the convent of Medina del Campo, as Prioress of the new <term>foundation: a newly established religious house</term>. "But she reads only with difficulty and does not even know how to write," remarked someone. "She is a saint," said the <term>foundress: Saint Teresa herself, as the creator of the Discalced Carmelite reform</term>, "and that is enough to make a good Prioress of her." On June 27, 1581, she had occasion to write to Father <note>Jeronimo Gratian, a high-ranking official in the Carmelite order and Teresa's close collaborator</note> Gratian: "The Prioress fills her office admirably."

When, however, she <note>The Prioress, the woman in charge of the convent</note> is swayed by regard for other people’s feelings or the demands of etiquette, she will be sure to make some mistake. <meta>This page continues Chapter 15, which focuses on the spiritual discipline of remaining silent when one is unfairly criticized. <page-num>91</page-num>
**CHAP. XV.] UNJUST BLAME.**

take the work—you must read it as best you can, since I do my best to write it, and you must not criticise its shortcomings. Such a book <note>Teresa refers to the very text she is writing, "The Way of Perfection."</note> requires leisure: as you know, I have so little, that I have been unable to go on with it for a week, and I forget both what I have already written and how I intended to continue. I can do nothing but blame myself for my failings, and beg you not to imitate me, by excusing yourselves, as I am doing here. Not to <term>exculpate: to clear oneself from a charge of guilt or fault</term> ourselves when unjustly accused is a sublime virtue <margin>Constitutions 30, 47 <note>original: "Const." — a reference to the governing rules of the Discalced Carmelite Order.</note></margin> and very edifying and meritorious; but, although I have often taught it you, and, by the mercy of God, you practise it, yet His Majesty <note>original: "Su Majestad" — a title St. Teresa frequently uses to refer to God or Jesus Christ, emphasizing His divine authority.</note> has never given me the grace to do so myself—may He grant that I may before I die! Yet there always seems to me some good reason for thinking it would be better to make some excuse for myself. This is often lawful, indeed, sometimes it would be wrong to omit it, yet I have not sufficient humility to do so when it is necessary. For indeed it requires great humility to see oneself blamed without cause, and to be silent; we thus imitate our Lord, Who freed us from our sins. Be most careful to act in this way, which does us great good, while I can see no use in our <term>exculpating: making excuses or justifying one's actions</term> ourselves, unless, as I said, when we might cause offence or scandal by not telling the truth. Any one who is more prudent than I am will easily understand

-># THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XV.<-

...this. <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...to see oneself blamed without cause, and to be silent; we thus imitate our Lord, Who [was silent]."</note> I think it is a great gain to accustom oneself to practise this virtue, and to endeavour to obtain from God the sincere humility that must be the result. Whoever is really humble ought to wish sincerely to be despised, <note>original: "Found. ch. xxvii. 19, 20. Life, ch. xix. 12; xxxi. 13-17, 25. Rel. ii. 4. Castle, M. vi. ch. i. 7-11; ch. iii. 6." No bodily strength is needed here, nor the help of any one, save of God.

2. I wish these great virtues, my sisters, to be both our study and our <term>penance: an act of self-mortification or devotion performed to show sorrow for sins</term>. You know that I restrain you from other severe and excessive <term>austerities: harsh physical practices like extreme fasting or self-flagellation</term>, which if performed imprudently might injure your health. Here, however, there is nothing to fear; for however great the interior virtues may be, they do not weaken the body so that it cannot keep the <term>Rule: the formal set of regulations (The Constitutions) that govern the daily life and conduct of the Carmelite sisters</term> of the religious life. These strengthen the soul, and, as I have often told you, by constantly conquering yourselves in little things, you may train yourselves to gain the victory in great matters. <summary>Teresa advocates for "interior penance," specifically the humility of accepting unjust blame, as a safer and more effective spiritual discipline than harsh physical austerities. She notes that these internal virtues strengthen the soul without damaging the health required to follow the religious Rule.</summary>

-># CHAPTER XV. UNJUST BLAME.<-

...and how badly I practice it!—indeed, I have never been tried thus in any important affair, for I never heard any ill spoken of me that did not fall far short of the truth, if not in that particular matter, yet often enough in similar things: only too often in other ways have I offended our Lord God, and I thought people showed me great kindness in not speaking of these. <note>original: *The Interior Castle*, Mansion VI, chapter i, 12. *The Way of Perfection*, chapter xvii, 4.</note>

I always prefer that they should find fault with what I have not done, for the truth is very painful to hear; but for a false accusation, however grave, I care nothing, and in minor matters I follow my natural bent, without thinking of what is most perfect. For this reason, I wish you to understand from the first, and I desire each one of you to consider how much is gained by this habit of not excusing yourselves. <note>Teresa encourages her nuns to avoid the "natural bent" of defending their ego, suggesting that silence under false blame is a shortcut to spiritual growth.</note> I think, it can never do—

---

<note>St. Teresa was wonderfully patient under <term>persecution: persistent ill-treatment or hostility, especially because of religious beliefs</term>. Sometimes I used to speak to her of the <term>calumnies: false and malicious statements meant to damage someone's reputation</term> uttered against her. She would answer with perfect simplicity and sweetness: "Whoever has said any harm against me has done me a great favor." One day she said to me: "I assure you, Father, that whenever I hear that people have spoken ill of me, I always pray to God for them. I beg Him to preserve their heart, their lips, and hands from all offence; I do not look upon them as ill-intentioned, but I see in them the ministers of Jesus Christ, instruments used by the <term>Holy Ghost: the Holy Spirit, the third person of the Trinity in Christian theology</term> to do me good and to further my salvation. Believe me, Father," she added, "the best and most efficient means of winning heaven is patience during trials; this makes man master and ruler of himself, as our Lord told His Apostles." I remember that sometimes, when I spoke to her about scandals that had been spread about...</note>

<summary>In this passage, St. Teresa of Avila admits her own difficulty in practicing silence under blame but explains that she prefers false accusations over true ones because the truth is more painful. An accompanying biographical note highlights her extraordinary habit of praying for her detractors, viewing them as instruments of God’s will for her own spiritual benefit.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XV.

->**OBLOQUY**<-
<note>The term <term>obloquy: strong public criticism or verbal abuse</term> serves as the title for this section, focusing on how to handle being spoken of ill by others.</note>

I deserved the graces Thou hast already bestowed on me. Can it be that I should wish men to think well of a thing so vile as I am, when they said such evil things of Thee, Who art above every other good? Do not permit this : forbid it, O my God, nor let me wish that anything displeasing to Thine eyes should be found in me, Thy **handmaiden**. See, O my Lord, I am blind and I care but little for the light. Enlighten me and make me sincerely desire that all men should hate me, since I have so often abandoned Thee, Who lovest me so faithfully. Why do we act thus, O my God? What joy do we think to find by pleasing creatures? What does it matter of what guilt they accuse us, if we are guiltless before Thee, O Lord ?

4. Ah, my sisters, how far we are from grasping this truth! We shall never reach the summit of **perfection** unless we come to understand what is the great reality, and what is of no account.<note>This reference to the Escorial edition—a primary manuscript of Teresa's work held at the Royal Site of San Lorenzo de El Escorial—notes that this section appears in Chapter 23 of that version, though the subject matter remains the same.</note> Were there no other gain but the shame felt by your accuser at seeing that you permit yourselves to be unjustly condemned, it would be very great, for to witness such an action sometimes benefits a soul more than listening to ten sermons. # CHAPTER XVI.<gloss>This chapter is based on the edition by Fuente. It is missing from the Valladolid manuscript until the third paragraph, and in the Escorial manuscript, it is labeled as a continuation of chapter twenty-three.</gloss>

## The game of chess.

*1. The game of chess. 2. Meditation and contemplation. 3. Difficulties of rising to contemplation. 4. The virtues usually required for it. 5. Sufferings borne for us by Christ. 6. Why God sometimes raises imperfect souls to contemplation. 7. Abandonment to God's care. 8. That all should aim at sanctity.*

1. Do not imagine that a great part of my work is done. No, I have only been “placing the board” for the game. You asked me to teach you the foundation of prayer, my daughters, although God did not establish me on this foundation, for I am almost destitute of these virtues; yet I know no other. But, be sure that any one who does not understand how to set the pieces in the game of chess, will never be able to play well, nor, if he does not know how to give check, will he ever succeed in effecting checkmate. You may blame me for speaking of a game, for such things are neither played nor permitted in our convent.<gloss>This refers to Rule 27 of the Constitutions of the order, which prohibited secular games.</gloss> This will show you what a mother God has given you, skilled even in such <note>St. Teresa uses this worldly analogy to show that even "holy" pursuits require strategy and preparation.</note>

<note>2 Constitutions, rule 27.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa finishes her thoughts on spiritual detachment and begins a new chapter using the game of chess as a metaphor for the discipline and preparation needed for prayer. She acknowledges the irony of using a game analogy while living in a convent where games are forbidden.</summary>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION [CHAP. XVI.]<-

->### CONTEMPLATION ###<-

...have instructed you, and I advise every one to practise it, <note>See Teresa's other works: *Life*, ch. iv, 11 and ch. xi, 20; *Foundations*, ch. v, 2, 3; *The Interior Castle*, Mansion II, ch. i, 19, 20 and Mansion VI, ch. vii, 12.</note> even though they do not possess the virtues, for this is the first step to obtain them all: it is most essential for all Christians to begin this practice. No one, however desperate their case may be, ought to neglect it, if God incites them to make use of it. I have written this elsewhere, as have other people who understand the subject, which, as God knows, I certainly do not. 

**Contemplation**, however, is quite another thing, daughters. We fall into a mistake on this point, so that if any one thinks about his sins every day for a certain time (as he is bound to do, if he is a Christian in anything but name), we at once call him a "great contemplative," and expect him to possess the sublime virtues proper to such a state: he even thinks so himself; but he is quite wrong. He has not yet learnt how to “place the board,” but thinks he can effect checkmate simply by knowing the names of the pieces—in this he is deceived; this **King** will not let Himself be taken, except by one who is entirely given up to Him. <note>This commentary appears in the Valladolid edition (ch. xviii) and the Escorial manuscript (ch. xxv). It explores the difference between contemplatives and those who content themselves with <term>mental prayer: a form of prayer where one focuses the mind on God or divine mysteries through meditation rather than reciting set formulas</term>. It explains that God may occasionally raise a worldly soul to perfect contemplation and provides the reasoning. Note: Due to a calculation error, this chapter is numbered xviii instead of xvii in the Valladolid manuscript.</note>

<summary>Saint Teresa distinguishes between basic mental prayer—which all Christians should practice to gain virtue—and the advanced state of contemplation. She uses the chess metaphor to explain that one cannot reach spiritual "checkmate" (union with God) without total self-surrender.</summary>

<keywords>St. Teresa of Avila, mental prayer, contemplation, humility, chess metaphor, Valladolid manuscript, Escorial manuscript, spiritual virtues</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XVI of "The Way of Perfection." Having previously used the metaphor of a chess game to describe the spiritual life, St. Teresa now transitions into a practical discussion of the requirements for reaching the state of contemplation, emphasizing that it cannot be bypassed without first mastering mental prayer and the virtues.</meta>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XVI.]<-

3. Therefore, daughters, if you wish me to tell you the way to attain to <term>contemplation</term> <note>In Teresa’s writings, contemplation is a "supernatural" state where God acts upon the soul directly; it is a gift that cannot be achieved by human effort alone, unlike meditation.</note> you must allow me to speak at length on certain matters, although to you they may seem unimportant. I think that they are important, and unless you intend learning and practising them, continue your <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Mental prayer, or *oración mental*, is a form of active meditation where the individual focuses their mind and heart on God, often by reflecting on a scene from the Gospels or a divine attribute.</note> all your life; for I assure you, and all others who aspire to this state, that you will never otherwise attain to genuine contemplation. I may be deceived about this, but I judge from my own experience; and I have been striving to become a contemplative for the last twenty years.

4. I will now describe <term>mental prayer</term>, as perhaps some of you do not understand what it is. God grant that we practise it as we ought, but I am afraid this can only be done by a great effort, unless we possess the <term>virtues</term>, <note>Teresa identifies three primary virtues as the foundation for prayer: love for one another, detachment from created things, and true humility.</note> although they are not here necessary in so high a degree as for contemplation. The **King of glory** <note>original: "Rey de la gloria," a traditional title for Christ, emphasizing His majesty as the guest of the soul.</note> will not come into our souls, so as to be united to them, unless we strive to obtain the highest virtues. I will explain this, for if once you found me out in an untruth you would believe me no longer—as would be only right if I did so purposely, but God preserve me from any falsehood; my error would come either from ignorance or want of understanding. I wish you to know that He sometimes shows great favour to people whose souls are in an evil state, that by this means He may snatch them out of the devil’s grasp. I do not mean persons who are in

<summary>St. Teresa explains that contemplation requires a foundation of mental prayer and the cultivation of virtues, warning that divine union with the "King of Glory" is reserved for those who strive for spiritual excellence. She also notes that while God may grant favors to souls in a state of sin to rescue them, this is an exception rather than the standard path to contemplation.</summary>

[CHAP. XVI.] QUALIFICATIONS. 101

<margin>10 *Life*, ch. xxii. 22, 23. *The Interior Castle*, Mansion iv. ch. i. 3. *Conceptions of the Love of God*, ch. v. 3. *Way of Perfection* ch. xli. 2.</margin>
<insert>...committed</insert> <term>mortal sin</term> <note>A "mortal" or deadly sin is a grave offense—such as murder or profound betrayal—committed with full knowledge, which in Catholic theology is believed to sever the soul's relationship with God's sanctifying grace.</note>, but those who are very faulty: our Lord may permit them to behold some very high vision in order to turn their hearts to Him.10 I cannot, however, believe that He would actually raise them to <term>contemplation</term> <note>As established previously, Teresa defines contemplation as a supernatural gift of God's presence, distinct from the human effort of meditation.</note>, for this is a **divine union**, in which our Lord takes His delight in the soul while the soul rejoices in Him, and there is no way in which heavenly purity can take pleasure in what is impure, nor can He Who is "the Joy of the Angels" find His happiness in one who is not His own. We know that those in mortal sin are the slaves of Satan, and must find their joy in him, since they have gratified him; the pleasures he gives are, as we know, nothing but ceaseless torment, even in this life. Still, without taking those who are not His, yet His Majesty will do what He has often done—snatch them out of the hand of the enemy and make them His own.

5. O my God, how often do we force Thee to struggle with the devil on our account! Was it not enough that Thou didst suffer him to bear Thee in his arms to the pinnacle of the Temple,11 to teach us how to vanquish him? What a sight, daughters, to see this **Sun** <note>Teresa frequently uses "the Sun" as a metaphor for Christ, the source of all spiritual light and warmth.</note> encompassed with darkness, and what terror that wretch <note>Referring to the devil.</note> must have felt, without knowing why, for God did not allow him to understand.

<summary>St. Teresa explains that while God may grant visions to sinners to convert them, the intimate state of contemplation is reserved for souls in a state of grace. She reflects on the humility of Christ in allowing Himself to be tempted by the devil to provide an example for humanity.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION [CHAP. XVI]

...must have felt, although he did not know the reason why, for God did not permit him to understand it! Blessed be such immense pity and mercy. How ashamed we Christians ought to feel, as I said above, at constraining our Lord to combat daily this <term>unclean beast</term> <note>A common metaphor in 16th-century spiritual writing referring to the lower, worldly, or sinful nature of humanity that resists the divine.</note>! There was need for Thine arms to be so strong, O Lord, but how was it that they were not weakened by the many tortures Thou didst endure upon the cross? Oh, how soon all hurts that are borne with love heal again! I believe that if Thy life had lasted longer, the love Thou hast for us would have healed Thy wounds, without need of any other medicine. O my God, who will apply such a <term>lotion</term> <note>Teresa frequently uses medical imagery—ointments, lotions, and medicines—to describe how divine love repairs the spiritual damage caused by life's trials.</note> to all the trials which give me pain and trouble? How eagerly I should desire them, if they were to be cured by such a health-giving ointment! It seems extravagant for me to speak thus, as my actions are in such contrast to it, yet divine love does greater things than this. But lest I should seem fond of what is sensational (as indeed I am), and that I may not set you a bad example, I will not tell you about them.12

6. But, to return to what I was saying—God knows that He can attract certain souls to Himself by means of divine favours: He sees they are on the way to be lost, but He does not wish it to happen through any...

<summary>Teresa marvels at God's mercy and the healing power of divine love, which she compares to a medicinal ointment capable of curing all suffering. She suggests that God may grant special favors to souls on the brink of being lost as a way to draw them back to Him.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION [CHAP. XVI]

## IMPERFECT SOULS

->103<-

...fault of His, therefore, though they are in a bad case and are lacking in goodness, He gives them consolations, delights, and tenderness of devotion, which begin to excite their desires; He even sometimes raises them to <term>contemplation</term> <note>In Teresian spirituality, contemplation is a "supernatural" gift where God suspends the natural activity of the mind to communicate Himself directly to the soul.</note>, although but rarely, and for a very short time. This is to prove whether such a grace will induce them to prepare themselves to enjoy His favours more often.<gloss>13</gloss> <note>original: "Castle, M. iv. ch. iii. 9, 10..." refers to Teresa's work *The Interior Castle*, Fourth Mansions, Chapter 3, paragraphs 9-10, as well as several sections in the Fifth Mansions.</note> But if they do not respond to Thee, and are unwilling to pardon their enemies, then pardon us, O Lord, for it is a terrible misfortune for a soul which Thou hast thus drawn to Thee to become attached afterwards to any earthly thing. 

I feel sure there are many souls which our Lord God puts to this proof, but few who correspond rightly to the favour, for when He acts thus, unless we offer any hindrance, I am convinced that He never ceases bestowing His graces until He has brought us to a very high state of prayer. If we do not yield ourselves to Him as entirely as He gives Himself to us,<gloss>14</gloss> <note>original: "Castle, M. v. ch. i. 3. Life, ch. xi. 2-4..." refers to *The Interior Castle*, Fifth Mansions, and Teresa's *Autobiography* (The Life), where she discusses the necessity of total self-surrender to God.</note> He does what suffices, by leaving us to <term>mental prayer</term> <note>A form of prayer where the heart and mind speak to God without a set formula, often described by Teresa as "an intimate sharing between friends."</note>, and visiting us now and then, as servants working in His vineyard. But the other souls are His beloved children, whom He will not allow to quit His side, nor will He leave them, since they do not wish to forsake Him. He seats them at His table—

7. O happy <term>custody</term> <note>In a religious context, "custody" refers to the protective care of the soul or the enclosure of the convent where one is safe from worldly distractions.</note>, my daughters! O joyful renunciation of things so trifling and so base, which leads us to such a blessed state! Then why, my sisters, should we not show Him all the love we can? See, what a rich exchange—to give our love for His; to give it to Him, Who can give us all things, while we can do nothing without His aid. But what is it that we do for Thee, O Lord, our Creator? We do nothing, but make a <term>paltry resolution</term> <note>Teresa often humbles herself by describing human efforts as "paltry" or "mere nothing" compared to the vastness of divine grace.</note>. If, then, <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Teresa's favorite title for God or Jesus Christ, emphasizing His sovereignty as a Great King.</note> wills that we should purchase all things by this mere nothing, let us not be so mad as to refuse it.

### CHAP. XVI.] ALL TO AIM AT IT. 105

->105<-

8. O Lord, all our ills come from not fixing our eyes on Thee : if we looked at nothing else but where we are going, we should soon arrive, but we fall a thousand times and stumble and go astray, because we do not keep our gaze bent on Him Who is the “Way.” <note>St. Teresa refers here to the Gospel of John 14:6, where Jesus identifies himself as "the Way." She suggests that spiritual failure is simply a loss of focus on Christ's example.</note> It looks so new to us that one would think nobody had walked in it before. Indeed, this is a grievous pity : one would think we were not Christians at all, nor had ever read the **Passion** <note>The "Passion" refers to the final period of Jesus's life, including his trials, suffering, and death on the cross.</note> in our lives. Lord have mercy on us—we are touched in a **point of honour**! <note>In 16th-century Spain, "honour" (*honra*) was a strict social code regarding reputation and status. Teresa frequently argues that concern for one's social standing is the greatest obstacle to spiritual growth.</note> If people tell us not to notice it, we at once call them unchristian —I laugh, or I grieve sometimes, at what I hear of such foibles in the world, as I do at my own foibles in religion. To be undervalued in the least is unbearable to us : we at once cry out : “ We are not angels, nor saints ! ” That is true enough. God deliver us, my daughters, when we fall into any imperfection, from saying : “ We are not angels ! We are not saints ! ” Although we are not, still, it is the greatest help to believe that, with the aid of God, we can be, if we strive our hardest. There is no fear of His failing to do His part, if we do ours. Since we came here for nothing else, let us put our hands to the plough,<gloss>21</gloss> <note>original: "Nemo mittens manum suam ad aratrum, et respiciens retro, aptus est regno Dei." St. Luke 9:62. This translates as: "No one who puts his hand to the plow and looks back is fit for the kingdom of God."</note> as they say. Let there be nothing which we know would further our Lord’s service that we dare not undertake, with the assistance of His **grace**. <note>In this context, "grace" refers to the divine assistance given by God to help individuals lead a more virtuous life.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa exhorts her sisters to stop making excuses for their imperfections and to focus entirely on following Christ, warning that an obsession with social "honour" prevents spiritual progress.</summary>
<keywords>following Christ, the Way, the Passion, social honour, spiritual perfection, divine grace, perseverance, St. Luke</keywords>

I wish such audacity <note>Teresa uses the word "audacity" to mean a "holy daring"—a courageous spirit that refuses to be intimidated by one's own perceived unworthiness when approaching God.</note> to exist in this house—it always increases humility. Ever nourish this holy daring, for God aids the valiant, and is no respecter of persons <note>original: "Non enim respicis personam hominum." It may appear presumptuous on my part, but you will excuse everything. Perhaps you may understand my rough style better than other more polished writings. May our Lord grant me His assistance. Amen!

## CHAPTER XVII. <note>This chapter corresponds to chapter 19 in the Valladolid edition and chapter 27 in the Escorial manuscript.</note>

->That some souls are not suited to contemplation, and others are long in attaining it. Every one who is truly humble should be content to go by the way our Lord conducts her.<-

*1. Humility is necessary for prayerful souls. 2. All who practise prayer cannot become contemplatives. 3. Vocal prayer less dangerous than contemplation. 4. In the active way we serve like Martha <note>In the Bible, Martha is the sister of Mary and Lazarus. She represents the "active life" of service and work, while Mary represents the "contemplative life."</note>. 5. Both the active and contemplative life serve our Lord. 6. The cross the surest way.*

1. I SEEM now to have come to the subject of prayer, but there still remains something important for me to say: it concerns humility, which is most requisite in this convent, because it is the principal aid to prayer. As I said <note>referring to Chapter 16, section 1</note>, it is very necessary for you to know how to

<meta>This page continues Chapter 17, where St. Teresa of Avila discusses the diversity of spiritual paths. Having established that some souls are led to contemplation while others are not, she now focuses on why humility is the essential foundation for both paths and why those not called to contemplation should not feel discouraged.</meta>

### [CHAP. XVII.] HUMILITY.

practice humility on every occasion: this is one of the chief points, and most essential for persons given to prayer. How can any one who is truly humble think herself as good as others who are <term>contemplatives</term>? <note>In this context, "contemplatives" refers to those who have been granted the specific gift of "infused contemplation," a form of wordless, supernatural prayer given by God.</note> God may, by His goodness and mercy, and by the merits of Christ, make her deserve to receive such graces as they do, but, if she takes my advice, she will always rank herself in the lowest place, as our Lord taught us, both by His word and example.³ Let her respond to His call, if God leads her by the way of contemplation ⁴; otherwise, if she thinks herself happy in being allowed to wait upon the servants of God, humility will serve in its stead.⁵ Let her praise <term>His Majesty</term> <note>St. Teresa consistently uses the title "His Majesty" to refer to God, reflecting the courtly language of 16th-century Spain and her reverence for the Divine King.</note> for having placed her in their company, although she deserves to be the slave of the devils in hell. I have good cause for saying this, for, as I told you, it is important to understand that His Majesty does not lead all souls in the same way, and perhaps she who thinks herself the least of all may be the highest in His eyes.

2. Thus it does not follow, because all the nuns in this convent practise prayer, that they must all be contemplatives. Such an idea would greatly discourage those who do not understand the truth, that contemplation is a gift of God, which is not necessary for salvation

---

⁴ *Interior Castle*, Mansion iv. ch. ii. 8. ⁵ *Interior Castle*, Mansion iii. ch. ii. 16.

<summary>St. Teresa explains that humility is the foundation of prayer and that not all souls are called to contemplation, though all can reach holiness. She encourages those who serve God through active humility rather than contemplative states to remain content and grateful.</summary>

...neither is it necessary for earning our eternal reward, nor does any one here require us to possess it. She who is without it, yet who follows the counsels I have given, will attain great **perfection**. It may be that she will gain far more <term>merit</term> <note>In Catholic theology, "merit" refers to a reward promised by God for good works performed through His grace.</note>, as she has to work harder ⁶ on her own account; our Lord is treating her like a <term>valiant woman</term> <note>A reference to the "Strong Woman" of Proverbs 31, symbolizing a soul that serves God with fortitude rather than seeking emotional comforts.</note> and keeping until hereafter all the happiness she has missed in this life. Let her not be disheartened nor give up prayer, or the other practices of which her sisters make use—sometimes our Lord comes very late, and pays as much all at once as He has given to others during many years.⁷ For more than fourteen years I could not <term>meditate</term> <note>Here, "meditate" specifically refers to "discursive prayer," or using the intellect to think through a religious scene or concept.</note> without a book.⁸ There are many people of this kind, and others cannot meditate even with the help of reading, but are obliged to recite <term>vocal prayers</term> <note>Prayers with a set form of words, such as the "Our Father," as opposed to internal, spontaneous conversation with God.</note>, which to a certain extent arrest their attention. Some have so volatile an **imagination** <note>Teresa famously called the imagination "the madwoman of the house" because of its tendency to distract the soul with wandering thoughts.</note> that they cannot fix their thoughts, which are always wandering, upon one thing; if they try to think of God, they are troubled with a thousand foolish fancies, <term>scruples</term> <note>In this context, unfounded fears that one has committed a sin or performed a religious duty incorrectly.</note>, and doubts.

3. I know a very old nun of most exemplary life (would to God my life were like hers!), very holy, very austere, and a perfect religious, who has spent many hours, and even a number of years, in vocal prayers, but cannot make use of <term>mental prayer</term> <note>A form of wordless prayer or internal dialogue with God where the focus is on love and presence rather than reciting specific texts.</note>: the utmost...

->### [CHAP. XVII.] VOCAL PRAYERS. 109<-

...she can do is to pause a little, from time to time, during her **Hail Marys** <note>original: "Ave Marias"</note> and **Our Fathers** <note>original: "Paternosters"</note>, which is a very holy custom. Many people resemble her: if they are humble, let them not think they are more imperfect, or believe they will be any the worse for it in the end, for they will be quite as well off as those who enjoy many consolations. In one way such souls are safer, for we cannot tell whether <term>spiritual delights</term> <note>Internal feelings of intense joy or sweetness experienced during prayer; while often from God, they are not the goal of prayer itself.</note> come from God or from the devil: if they are not divine, they are very dangerous, for the devil tries to excite pride by their means; however, if they are sent by God there is nothing to fear, for they bring humility with them,⁹ as I fully explained in another book.

4. Other souls, receiving no spiritual consolations, are humble, for they doubt whether it is not through their own fault, and are most anxious to improve. When they see any one else weeping, unless they do the same, they think they must be much more backward than her in God’s service, although perhaps they are more advanced, for tears,¹⁰ though a good sign, do not always indicate perfection. Humility, <term>mortification</term> <note>The practice of self-denial and discipline to strengthen the soul's focus on God.</note>, <term>detachment</term> <note>The spiritual freedom of not being overly tied to worldly things or even one's own preferences.</note>, and other virtues are the safest: there is no cause for fear, nor need you doubt that you may become as perfect as the greatest <term>contemplatives</term> <note>Individuals who experience a direct, quiet awareness of God's presence, often beyond words or thoughts.</note>. Saint Martha <note>The sister of Mary and Lazarus, often used as a symbol of the active life of service in contrast to the contemplative life.</note>...

->## THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XVII.]<-

...was holy,$^{11}$ <note>St. Martha, sister of Mary and Lazarus, known for her hospitality and practical service.</note> though we are never told she was a **contemplative**; would you not be content with resembling this blessed woman, who deserved to receive Christ our Lord so often into her home, where she feasted and served Him, and where He ate at her table, and even, perhaps, off her own plate? If she had always been enraptured, like the **Magdalen**, <note>Mary Magdalen, often identified in this period with Mary of Bethany, representing the contemplative life focused on listening to Jesus.</note> there would have been no one to offer food to this divine Guest. Imagine, then, that this community is the house of St. Martha, where there must be different kinds of people. Let not the nun who is called to the <term>active life</term> <note>A way of life focused on practical works of charity and service, as opposed to the "contemplative life" focused primarily on prayer.</note> murmur at others who are absorbed in contemplation, for she knows our Lord will defend them$^{12}$; as a rule, they themselves are silent, for the **"better part"**$^{13}$ <note>Referring to Christ's words in Luke 10:42: "Mary has chosen the best part, which shall not be taken away from her." original: "Maria optimam partem elegit, quæ non auferetur ab ea."</note> makes them oblivious of themselves and of all else. Remember that some one must cook the food, and think yourself favoured in being allowed to serve with Martha. Reflect that true humility consists in being willing and ready to do what our Lord asks of us: it always makes us consider ourselves unworthy to be reckoned among His servants.

5. If contemplation, <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Internal, wordless prayer where the soul speaks to God from the heart.</note> and <term>vocal prayer</term> <note>Prayer spoken aloud or recited, such as the Our Father.</note>, nursing the sick, the work of the house, and the most menial labour, all serve this Guest Who comes to eat and drink...

<summary>Saint Teresa uses the biblical story of Martha and Mary to explain that both active service and quiet contemplation are necessary and valuable in a religious community. ...and talk with us, why should we choose to serve Him in one way rather than in another? ^14 I do not mean that we have a choice in the specific work we do—you should practice every kind of service—but the final decision does not belong to you; it belongs to our Lord. If, after many years of testing, He makes it clear what role each person is meant to fill, it would be a strange kind of <term>humility</term> <note>For Teresa, humility is "walking in truth"—accepting one's actual place in God's plan without pride or false modesty.</note> for you to try to choose for yourself. Leave that choice to the Master of the house: He is wise and powerful, and He knows what is best for you and for Himself. ^15

6. As I have said elsewhere, He wants to treat us like **valiant women**,^16 giving us the same **cross** that His Majesty always carried Himself. What truer friendship can He show than to choose for us exactly what He chose for Himself? Furthermore, it is possible we would not have earned such a great reward through contemplation alone. His own—we have no right to interfere with them. It is well the decision does not rest with us, for, thinking it a more peaceful way, we should all immediately become <term>contemplatives</term>! <note>A contemplative is someone dedicated to a life of deep, internal prayer and focus on God's presence, rather than outward works of service.</note> What a gain is ours, if, for fear of losing by it, we do not seek to gain by what we think is best; since God never permits the truly <term>mortified</term> soul <note>In this context, a "mortified" soul is one that has practiced self-discipline and "died" to its own selfish desires in order to be more open to God.</note> to lose anything, except for its greater gain!

## CHAPTER XVIII. <margin>Valladolid edition, chapter 20; Escorial manuscript, continuation of chapter 28. <note>These refer to two of the primary historical copies of St. Teresa's writings.</note></margin>

->Continuation of the same subject. Shows that contemplatives have to bear far heavier <term>crosses</term> <note>A "cross" is a traditional Christian metaphor for the specific burdens, hardships, or spiritual trials a person must endure.</note> than people who lead the active life. This chapter offers great consolation to the latter.<-

*1. Trials of the contemplative life. 2. Its reward. 3. Preparation for contemplation. 4. Contemplatives bear the standard of humility. 5. The virtues preferable to supernatural favours. 6. Obedience. 7. Danger of supernatural favours.*

1. **DAUGHTERS**, I assure those of you whom God does not lead by the way of contemplation, that, both by observation and experience, I know that those following it do not bear a lighter cross than you: but indeed you would be aghast at the different kinds of trials God sends them. I know a great deal of both <term>vocations</term> <note>A "vocation" is a specific calling from God to a particular way of life or service.</note>, and am well aware that the sufferings God inflicts on contemplatives are of so unbearable a kind that, unless [the text continues...]

<meta>This page continues the opening of Chapter 18. In the previous section, St. Teresa established that while many people desire the "peace" of the contemplative life, it is God who chooses each person’s path. She now begins to explain that this path of deep prayer is not an easy escape, but a calling that requires the greatest endurance and is marked by significant suffering.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>
->### CHAP. XVIII.] TRIALS OF CONTEMPLATIVES.<-

He sustained such souls by the <term>manna</term> <note>A biblical reference to the "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites in the desert; here it refers to spiritual nourishment provided by God.</note> of divine consolations, they would find their pains insupportable. God guides those He loves by the way of afflictions; the dearer they are to Him, the more severe are their trials. It is incredible that He should hate contemplatives, whom He Himself praises and calls His friends, and absurd to imagine that He would admit self-indulgent and easy-going people into His friendship; I feel certain that God gives by far the heaviest crosses to His favourites. The road He chooses for them is so uneven and rugged as to make them fancy that they have lost their way and that they must turn back again and start afresh. Then His Majesty <note>St. Teresa’s respectful and preferred title for God.</note> is obliged to give them some refreshment <gloss>2</gloss>—water would not be enough, it must be *wine* <gloss>3</gloss>: inebriated <gloss>4</gloss> with this draught from God they become unconscious of their pain and enabled to sustain it. Thus one rarely finds true contemplatives who are not valiant and resolved to suffer. If they are weak, the first thing our Lord does, is to infuse courage into them, so that they may fear no trials. <gloss>5</gloss> I believe that those who

2. Pay attention to what I am saying, for we all have to serve God, and that not for one or two years, or for ten years either, that we should desert our duties like cowards. It is well to show our Lord that we are not defaulters; we must be like soldiers who receive their pay whether they fight or no, and so must always stand at their post, ready to set to work whenever their captain gives his orders. How much better pay shall we receive from our King than they get from theirs, for sometimes the poor fellows die in battle and God knows what wages they get afterwards! The captain reviews his regiment as it stands on service, and knows the capabilities of each soldier, although not so well as our heavenly Captain knows ours. He assigns to them the duties for which they are fit, but if the men were absent they would receive neither pay nor orders.

<page-num>115</page-num>
### CHAP. XVIII.] PREPARATION.

If you cannot manage that <note>Referring to the higher states of mental prayer discussed in previous chapters.</note>, then use vocal prayer, reading, and the <term>colloquies</term> <note>Informal, intimate conversations with God, rather than reciting fixed formulas.</note> with God which I will teach you later on. Never give up your hours of prayer: you do not know when the Bridegroom <note>In mystical tradition, the "Bridegroom" refers to Jesus Christ, following the imagery of the Song of Solomon.</note> will summon you, and you might share the fate of the foolish virgins.⁷ Our Lord may give you some heavier cross, under the guise of divine consolations. If He does not, be convinced that you are not meant for <term>contemplation</term> <note>A state of prayer where the soul is granted a direct, experiential awareness of God's presence without the use of logic or imagination.</note> but for the <term>active life</term> <note>A path of serving God through outward works of charity and duty, rather than interior silence.</note>. This will give you an opportunity of gaining merit through humility. Let such a one believe that she is unworthy even of the place she holds. Let her cheerfully do what she is told, and as I said,⁸ if only her humility is genuine, blessed is such a servant in the active life, for she will complain of none but herself, and I would far rather resemble her than some contemplatives with whom I am acquainted.

4. Let her leave others to wage their own wars, which are no easy ones. Although <term>standard-bearers</term> <note>In 16th-century military tactics, the standard-bearer held the company’s flag (the "colours"). They were often the primary target for the enemy and were expected to hold the flag high even if they were being struck down.</note>, like officers, do not actually fight, yet they expose themselves to great danger and must suffer, in a way, more than the rest of the men, because they carry the colours, which they must not relinquish, even though they themselves are being cut to pieces. In the same way, contemplatives must uphold the standard of humility, and must bear—

...all the blows aimed at them, without making any reprisal <note>Retaliation or striking back.</note>, for their duty is to suffer as Christ did, bearing aloft the cross, and never letting it fall, whatever the danger may be, unless they would prove cowards in suffering. It is for this they are advanced to their high and honourable office. Do you suppose the position given them by the King <note>Teresa frequently refers to God or Jesus as "the King," drawing on the courtly language of 16th-century Spain.</note> is an easy one? For the sake of some slight distinction they undertake to incur far more danger than the rest, and if they turn cowards, the battle will go against their side. 

Let <term>contemplatives</term> <note>Those who practice a form of silent, experiential prayer focused on God’s presence.</note>, then, look to their conduct, for if the <term>standard-bearer</term> <note>In historical warfare, the standard-bearer carried the flag. They were high-priority targets and could not use their hands to fight because they had to keep the banner visible to guide the troops.</note> quit his colours the day will be lost: in the same way, I believe that souls less advanced in religion are much injured by seeing those they hold as captains and friends of God act in a way ill suiting their position. The common soldiers march as they can: if sometimes they withdraw from the thick of the fray, no one notices them, and they lose neither their honour nor their lives, but all eyes are on the <term>standard-bearer</term>, who cannot move without being seen. His duty is a noble one, and a great honour, and the King shows him special favour by His choice; but he has undertaken a heavy responsibility.

5. My daughters, as we do not understand our own needs nor what to ask for, let us leave all to our Lord,⁹

# CHAP. XVIII.] VIRTUES.

Who knows us better than we know ourselves? <note>Referring to God's omniscience regarding the human soul.</note> A lowly heart is content with what is given it, yet there are people who ask for favours from God as a matter of simple justice.<gloss>10</gloss> What humility! Therefore He Who knows all things, rightly abstains from granting these gifts to such persons, seeing them unfit to drink of His chalice.<gloss>11</gloss> <note>The "chalice" or "cup" refers to the sharing in the suffering of Christ, as mentioned in the Gospels.</note> The best sign that any one has made progress is that she thinks herself the last of all and proves it by her behaviour, and that she aims at the well-being and good of others, in all that she does. This is the true test<gloss>12</gloss>—not sweetness in prayer, ecstasies, visions, and other divine favours of the same kind. The value of these latter we cannot estimate rightly until the next life, but the former are current coin, a constant revenue and a perpetual inheritance, not mere part-payments which, when acquitted, cease—I speak of great humility and mortification, and implicit obedience<gloss>13</gloss> which will not

> "...would be its gain, might be its ruin. If I ask Thee to free me from a cross by which Thou seekest to mortify me <note>To "mortify" means to practice self-denial or to subdue one's physical desires for spiritual growth.</note>, what do I ask of Thee, O my God? If I entreat Thee to send me such a trial perhaps it may be beyond my patience, which is too weak to bear the heavy burden: or if I were to endure it, but were wanting in humility, I might fancy that I had performed some great deed, while Thou, O my God, didst do it all." <note>This excerpt is from Teresa’s work, *Exclamations of the Soul to God*, chapter 16, sections 1-3.</note>

disobey one tittle <note>The word "tittle" refers to the smallest possible detail or a tiny stroke in writing; here it means not disobeying even the smallest part of an order.</note> of the orders given by the **Prioress**, but submits to them as to the commands of God, of Whom she is the representative.<gloss>14</gloss>

6. **Obedience** is of the greatest importance: she who is lacking in it is not a nun at all: I will say no more about this, as I am speaking to nuns (who are good religious, I believe, or at least desire to be so). But as obedience is most essential I mention it lest you might forget this. I cannot understand what any one is doing in a <term>monastery</term> <note>A monastery here refers to a cloistered community of nuns living under religious vows.</note> if, after she has made a vow of obedience, she does not fulfil it as perfectly as possible.<gloss>15</gloss> I can assure her, that while she fails in this, she will never reach <term>contemplation</term> <note>Contemplation is a high form of quiet, wordless prayer where the soul experiences a direct sense of God's presence.</note>, nor even lead the **active life** well:

---

<note>14 When St. Teresa was journeying to make a foundation <note>A "foundation" is the formal establishment of a new convent.</note> she always put her companions under obedience to some religious who was with them, or, if there were none, to the priest who accompanied them, and though, on account of her office and dignity she had so much claim to exact obedience, yet she was the first to obey. So dearly did she love this virtue that, when she conferred on one of her nuns the post of Prioress to a new foundation, she, who had herself held that position for so many years, immediately rendered obedience and subjected herself, not as foundress, but as one of the last members of the house, and asked permission for everything she did. She behaved in the same manner when staying at convents of nuns of other Orders, submitting at once to the Superior as if she had been one of her community. (Reference: Diego de *Yepes*, biography of St. Teresa, book 2, chapter 36.)</note>

<note>15 St. Teresa was prompt in her obedience. One day, during prayer-time in choir, she happened to make a slight noise. The Prioress said: “Whoever made that disturbance must go away.” And the Saint withdrew in silence. (From the witness testimony of Sister Frances of Jesus. Reference: Vicente de la *Fuente*, volume 6, page 290, note 9.)</note>

## [CHAP. XVIII.] DANGERS.

of this I am certain. Those who have not undertaken this obligation <note>The "obligation" refers to the formal religious vows, specifically obedience, taken by members of a religious order.</note>, but who wish to be <term>contemplatives</term> <note>Contemplatives are those who seek a life of deep, wordless prayer and direct communion with God.</note>, must, if they would walk in safety, resolutely submit their will to an experienced <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest who hears confessions and provides spiritual direction to guide a soul's progress.</note>. It is a well-known fact that they will thus make more progress in a year than they would otherwise have done in a very long time! Much has been written on this subject; however, as it does not concern you I will not enlarge upon it.

7. These, then, are the virtues that I wish you, my daughters, to possess, and to strive to obtain, and of which you should feel a holy envy. You must not be distressed if you do not experience these other devotional feelings which are unreliable. Although, in other people's case, it may come from God, yet in yours He might permit it to be an illusion of the devil, who would mislead you as he has misled many others, particularly women. Why run into danger in serving our Lord, when there are so many secure ways of doing so? Who wants you to incur such risk? There is need for much insistence on this point, for we are weak by nature, though God will strengthen those He calls to be contemplatives. I am glad to have given this advice to other persons: it will also animate those called to the <term>contemplative life</term> to the practice of <term>humility</term> <note>Humility is the religious virtue of recognizing one's own insignificance and complete dependence on God.</note>. If you think you do not require it, perhaps it may serve to amuse you. May our Lord give us light to follow Him in all things and we shall have nothing to fear.

## How souls should pray if they cannot make use of the understanding in prayer.

1. IT is a long time since I wrote the last pages, and I have had no opportunity of resuming the book, so that unless I read over the latter part I cannot remember what I said. <note>Teresa often wrote in the midst of a very busy life as a reformer and foundress, leading to long breaks in her writing.</note> However, to save time, I must go straight on, without either order or connection.
