2. One who is accustomed to this kind of prayer requires no further instruction: our Lord will thus bring her to the port of light; such a good beginning is sure to end well. Those who can walk in this way enjoy peace and security, for when the thoughts are kept under control the journey becomes easy. But, with God's permission, I wish to offer some help to those who cannot practise such prayer. Even if I fail, at least you will have learnt that many others suffer in the same way as yourselves, so that you need not be distressed about it. When you begin to practise prayer, I will give you some advice about this matter.

3. Some minds are as disorderly as unbroken horses—no one can quiet them: they rush about, hither and thither, and are never at rest. Although, with prudence, one may, in spite of this, escape harm, yet this is not always possible. Perhaps no vital injury may be done, yet there is a risk of making mistakes, and the soul is in a continual state of agitation and trouble. Either this is natural to certain persons, or God permits it. I pity them deeply. They are like a man parched with <note>The text ends mid-sentence, introducing a comparison to a thirsty person, which leads into Teresa's famous "living water" metaphor.</note>

thirst, who sees water in the far distance, and, while trying to get to it, is hindered by other people, both at starting, when he has got half-way, and again just before the end of his journey. They resemble those souls who, after a great effort and immense trouble, have defeated their first enemies, yet are beaten by the second adversaries, and would rather die of thirst than drink of water that costs them so dear. They lack strength, their courage fails them: though sometimes they overcome their second set of opponents yet they succumb before the third.

4. Perhaps they were not two steps off the **living fountain of water** of which our Lord spoke to the **Samaritan woman**,⁶ <note>In the Gospel of John, Jesus meets a woman at a well and offers her "living water" representing the Holy Spirit and eternal life.</note> promising that, whoever drank of it, should never thirst again.⁷ How true is this which was told us by Truth Himself, for the soul thirsts no more for the things of this world, although its craving for the next life exceeds any natural thirst that can be imagined. Yet, how the heart pines for this thirst, realising its priceless value! This drought brings its own remedy with it: it allays all desire of created things, and satisfies the soul. When it has been satiated by God, one of the greatest graces He can bestow on the spirit is to leave it with this thirst, which, after

->### CHAP. XIX. WATER AND LIVE-FIRE.<-
<page-num>123</page-num>

drinking, increases the longing to partake again and again of this water.<sup>8</sup>

5. As far as I can remember, water has three properties—there must be many more, but these suit my purpose. One property is that it chills other things. However warm any one may be, he is cooled by plunging into a river, and water extinguishes the fiercest fire, except **wild-fire**, <note>Also known as "Greek fire," a historical incendiary composition that was famous for its ability to burn on or even be ignited by water.</note> which it only kindles the more. How strange it is that water should only increase this fire, which is fierce, raging, and subject to none of the elements, so that its opposite, instead of putting it out, only adds fuel to its flames. I could explain a great deal by this, if only I understood **philosophy**. <note>In the 16th century, "philosophy" included what we now call natural sciences—the study of the physical laws and properties of the natural world.</note> If I knew the properties of things I should be able to make my meaning clear: as it is, though I am amused and interested by them I do not know how to express myself—perhaps I do not even understand the matter. When God gives you this water, sisters, this comparison will please you, and you will understand, as those do who drink of it, how a genuine love of God, that is powerful and freed from earthly dross, rises above mortal things and is sovereign over all the elements of this world. Though water may flow from the earth, there is no fear of its quenching the fire of divine love, over which it has no empire. Although they are the antidotes of one another, its

6. therefore they may almost seem to have claimed this power as a right, for, as the Psalmist <note>Referring to King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> says : “ Thou hast subjected all things under his feet.” ¹² Do you think this applies to every one ? I see some men “ under the feet ” of the things of this world. I knew a gentleman who was literally kicked to death in a struggle with his horses—you see how miserably he was “ subject ” to them ! Things occur every day that witness to the truth of what I say. Indeed, the Psalmist could not speak untruth, being guided by the Holy Ghost <note>The Holy Spirit.</note>. However, I may be mistaken in his meaning, or misquote the text as saying that perfect souls are rulers of the whole earth. The water I spoke of has an earthly source, and has no influence over the fire of the love of God, whose flames rise high above it, and spring from nothing so base. There are fires of a tepid love for God, which any passing event may extinguish, but although a tempest rose against it, fervent charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *caritas*, the highest form of Christian love—a selfless, divine love for God and others.</note> would not be destroyed, but would vanquish its enemy : if water should rain from heaven, ¹³ instead of putting out this fire, it would revive the flames. These two elements are not opposed to one another, but spring from the same origin. There is no fear of their harming one another—they only increase each other’s effects. <insert>#</insert> The water of genuine tears, shed during real prayer, is a

7. Ah, how delightful and wonderful a thing is this fire ! When it is combined with the rain from heaven from whence flow the tears I spoke of, it chills and even freezes all worldly affections. These waters are given us, and not obtained by any effort of our own : thus they leave no warmth that might attract us in anything of this world, unless it is something tending to feed this fire, which by its nature is insatiate <note>In this context, it means "never satisfied" or "quenchless."</note> and would, if possible, envelop the whole earth in its flames.

8. A second property of water is the cleansing of what is foul. What would become of mankind with no water to wash in ? This living, celestial water is limpid, undisturbed, and unmixed with any earthly matter, for it has come straight from heaven. The soul which has once drunk of it is cleansed and left pure and free from all sins.<sup>14</sup> <note>Original: "M. vi." refers to the Sixth Mansions of St. Teresa's work, *The Interior Castle*.</note> As I said, we are powerless to obtain this water for ourselves, because <term>perfect contemplation</term> <note>A state of prayer where the soul is quiet and God works directly upon it without the need for the person's own reasoning or imagination.</note> and <term>divine union</term> <note>A high state of spiritual experience where the human soul is perfectly joined with God's will.</note> are high and supernatural graces given by God to the soul that it may be washed and left stainless and purified from the mire contracted by its sins. I do not call prayer made by thinking over a subject, "**living water**," <note>A reference to the "Living Water" promised by Jesus to the Samaritan woman in the Gospel of John. 9. I will explain myself more clearly. While <term>meditating</term> <note>Teresa distinguishes "meditation" (active thinking about holy things) from "contemplation" (passive receiving of God's presence).</note> on the world and the contempt it deserves on account of its short duration, almost without knowing it, we find ourselves thinking about the worldly matters we used to care for. Although we try to check these thoughts, they distract us all the more by the remembrance of what happened, and speculations about what will come of it, what will be the consequences, what we did, and what we shall do. By pondering over the means of freeing ourselves from faults, we sometimes run into fresh danger. Not that we ought to omit such meditation, but we must be cautious and watchful. In <term>contemplation</term> <note>A higher state of prayer where the mind is silent and the soul is directly influenced by God's grace without the need for active reasoning.</note> our Lord Himself takes care of us, for He will not entrust us with our own interests. Our souls are so dear to Him that He prevents them from running into danger, while He is bestowing this **grace** <note>Divine help or favor given to the soul.</note> on them. 10. The third property of water is to satisfy and quench thirst. Thirst seems to me to be a desire of something which we need so greatly that we should die were we altogether deprived of it. Most certainly, our Lord and our supreme Good possesses no imperfection, and all He does is for our welfare; therefore, however abundant this water may be, it is never in excess, for there is nothing superfluous in His gifts. It is always wrong of us to wish for this water: it is solely through the grace of God that we reap any benefit from such a desire. We are **indiscreet** and think that, as this pain is sweet and enjoyable, we cannot have too much of it. We covet it beyond all measure, and do all we can to augment our longings, so that sometimes people die of such emotions. What a blessed death! Yet perhaps, by living, one might have helped others to die with the desire of such a death. I believe the devil has a hand in this: he knows what harm such people do him by living, and incites them to perform **imprudent penances** <note>In this context, "penances" refers to physical acts of self-denial or mortification. In such an extreme case, in my opinion, <term>humility</term> <note>In Teresa's writings, humility is not just modesty, but "walking in truth"—knowing exactly who one is in relation to God.</note> should make us fear, for we ought not to believe that our <term>charity</term> <note>Here, "charity" refers to *caritas*, the highest form of Christian love for God and neighbor.</note> is fervent enough to bring us to such a state : I think there would be no harm in our changing the bent of our wishes, although sometimes this is impossible. We may consider that by living longer <margin>Exclamations of the Soul to God, XIV, 4.</margin> we might serve God more, and might be able to enlighten some soul that would otherwise be lost ; and that if we did more for God we should deserve to enjoy Him more. Besides, we ought to feel alarm at thinking how little we have done for our <term>Creator</term>. These are fitting consolations for this great distress. Thus we shall assuage our pain and gain great merit, since for the sake of serving the God we long for so keenly we are willing to suffer and to bear our cross. It is like comforting some one who is in great sorrow by bidding him to be patient and to resign himself into the hands of God, that the divine Will may be done in him : this <term>resignation</term> <note>A state of total surrender where the soul stops fighting against God's plan and accepts present suffering with peace.</note> of ourselves is always the safest course to take.

13. But what if the devil were in any way concerned in these vehement desires ? <sup>21</sup> The hermit Heron was so austere <note>Extremely strict and self-denying.</note> that he refused to join the brethren even at their usual feast at Easter. Deceived by his presumption, he obeyed the order of Satan, disguised as an **angel of light**, who assured the recluse that if he threw himself down a deep well in the neighbourhood he would prove his sanctity by remaining unhurt. Heron was pulled out by the brethren and died three days afterwards from the injuries he had received, still persisting in his delusion. The Abbot Paphnutius considered that he had committed suicide and was with great difficulty persuaded to allow him the usual <term>memorial and oblation</term> <note>These are formal prayers and the offering of the Eucharist (Holy Communion) for the soul of the deceased.</note> granted to those at rest. <header>CHAPTER XX. ALL ARE INVITED.</header>

...and do not grow weary. Remember, our Lord invited “Any man.” He is Truth itself; His word cannot be doubted. If *all* people had not been included, He would not have addressed everybody, nor would He have said: “I will give you to drink.” He might have said: “Let all men come, for they will lose nothing by trying, and I will give a drink to those I think are worthy.” But since He said, without any conditions, “If *any man* thirsts, let him come to Me,” I am certain that no one will fail to drink of this <term>living water</term> <note>Based on John 4:10, this is Teresa’s term for the supernatural peace and direct experience of God that satisfies the soul.</note> unless they stop halfway. ### CHAP. XX.
# MENTAL PRAYER
<note>Mental prayer is a form of prayer where the individual focuses on a silent, internal conversation with God, rather than reciting pre-written vocal prayers.</note>

...be resolved to die rather than fail to reach the end of your journey. If at times our Lord allows you to feel parched and spiritually dry during this life, in eternity He will give you an abundance of water, with no fear of it ever failing through any fault of your own. God grant that we may never fail Him! Amen.

2. <gloss>The Escorial edition (an early manuscript of this work) marks this as chapter 34. Here, Teresa provides advice to the sisters on how to encourage others to practice prayer.</gloss> Let us now consider how to start on this journey. This is most important, for if we do not begin correctly, we might wander off the right track from the very first step; indeed, I believe everything depends upon this beginning. I do not mean that someone who is not yet firmly resolved to persevere in prayer should be forbidden from starting it; our Lord will gradually lead her toward perfection. Prayer possesses such virtue that one should never fear losing anything by trying it. If a person takes but one step, she will be richly rewarded, for prayer brings us many graces, both great and small. 

It is like an <term>indulgenced rosary</term>—one bead earns a certain <term>indulgence</term> and several beads gain many more. Yet, if we never actually used the <term>chaplet</term> but kept it shut up in a box, it would be better for us to be without it. <note>In Teresa’s time, a rosary was "indulgenced" if it was blessed by the Church to grant specific spiritual benefits (indulgences) to the user. A "chaplet" is another name for the string of prayer beads.</note> Thus, though many people do not persevere in prayer, any little progress they have made will give them light for other things; the farther they have advanced, the more light they will have. In short, they will certainly not harm themselves by beginning to practice it: good never brings forth evil. If you perceive a disposition or wish for prayer in people—

As you see, try to remove any fear they may feel of beginning <note>Referring to the beginning of a life of prayer.</note>. I ask you, for the love of God, always to make your conversation helpful to your visitors. Your prayers are for the good of souls, <margin>See *The Way of Perfection*, chapter 3, paragraphs 3-5; chapter 4, paragraphs 1 and 2.</margin> for whom you are bound to <term>intercede</term> <note>Intercession is the act of praying to God on behalf of others.</note> continually, and it seems wrong not to try to help them in every other possible way. If you would be a good kinswoman <note>A female relative.</note>, this is genuine affection: if you would wish to be a true friend, this is the only feasible way. Let the truth grow as it ought in your hearts by meditation and you will know what sort of love we should bear for our neighbours.

3. This is no time for child’s-play, and worldly friendship, even when innocent, seems nothing else. Neither with your relations nor with any one else, must you indulge in such foolish talk as: “Do you love me? Do you not like me?” unless it serves some real end by benefiting a soul. To persuade your kindred, or a brother, or any other person to listen to and to admit the truth, some such words and signs of affection, which are always grateful to the senses, may be needed. Perhaps he will be better pleased by one kind word, as he would call it, than by a great deal you might say about God: afterwards, however, it will make him more inclined to talk about religion. I do not forbid your using such endearments for a good end; otherwise they are useless and may do harm without your knowing it.

<summary>St. Teresa advises the nuns to transform their social interactions with relatives and visitors into opportunities for spiritual guidance, using kindness as a tool to lead others toward God. She warns against trivial "worldly friendship" that does not serve the ultimate goal of saving souls.</summary>

### CHAP. XX. INTERCOURSE WITH SECULARS. <note>"Seculars" refers to "the laity" or people living in the world rather than in a religious order.</note>

4. Every one knows you are **nuns** and that **prayer** is your business. Never say to yourselves: "I do not wish to be thought good." People will receive either profit or harm from what they see in you. It is very blameworthy of you, who are bound to speak solely on **religious subjects**,⁵ to refrain from doing so, unless sometimes, greater good may be done in this way. Your intercourse and character must be devout. Let those acquire it who wish to talk to you, but beware of learning worldly ways—they would be a hell to you. Never mind if you are considered ill-bred—still less if you are taken for a hypocrite—this would be an advantage. <note>Teresa suggests that if people think you are a hypocrite for being "too holy," they will stop bothering you with worldly gossip, which is a benefit to a nun's peace.</note> No one would wish to see you unless they spoke your language, for if one cannot understand Arabic <note>Teresa uses "Arabic" here as a metaphor for any language that is completely unintelligible to the listener.</note> one does not want to talk to a person who knows no other tongue: thus the world would neither weary you nor hurt you, for to spend all your time in learning a new dialect would be no small hurt. Not having had my experience, you cannot understand as I do how this injures the soul, which forgets one thing while learning another. It is a perpetual source of worry; this must by all means be avoided, for peace and quiet of mind

# CHAP. XXI.] FORTITUDE.

the heavenly road that leads to Paradise. This journey gains such vast treasures that no wonder the cost should seem dear. Some day we shall discover that all we have paid was nothing compared with the prize it has purchased for us. Let us return to speak of those who wish to travel by this path to the very end, and to the fount itself, where they will drink of the water of life. 

Although there are books written on the subject, yet I do not think it will be waste of time to speak of it here. How must one begin? I maintain that this is the chief point; in fact, that everything depends on their having a great and a most resolute determination never to halt until they reach their journey’s end,² happen what may, whatever the consequences are, cost what it will, let who will blame them, whether they reach the goal, or die on the road, or lose heart to bear the trials they encounter, or the earth itself goes to pieces beneath their feet.³ 

Men will warn us, again and again, of the risks we run: “Such a person was lost through this; another fell into error through it; some one else, who practised prayer, went wrong; it injures virtue, and is not fit for women, whom it may lead into illusions; it is best for them to keep to their spinning; they have no need of all these subtilties—the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original Latin: "Pater Noster"; the primary prayer taught by Jesus to his disciples.</note> and

⁴ In 1559 the Grand Inquisitor <note>The lead official of the Inquisition, an office of the Church responsible for identifying and suppressing heresy.</note>, Don Fernando de Valdes, published an **Index** <note>The *Index Librorum Prohibitorum*, a list of books the Church forbade Catholics to read.</note> in which, besides many heretical books, he also proscribed excellent Catholic works of devotion on the plea that they were unfit for "the wives of carpenters" <note>A dismissive phrase used by the Inquisitor to imply that ordinary, uneducated people (especially women) should not be reading deep spiritual texts.</note>. His Majesty has been to me a veritable Book, in which I saw all truth." (*Morel-Fatio, The Readings of Saint Teresa, Paris, 1908.*)

[CHAP. XXI.]                 THE LORD’S PRAYER.                 141

The words of the **Gospel** <note>The first four books of the New Testament (Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John), which record the life and teachings of Jesus Christ.</note>, which came from our Lord’s most sacred mouth, have always been dear to me, and arouse a more fervent devotion in me than the most carefully-written and learned books, especially those that were not composed by an **approved author** <note>In the context of the Spanish Inquisition, "approved" meant books that had passed official censorship and were deemed safe for the public to read without risk of heresy.</note>, for in such a case I never cared to glance at them.

3. I will keep close to the side of this Master of all wisdom, Who perhaps may teach me how to say what will meet your needs. I do not say that I will explain these divine petitions—I dare not presume to do so. Much has been written on the subject, and even if it had not, it would be absurd *for me* to undertake the task. I will only put before you some considerations on the words of the **Our Father** <note>original Latin: "Pater Noster"; the central prayer of the Christian faith, which St. Teresa is preparing to analyze for her nuns.</note>, for sometimes too many books destroy our devotion just when we need it most. There is no doubt that a master has a kind feeling for his pupils, that he wishes them to understand his teaching, and does his best to make them comprehend it.⁵

If, when you are trying to earn or "bear away" this treasure for yourselves—for, as our Lord says, "The violent bear it away" ⁶—and are travelling by the royal and safe road by which our Lord, all the elect, and the saints passed—if, even then, men warn you of so many dangers, so many horrors—what must be the risks incurred by those who seek these riches with no path to guide them?

O my daughters! there can be no comparison between the hazard of those travellers and our own. Yet such souls never realise this until they fall headlong into peril, with no one to help them out of it. Thus they lose this water altogether; they drink of it neither much nor little, and do not even taste it from a pool or streamlet. How can they travel among so many dangers without a drop of it to support them? At the best, they must die of thirst, for whether we will or no, daughters, we must all journey to this fountain, although we may take different routes. Take my advice, and let no one mislead you by pointing out any other way than prayer.

A want of **humility** <note>For St. Teresa, humility is "walking in truth"—honestly recognizing one’s own weakness and God’s greatness.</note>, of the virtues, may endanger you, but prayer—*prayer*! ...from those who gratify him. In troublous times, when the enemy has sown his **cockle** <note>original: "zizania" / cockle. This is a biblical reference to "tares" or weeds that look like wheat, used here to describe false teachings or distractions sown by the devil.</note> and seems to be leading all mankind in his wake—half-blinded as they are by misguided zeal—God raises up someone to open their eyes and bid them look at the mists the devil has raised to hide the way from them.

If people say prayer is dangerous <margin>Book of Foundations, chapter 3, paragraph 3.</margin>, he endeavors to teach them its benefits by his deeds, if not by his words. In Teresa's time, there was significant debate about how often a person should receive the Sacrament.</note>. Thus, if only one or two souls, throwing aside all misgivings, follow the better way, our Lord gradually regains what had before been lost.

6. Therefore, sisters, banish these misgivings: take no notice of public opinion. This is no time to believe everything you hear. Be guided only by those who conform their lives to that of Christ; try to keep a good conscience; practice humility; despise all earthly things; and firmly believe the teaching of our holy Mother the Church—then you may feel sure you are on the right road. Cast aside these causeless fears. If anyone tries to frighten you, humbly explain the matter to him: tell him that our **Rule** <margin>The Carmelite Rule, chapter 5.</margin> <note>The official set of guidelines for the Carmelite Order, which emphasizes silence and constant prayer.</note> bids us pray constantly.

## CHAPTER XXII.<gloss>1</gloss>

### This chapter explains the nature of mental prayer.

*1. Vocal prayer inherently involves mental prayer. 2. The necessity of reverence during prayer. 3. God is the King, and we are his petitioners. 4. A prayer for divine guidance. 5. Christ is the Bridegroom of our souls. 6. We learn to know and love Him through mental prayer.*

1. You must understand, daughters, that there is no need to keep our lips closed in order to pray mentally. If, while 

---

<gloss>10</gloss> "As far as I can understand, the gate by which one enters this castle is prayer and meditation. I am not referring more to mental than to vocal prayer; for if it is truly prayer, the mind must be involved. If a person does not consider whom he is addressing, what he is asking for, or who he is to dare to speak with God, then even if his lips move through many words, I do not call it prayer. Sometimes, indeed, one may pray with devotion without active reflection because they have practiced it so often before. However, the habit of speaking to Almighty God as casually as one would to a slave—not caring if the words are appropriate, but simply rattling off a memorized script—cannot be called prayer. God grant that no Christian addresses Him in such a way" (*The Interior Castle*, Mansion 1, chapter 1, paragraph 9). <note>Teresa's "Interior Castle" is her masterpiece on the soul's journey toward God; here the translator links the two books to show her consistent teaching on the necessity of "mindfulness" in prayer.</note>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXII.<-

I utter a prayer, I carefully consider its meaning and pay more attention to what I am saying to God than to the words themselves, this is both **mental prayer** <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Internal, heart-to-heart conversation with God.</note> and **vocal prayer** <term>vocal prayer</term> <note>Prayers spoken aloud or recited from a formula.</note>. Should people affirm that you are praying to God, if while you recite the *Our Father* <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note>, or the *Hail Mary* <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note>, you are thinking of earthly things—then I have no more to say. But if you wish to behave with due respect while speaking to such a mighty Monarch, you should reflect on Who He is you are addressing and what you yourself are, that you may show fitting reverence. For how can you accost Him, and petition His Majesty the King, or know what ceremonies to use, unless you realise His rank and your own position? This it is that regulates the respect to be paid, and you must learn it, unless you wish to be turned away as a boor, with your requests ungranted. More than that, unless you are well versed in the matter, you should take care to find out the proper titles to use. I happened once, before I was accustomed to deal with **grandees** <term>grandees</term> <note>The highest rank of Spanish nobility.</note>, to be brought in contact concerning business matters with a person of rank, whom I was told to address as “*your Lordship*.” But my wits are dull and I am unused to such customs, so when I met him I forgot all about it, and he burst out laughing, because I condescended to call him “*Your Grace!*” <note>Teresa used a title of lesser rank than the gentleman's true status, a humorous social blunder that she uses here to illustrate the importance of knowing whom one is addressing in prayer.</note>

2. How canst Thou, Ruler of all creation as Thou <note>Text ends mid-sentence; Teresa is beginning an address to God regarding His humility and accessibility despite His infinite majesty.</note>

From the Nicene Creed: "Of His kingdom there shall be no end."</note> Blessed mayest Thou be! What are you saying, Christians, when you affirm that there is no need for <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Internal prayer where the mind and heart engage with God, rather than just reciting words.</note>? Do you understand your own words? Really, I think you cannot do so, and you wish us all to fall into the same mistake. You do not know what mental prayer is, how <term>vocal prayers</term> <note>Prayers that are spoken aloud or recited from a set text.</note> must be said, nor what <term>contemplation</term> <note>A higher state of wordless prayer and divine union.</note> is, or you would not distrust and condemn in one place what you approve of in another.

3. Whenever I remember it, I will always speak of mental and vocal prayer together, my daughters, lest I should alarm you. I know how such matters end, having suffered a great deal of trouble from them, and I do not wish any one to disturb you, nor to confuse your mind on the subject, for timidity about prayer injures the soul. It is most important for you to know the right way to set about it. If one told a traveller that he

Yet this **King** is so humble that, however unmannerly my speech may be, He does not refuse to hear me on that account, nor do His guards repulse me. For the angels who attend Him know that their **Monarch** prefers the rusticity of a lowly shepherd, who would be more polite if only he knew how, to the courtly speeches of a learned but proud man. To atone for what **God** suffers from bearing with the loathsome presence of such a creature as myself, we ought to try to understand His purity and His sublimity. True, we know already Who it is that we approach when we draw near Him as we recognise the <term>magnates</term> <note>People of great power, rank, or influence in the world.</note> of this world. When we have been told who their father was and what their revenues and titles are, there is no more to know as we honour them, not for their good...

<page-num>151</page-num>

## [CHAP. XXII.] TO KNOW GOD.

...leave Thy brides <note>St. Teresa refers to the nuns who, through their religious vows, are spiritually "married" to Christ.</note> to do so in peace, since they spend their lives with Thee. Sometimes a husband is so jealous that he will permit his wife to speak to no one but himself. In order to please him and to live happily with him, this is but a small sacrifice for her to make: she should not wish to talk to others since she has all she can desire in him.

6. To understand these truths, my daughters, is to practise <term>mental prayer</term> <note>A form of internal prayer where the soul speaks directly to God from the heart, focusing on His presence rather than just reciting set formulas.</note>. If you like to meditate on them and to pray <term>vocally</term> <note>Vocal prayer refers to prayers said aloud or whispered, such as the "Our Father" or the "Hail Mary."</note> also, that is all well and good, but never address your words to God while you are thinking of something else—whoever does so, knows nothing of <term>mental prayer</term>. I think I have explained the matter clearly: God give us the grace to know how to practise it! Do not let yourselves be alarmed by any misgivings. Praise God, Who is almighty, and let no one hinder your making <term>mental prayer</term>. If any one of you cannot recite her prayers with such attention, let her know that she does not fulfil her obligation. If she wishes to pray with perfection, she must try with all her heart to be <term>recollected</term> <note>The act of "recollection" involves drawing one's wandering thoughts and senses inward to focus entirely on the divine presence within the soul.</note>, unless she would fail in her duties as the <term>bride</term> of this great King. Beg Him, daughters, to enable me to practise what I have taught you, for I fall very short of doing so. May His Majesty aid me, for His own sake. Amen.

<summary>St. Teresa concludes her teaching on the intimacy of the soul's relationship with God, defining mental prayer as a focused, attentive conversation with the Divine that requires the heart's full presence.</summary>

## CHAPTER XXIII. <note>In the Valladolid manuscript, this is chapter 25; in the Escorial manuscript, it is chapter 39.</note>

### How important it is that one who has entered on the way of prayer should not turn back.

->*1. Why we should persevere in prayer. 2. Because we must be generous. 3. Perseverance in prayer defeats the devil and gives us courage. 4. Reward gained by mental prayer.*<-

1. How I have wandered from the subject! I think it is essential to begin the practice of prayer with a firm resolution of persevering in it. The reasons for this are so many that it would take too long to list them—besides, they are explained in a number of books. I will content myself, then, with mentioning two or three. Firstly, when we give this attention (which is slight in itself) of our thoughts to God—an attention which is not fruitless, but which brings us a rich reward—when we render this <term>homage</term> <note>A formal expression of loyalty or honor. In this context, it refers to the soul’s duty to acknowledge God as its Creator.</note> to Him, Who has bestowed so much on us and Who continues to shower benefits upon us, it would be wrong not to give it to Him entirely. We should not be like one who gives a thing, meaning to take it back again. This cannot be called “giving”; in fact, anyone who has received a present always feels more or less annoyed if it is reclaimed by the donor, especially when they had come to look upon it as their own property. If this occurs between friends, and the giver is already indebted to the recipient for many <term>gratuitous</term> <note>Given freely, without being earned or deserved.</note> favors, the latter may justly

<summary>St. Teresa introduces the vital importance of perseverance in prayer, arguing that once we resolve to give our time and attention to God, we must not take that gift back.</summary>

Does God merit less than this, that we should mock Him by first giving Him this trifle and then taking it away? Since we have resolved to devote to Him this short space of time (which we should otherwise bestow on our friends, who would not thank us for it) let us yield it Him with thoughts that are free, and withdrawn from all else. Let us fully resolve never to take it back, whatever crosses it may bring us, and in spite of all <term>aridities</term> <note>Spiritual "aridity" refers to a period in prayer where a person feels no emotional satisfaction, warmth, or "sensible" devotion, often described as a spiritual desert.</note>.² <note>This concept is also discussed in St. Teresa’s other major work, *The Interior Castle*, Second Mansions, Chapter 1, Paragraph 15.</note>

God is not exacting: He does not scrutinise details, and if you seek to please Him, you are offering Him a gift. even then, they do something: this Lord of ours takes any payment and accommodates Himself to our <term>humours</term> <note>In the 16th-century context, "humours" refers to a person's specific temperament, moods, or physical disposition.</note>. 3. A second reason why our resolution should be firm is that this lessens the devil’s power of tempting us. He is very frightened of determined souls, knowing by experience how they injure him, and that, by trying to do them mischief, he only profits them and others, and damages himself. Still, we must not grow careless, or trust to this, for we have to deal with traitors who, though too cowardly to attack the wary, yet inflict great harm on the negligent. If they find that a soul is fickle, irresolute, and wanting in <term>perseverance</term> <note>A central theme for St. Teresa; she believed steady persistence was more important than emotional fervor.</note> in the right path, they will never leave it alone day or night, and will suggest to it endless fears and difficulties. Experience has convinced me of this, and I have been able to explain it to you: most people do not realise its gravity. A third and very weighty reason is that a resolute soul fights more courageously, knowing that, come what may, it must never retreat. It is like a soldier in the midst of the fray, who knows that if he is vanquished he must expect **no quarter**, <note>A military term meaning that no mercy will be shown to those who surrender; they will be killed regardless.</note> but that, if he does not fall during the battle, he will be killed after-

4. We ought to feel no doubt that, unless we allow ourselves to be defeated, we are sure to succeed. This is certain, for, however insignificant our conquest may be, we shall come off with great gains. Never fear that our Lord will allow us to die of thirst after inviting us to drink of this fountain. I have said it before, and I shall often repeat it: for people who have not learnt our Lord’s goodness by experience, but only know of it by faith, are often discouraged. It is a great grace to have proved for oneself what friendship and caresses He bestows on those who walk by the way of prayer, and how, as it were, He defrays all the costs. be of little use for me to wear myself out with telling you about it. This is incontestable: I know it from experience. If you find it is false, never believe a single word I have ever said to you. Those who, by the mercy of God, have learnt it for themselves can bear witness to what I affirm. It is well to have said this for the sake of those who will come after me.

->How vocal prayer may be made with perfection.<-

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XXIV.] RECOLLECTION.</header>

same way. What I will advise you about, or I may say, *teach* you (for as <term>Prioress</term> <note>The superior or head of a convent of cloistered nuns; Teresa uses her authority here to guide her sisters' spiritual practices.</note>, I am your Mother, and have the right to do so), is how to pray vocally, because you ought to understand the words you utter. Since long prayers may tire one who cannot fix her mind on God, I will not speak of them, but only of those which, as Christians, we are bound to repeat—namely the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> and the <term>Hail Mary</term> <note>original: "Ave Maria"</note>.²

2. Clearly, we ought to attend to how we say our prayers ; then no one can say we speak without understanding our own words. Perhaps we think it is enough for us to pray as a matter of habit and that it suffices if we simply pronounce the words. Whether it suffices or no, is not for me to say : I leave the decision to <term>theologians</term> <note>Scholars who study the nature of God and religious doctrine; Teresa often deferred to learned men on matters of dogma while relying on her own experience for prayer.</note> : God will give them light to guide those who consult them, and as to those who do not belong to our state of life, it is no business of mine. But, my daughters, I do not wish us to content ourselves with this. When I recite, in the <term>Creed</term> <note>original: "Credo"</note>, “ I believe . . .” it seems to me that I ought to know and to understand what it is that “ I believe.” If I say, “ Our Father,” love requires that I should know *Who* is “ our Father,” and *Who* the Master That teaches us this prayer, for there is an immense difference between one master and another. If you tell me that it is enough to know this once for all and to think no more about it, you might as well say

-># THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXIV.<-

...that it is enough to recite the prayer itself once in a lifetime. It is shameful to forget even our human teachers, especially if they were very holy and were our spiritual guides: we could not do so if we were faithful pupils. We should preserve a strong affection and respect for them, and should often speak of them. God forbid, then, that whenever we say this prayer we should not think of such a Master, so loving and desirous of our good. Still, human nature is so frail that we may often forget Him.

3. I have already explained that we cannot speak both to God and to the world at the same time. I am not alluding to times when people are out of health (especially if they suffer from <term>melancholia</term> <note>A historical term for depression or mental illness; Teresa was remarkably progressive in recognizing that such conditions were medical rather than spiritual failures.</note>), or when their brains are tired, for then no effort will control the attention. ->### CHAP. XXIV.] DISTRACTIONS.<-

...this is found to be impossible. Such a person cannot attend to what she is saying, strive as she may, nor can she fix her thoughts on any other subject: indeed she seems bereft of reason and her wits wander—still the pain this state causes her proves her to be blameless in the matter. She should not trouble herself about it; this would only increase the evil: let her not tire herself by trying to reduce her mind to reason, of which, in such a state, it is incapable. Let her pray as best she can, or leave off praying and rest her brain as if she were ill, <margin>✓</margin> occupying herself with some other good work. This advice applies to persons who watch carefully over themselves and who grasp the truth that they cannot speak both to God and to the world at the same time.

4. On our part, we can endeavour to be alone—God grant this may suffice to make us realise in Whose Presence we are, and how He answers our petitions. <margin>✓</margin> Do you suppose He is silent, though we cannot hear Him? He <term>speaks to our hearts</term> <note>Teresa refers to "interior locutions" or the subtle ways God communicates through the conscience and feelings rather than audible words.</note> when our hearts speak to Him. It would be good for us to believe that He teaches this prayer to each one of us in particular. This Master is never so far off that His scholars need raise their voices to make Him hear: He keeps very close to them. I want to show you that to say the *<term>Our Father</term>* <note>original Latin: "Pater Noster"</note> well, you must not leave the side of the <term>Tutor</term> <note>Christ, who according to the Gospels, taught the "Our Father" to His disciples.</note> Who teaches it you. Perhaps you will say that this is meditation, and that you cannot pray except vocally, nor do you wish to do so.

<page-num>160</page-num>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXV.

Some people are impatient and self-indulgent, and find it difficult to collect their thoughts when they begin to pray, being unused to the habit; therefore to avoid some little trouble, they say they do not know how, nor can they do more than pray vocally <note>Reciting established, written prayers out loud or silently, such as the "Our Father."</note>. You are right in calling that which I am speaking of “mental prayer,” <note>A form of prayer where the heart and mind engage with God without relying solely on a set script; similar to meditation.</note> but I assure you that vocal prayers, properly recited, cannot be separated from it, if we are to realize with Whom we are speaking. We are bound to pray with attention, and may God grant that, with the aid of all these means, we may succeed in saying the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> well, without wandering thoughts. I sometimes suffer from them, and I find that the best remedy is to keep my mind fixed on Him to Whom my words are addressed. You must be patient, and try to accustom yourselves to this most necessary practice, which for nuns—and, in my opinion, for all good Christians—is indispensable.

...quite possible, while you are reciting the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> or some other prayer (if you say it well), that God may raise you to <term>perfect contemplation</term>.<gloss>2</gloss> <note>A mystical state where the soul is directly united with God, transcending ordinary thought or effort.</note> <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Teresa’s respectful and characteristic title for God, reflecting both His sovereignty and her personal relationship with Him.</note> thus shows that He is listening to His handmaid and speaks to her in return, suspending the understanding and stopping the thoughts—one might say, taking the words out of her mouth—so that speech becomes impossible, or at least requires a strong effort. The soul understands that the divine Master is teaching it without the sound of words. He <term>suspends the faculties</term>,<gloss>3</gloss> <note>In the theology of the time, the "faculties" are the powers of the soul: memory, understanding, and will. They are happy, without knowing why: the soul is inflamed with love without comprehending how it loves: it feels that it enjoys the Beloved, yet how it does so it cannot tell. It realises that this delight can never be gained through any desire of the mind itself: the will embraces its joy without apprehending how, yet it dimly perceives that every good work mankind could perform in this world would not merit such a reward, for it is the gift of the Lord of heaven and earth, Who gives it in the manner befitting His Godhead.

2. This, daughters, is perfect contemplation. Now you can see how it differs from mental prayer, which, as I explained to you, consists in thinking over and realising...

A traditional prayer of confession used to acknowledge one's faults before God at the start of religious services or private devotion.</note> and make the sign of the cross—then, my daughters, as you are alone, seek for some companion—and where could you find a better one than the Master Who taught you the prayer <note>Teresa refers to Jesus, who taught the "Our Father" (the Lord's Prayer) to His followers.</note> you are about to say? 2. O my sisters! let those among you who cannot pursue a train of thought nor restrain the wild wanderings of your imagination take this advice. 3. I am not now asking you to meditate on Him nor to produce great thoughts, nor to feel deep devotion <note>Teresa distinguishes between "meditation"—an active, intellectual effort—and simply "looking," which is a more direct, quiet focus of the heart.</note> : I only ask you to look at Him. Who can prevent you turning the eyes of your soul (but for an instant, if you can do no more) on our Lord? You are able to look on many ugly and vulgar things; then can you not gaze upon the fairest sight imaginable? If He does not appear beautiful to you, I give you leave never to think of Him, although, daughters, He never takes His eyes off you! He has borne with many offences and much unworthiness in you, yet these have not sufficed to make Him turn away: is it much to ask, that you should sometimes lift your gaze from earth to fix it on Him? See, He is only waiting for us to look on Him, as the Bride <note>A traditional reference to the soul as the "Bride of Christ," often drawing from the poetic imagery of the Song of Solomon.</note> says.² You will find that He suits Himself to whatever mood you are in. He longs so keenly for our glance that He will neglect no means to win it.

4. They say that a wife must do this if she wishes to live happily with her husband. If he is sad, she too must appear unhappy: if he is merry (although she may be feeling far from cheerful), she must appear light-hearted also: see from what bondage <note>Teresa points out that while earthly marriage in her time required a wife to mirror her husband's emotions regardless of her own, the spiritual relationship with Christ offers a different kind of freedom where He accommodates Himself to the soul's state.</note> you are freed, sisters!

-> THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXVI. <-

This is what our Lord really does with us. He subjects Himself to us and wishes us to take command, and He will do our will. If you feel happy, think of Him at His Resurrection <note>The Resurrection refers to Christ rising from the dead on the third day, the foundational event of Christian hope and joy.</note>, for the very thought of how He rose from the tomb will delight you. How He shone with splendour! How beautiful and majestic, how victorious, how joyful He was! What spoils He brought away from the battle <note>A reference to the "Harrowing of Hell," a traditional belief that Christ descended into the realm of the dead to liberate the souls of the righteous who died before His coming.</note>, where He won a glorious kingdom that He wishes to make all your own! Is it much for you to look but once on Him Who gives you such riches? 

If you have trials to bear, if you are sorrowful, watch Him on His way to the garden <note>The Garden of Gethsemane, where Jesus prayed in agony the night before His crucifixion.</note>. What grief must have arisen in His soul to cause Him, Who was patience itself, to manifest it and to complain of it! See Him, bound to the column <note>The "Scourging at the Pillar," where Jesus was whipped by Roman soldiers.</note>, <margin>Exclamations</margin> <note>original: "Exclamaciones." In the original Escorial manuscript, Teresa noted "Exclamations" in the margin to highlight the emotional intensity of this passage.</note> full of suffering, His flesh all torn to pieces because of His tender love for you—persecuted by some, spat upon by others, denied and deserted by His friends, with none to plead for Him. He is stiff with the cold, and in such utter loneliness that you may well console one another. Or look on Him again—laden with the cross, and not allowed to stay to take breath. He will gaze at you with those beautiful, compassionate eyes, brimming with tears, and will forget His own grief to solace yours, only because you went to comfort Him and turned towards Him.

you may say, if your heart has been so melted at seeing Him in this state that not only do you look at Him, but you feel delight in speaking to Him (not in any fixed form of prayer, but out of your compassion, which greatly touches Him),

Shame at seeing You in such plight shall make me endure all the trials that may come to me: I will count them gain that I may imitate You in something. 8. It would be very helpful if you wore a medal or some picture of our Lord that pleases you. Do not merely hang it round your neck never to look at it, but often speak to it: He will teach you what to say. If you can find words for other people, why can you not speak to God? Do not fancy that you are unable. I, at least, will not believe that you cannot do so if only you endeavour to accustom yourselves to it. Unless you do this, you will find nothing to say. When we never talk to people, we become estranged from them, and do not know how to address them: even if they are relations they seem like strangers, for kinsfolk and friends become lost to us if we keep aloof from them.

9. It is very helpful to read a book of devotion <gloss>8</gloss> in the <term>vulgar tongue</term> <note>The "vulgar tongue" refers to the vernacular—the common language spoken by the people (in Teresa's case, Spanish). At this time, many religious texts were only in Latin, which most women could not read.</note>, so as to learn how to collect the thoughts, and to pray well vocally, thus, little by little, enticing the soul by coaxing and persuasion, so that it may not take alarm. You must be very wary, for it has deserted its Lover <note>In the tradition of Christian mysticism, the "Lover" refers to God or Christ, representing the soul's intimate spiritual partner.</note> many years ago, and needs very careful management to induce it to return to its home. We sinners have so accustomed ourselves and our thoughts

->CHAP. XXVII.] GOD OUR FATHER.<-

# CHAPTER XXVII.¹

Speaks of the great love shown us by our Lord in the first words of the <term>Our Father</term> <note>Original Latin: "Pater Noster." The prayer taught by Jesus to his disciples.</note>. How important it is that those who sincerely desire to be the daughters <note>Teresa addresses her "daughters," the nuns of the Discalced Carmelite Order, for whom this book was originally written as a guide.</note> of God should despise all pride of birth.

1. *“ Our Father Who art in heaven.” God our Father.* 2. *Thanking our Lord for having given Him to us as our Father.* 3. *Our dignity as children of God.* 4. *Contempt to be felt for aristocratic lineage.* 5. *Love for our heavenly Father.*

## “ OUR FATHER, WHO ART IN HEAVEN.”

1. O MY God, how worthy art Thou to be the Father of such a Son, and how manifest it is that He is Thy Son indeed ! Mayest Thou be for ever praised ! Would not this great favour have come more suitably at the end of the prayer ? From the very first, Thou dost fill our hands and dost grant us a grace so great that it would be well if the understanding could be absorbed so as to preoccupy the will, and to make us unable to say another word. How appropriate, my daughters, would be perfect <term>contemplation</term> <note>In Teresa's teaching, contemplation is a high form of prayer where the soul experiences a direct, infused awareness of God’s presence, moving beyond spoken words or active reasoning.</note> here ! Well might the soul retreat into itself, the better to rise above self, so that this holy Son might teach us what the “ heaven ” is like in which He tells us that His Father abides. Let us leave this world, daughters, for we ought not to hold this favour so cheap, after we have once realised its value, as to remain on earth any longer.

2. O thou Son of God and Lord of mine! Why dost Thou give us so much with the very first word Thou speakest? Besides humbling Thyself to the dust by joining Thy petitions to our own, and by making Thyself the Brother of such miserable wretches as ourselves, Thou dost give us, in Thy Father's name, all that can be given—Thou dost ask Him to make us His children, and Thy word cannot fail, but must perforce accomplish its object. Thus dost Thou bind Him to do Thy will, which implies no slight obligation, for since He is our Father, He must bear with us, however deeply we offend Him, if, like the **prodigal son** <note>A reference to the famous biblical parable in Luke 15, where a son squanders his inheritance but is welcomed back by his father with open arms and total forgiveness.</note>, we return to Him. He must pardon us; console us in our trials; maintain us in a way that becomes Him Who must needs be a far better Father than any earthly parent, since all His attributes must be supreme in their perfection. More than this, He must make us brethren and **co-heirs** <note>A theological term based on Romans 8:17, meaning those who receive an inheritance together. Teresa is emphasizing that Christ shares His own divine status and "inheritance" with humanity.</note> with Thee! Thy love for us, O Lord, and Thy humility, remove all obstacles: besides, Thy having lived on earth, clothed with a mortal body, offers some reason for Thy caring for us, seeing that Thou dost share our nature. But remember, that, as Thou hast told us, Thy Father dwells in heaven, therefore Thou shouldst guard His honour. Although Thou hast offered Thyself to suffer shame for our sake, yet leave Him free! Impose no such ties upon Him on behalf of any one so guilty as myself,

I see that, like a dearly loved Son, You spoke both for Yourself and for us, and through Your power You ensured that Your prayer made on earth should be granted in heaven. May You be blessed forever, Lord, You who so love to give that nothing can hold back Your hand.²

[CHAPTER 28] LOVE FOR OUR FATHER.
<page-num>175</page-num>

5. You have a good Father given you by the holy Jesus: let no other father be known here through any words of yours. Strive, daughters, to merit God’s caresses; cast yourselves into His arms. You know that He will never send you from Him while you remain dutiful children. Who would not guard against losing such a Father? Ah, what a consolation this is! Still, rather than enlarge on the subject, I prefer to leave it to your own thoughts, for, however inconstant your imagination may be, between such a Son and such a Father the <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>The third person of the Trinity, often described in mystical theology as the bond of love between the Father and the Son.</note> must perforce be found. May He inflame your will and constrain you with most fervent love, since even your own great gain suffices not to urge you to it.

# CHAPTER XXVIII

**Describes the <term>prayer of recollection</term> <note>A method of prayer where the person withdraws their senses from external things to focus entirely on the presence of God within the soul.</note> and lays down rules for practising it.** <margin>Escorial manuscript: Begins to teach how to recollect the mind.</margin>

1. “*Who art in heaven.*” Where this “heaven” is. 2. How God makes His heaven in our soul. 3. It is false humility not to recognise this. 4. The prayer of recollection. 5. Its advantages. 6. Exterior signs of recollection. 7. Results of practising recollection. 8. How it helps our progress. 9. The interior palace. 10. How to treat our Divine Guest. 11. How He dilates our soul. 12. What He asks of us in return.

## “WHO ART IN HEAVEN.”

1. Do you suppose it is of little consequence whether or not you know what this heaven is, and where you <note original: "Qui es in caelis">Teresa is beginning her commentary on the first line of the Our Father.</note>

->CHAP. XXVIII.] FALSE HUMILITY*<-
<page-num>177</page-num>
<sig>I2</sig>

to take wings to find Him, but have only to seek solitude and to look within ourselves. You need not be overwhelmed with confusion before so kind a Guest <note>Teresa refers to the indwelling of the Holy Trinity within the soul, a core concept in her mystical theology.</note>, but, with utter humility, talk to Him as to your Father: ask for what you want as from a father: tell Him your sorrows and beg Him for relief, realising, at the same time, that you are unworthy to be called His daughter.

3. Lay aside a certain reticence which some people maintain towards Him, under the impression that it is humility. Humility would not lead you to refuse a favour from the king, but would make you accept and take pleasure in it, although you recognised how little it was your due. What humility! I receive in my house the Lord of heaven and earth, Who comes to show me kindness and to talk to me, and, because of my *humility*, I neither answer nor remain with Him, nor accept His gifts, but go away and leave Him alone! And though He allows me, and even bids me, to ask Him for wealth, yet through humility I remain in my poverty, and even permit Him to depart because He sees that I want resolution to speak to Him. Practise no such humility, my daughters, but address Him sometimes as a Father or as a Brother, or again as a Master or as your <term>Bridegroom</term> <note>The "Spouse" or "Bridegroom" is a central metaphor in Christian mysticism, particularly in the Carmelite tradition, representing the most intimate union possible between the human soul and God.</note>: sometimes in one way and sometimes in another, for He will teach you what He wishes you to do. Do not be foolish: remind Him that He has promised to be your Bridegroom, and treat Him as if He were.

**Key Vocabulary**
*   **False Humility:** A spiritual trap where a person avoids God's gifts or presence out of a misguided sense of unworthiness.
*   **Solitude:** The necessary environment for looking inward to find God.
*   **Guest:** The Divine presence residing within the person in a state of grace.
*   **Father / Brother / Master / Bridegroom:** The various relational roles through which Teresa encourages the soul to interact with Christ.
*   **Reticence:** A harmful shyness or silence that prevents the soul from speaking honestly to God.

<summary>St. Teresa argues against "false humility," which leads people to distance themselves from God out of a sense of unworthiness. Instead, she encourages practitioners to embrace the intimacy of God's presence within themselves, relating to Him as a father, brother, or spouse.</summary>
<keywords>false humility, prayer of recollection, spiritual intimacy, Bridegroom of the soul, internal prayer, Teresa of Avila, solitude</keywords>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXVIII.

<margin>5 Escorial manuscript, chapter 47. Begins to explain the prayer of recollection.</margin>
Be convinced of your need of realising that God dwells within you, and that you may remain there with Him.<gloss>5</gloss>

4. Although only vocal <note>Teresa emphasizes that even prayers spoken aloud, like the "Our Father," can lead to deep contemplation if said with awareness.</note>, yet this kind of prayer rivets the thoughts much more quickly than any other kind, and has many advantages. It is called <term>recollection</term><gloss>6</gloss> <note>From the Spanish "recogimiento," referring to the act of gathering one’s wandering senses and faculties inward to focus on the divine presence within the soul.</note>, because by its means the soul collects together all the faculties <note>In medieval psychology, the "faculties" usually referred to the memory, understanding, and will.</note>, and enters within itself to be with God. The divine Master thus comes more speedily than He otherwise would to teach it and to grant it the <term>prayer of quiet</term> <note>A level of mystical prayer where the soul experiences a deep, peaceful rest in God's presence.</note>. For, being retired within itself, the spirit can meditate on the **Passion** and can there picture in its thoughts the Son, and can offer Him to the Father, without tiring the mind by journeying to find Him on Mount Calvary, or in the garden, or at the column <note>Refers to the locations of Christ's suffering: the Crucifixion at Calvary, the agony in Gethsemane, and the scourging at the pillar.</note>.

5. Those who are able thus to enclose themselves within the little heaven of their souls where dwells the Creator of both heaven and earth, and who can accustom themselves not to look at anything nor to remain in any place which would preoccupy their exterior senses, may feel sure that they are travelling by an excellent way, and that they will certainly attain to drink of the water from the fountain, for they will journey far in a short time. They resemble a man who goes by sea, and who, if the weather is favourable, gets in a few days

### CHAPTER XXVIII. SIGNS OF RECOLLECTION.

...to the end of a voyage which would have taken far longer by land. These souls may be said to have already put out to sea, and though they have not quite lost sight of <term>solid ground</term> <note>original: "terra firma"</note>, still they do their best to get away from it by collecting their <term>faculties</term> <note>In the psychology of Teresa’s time, these "faculties" are the powers of the soul: memory, understanding, and will.</note>.

6. If this <term>recollection</term> <note>The act of gathering the mind's wandering thoughts and focusing them inward on the divine presence within the soul.</note> is genuine it is easily discerned, for it produces a certain effect that I cannot describe, but which will be recognised by those who know it from personal experience. The soul seems to rise from play—for it sees that earthly things are but toys—and therefore mounts to higher things. Like one who retires into a strong fortress to be out of danger, it withdraws the <term>senses</term> <note>The physical inputs of sight, sound, and touch that usually distract the mind from spiritual focus.</note> from outward things, so thoroughly despising them that involuntarily the eyes close so as to veil from the sight what is visible, in order that the eyes of the soul may see more clearly. Those who practise this prayer almost always keep their eyes shut during it. This is an excellent custom for many reasons, because one thus forcibly prevents oneself from looking at earthly things. This restraint is only required when one first begins to practise this prayer: later on a strong effort would be needed to open the eyes. The soul appears to gather strength and to dominate itself at the expense of the body, which it leaves lonely and enfeebled, and thereby gains a stronger empire over it.

7. At first these signs are not apparent, as the recollection, which may be more or less, is not very great.

<summary>Teresa describes the physical and spiritual symptoms of deep prayer, such as the involuntary closing of the eyes and a sense of the soul withdrawing from the world into an internal "fortress." This inward focus allows the soul to gain control over the body and view worldly life as mere child's play.</summary>

<keywords>recollection, interior life, detachment, spiritual fortress, faculties of the soul, prayer, mystical experience</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 28 of St. Teresa’s "The Way of Perfection," which explains the "Prayer of Recollection." Following the discussion of why one should close their eyes and withdraw from the world, Teresa now describes the rewards of this discipline: a state where the soul’s internal "powers" eventually obey the will without effort.</meta>

-># THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAPTER XXVIII.]<-

In the beginning it requires a painful effort, for the body claims its rights, not understanding that its rebellion is <note>Teresa suggests that when the body resists spiritual discipline, it is "suicidal" because it is working against the soul's eternal life.</note> suicidal. Yet, by persevering in the habit for several days, and by controlling ourselves, the benefits that result will become clear. We shall find that when we begin to pray, the bees will return to the hive and enter it to make the honey, without any effort on our part, for our Lord is pleased to reward the soul and the will by this <term>empire</term> <note>A sense of mastery or sovereign control over one's internal distractions.</note> over the <term>powers</term> <note>In the theology of the time, the "powers" or "faculties" of the soul were the memory, the understanding, and the will.</note>, in return for the time spent in restraining them. Thus the mind only requires to make them a sign that it wishes to be <term>recollected</term> <note>Recollection refers to the act of "collecting" one's thoughts from the external world and focusing them entirely on the presence of God within.</note> and the <term>senses</term> <note>The physical inputs of sight, sound, and feeling that usually distract the mind.</note> will immediately obey it and retire within themselves. Although afterwards, they may wander again, still it is a great thing to have conquered them, for they go forth as captives and servants and cannot do the mischief they did before. When the will recalls them they return more quickly, until, after they have re-entered a number of times, our Lord is pleased that they should settle entirely in perfect <term>contemplation</term> <note>A deep state of prayer where the soul experiences God's presence directly, rather than thinking about Him through logic or images.</note>.

8. Pay great attention to what I have said, for though it may seem obscure, yet any one who practises it will understand it. Since we can thus make the journey by sea <note>Teresa often uses the metaphor of traveling by sea to represent a faster, more direct route to God through internal prayer, compared to the "land journey" of external works and discursive meditation.</note>, and it is imperative for us to lose no time over it, let us consider how to accustom ourselves to such a good habit. Souls are thereby delivered from many occasions of sin, and are more easily inflamed by the

This practice of recollection speeds the soul's journey toward God and protects it from the temptation to sin.</summary>

9. I want you to understand this prayer thoroughly: as I told you, it is called the <term>prayer of recollection</term> <note>A method of prayer where the practitioner "collects" their thoughts and senses from the outside world to focus entirely on the presence of God dwelling within.</note>.⁷ Let us imagine that we have within us a most splendid palace,⁸ built entirely of gold and precious stones—in short, a dwelling fit for so great a Lord—and that we are partly responsible for the condition of this building. There is no structure so beautiful as a soul filled with virtues, and the more perfect these <term>virtues</term> <note>Moral excellences or good habits, such as humility, patience, and charity, which Teresa views as the "jewels" of the soul.</note> are, the more brilliantly do the jewels shine.⁹ Within this palace dwells the mighty King who has deigned to become your Father, and who is seated on a throne of priceless value—by which I mean, your heart.

10. At first glance you may think that using such a <term>simile</term> <note>A comparison used as a teaching tool.</note> to explain this truth is far-fetched, yet it may prove very useful to you. Since we women are not <term>learned</term> <note>In the 16th century, women were generally barred from formal university education in theology. Teresa often uses this self-deprecating tone to appear humble to the authorities while teaching deep spiritual truths.</note>, we must make use of every means to understand clearly that we have within us an incomparably greater treasure than anything we can see in the world around us.

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXVIII. #

Let us not fancy that the centre of our soul is empty: God grant that none but women may overlook this fact <note>Teresa often uses self-deprecating language regarding her gender, a common strategy in the 16th century to avoid scrutiny from the Inquisition while teaching deep theology to her nuns.</note>. If we took care to remember what Guest we have within us, I think it would be impossible for us to give ourselves up so much to worldly vanities and cares, for we should see how vile they are in comparison with the riches within us. What more do the brutes <note>A term used for animals or irrational creatures.</note> do than satisfy their hunger by seizing on whatever takes their fancy? Yet how different should we be from them, seeing that we are children of a heavenly Father! 

Perhaps you will laugh at me and say that this is obvious enough. You may be right, yet I took a long time to realise it. Although I knew that I possessed a soul, yet I did not appreciate its value, nor remember Who dwelt within it, because I had blinded my eyes with the vanities of this life. I think that, had I understood then as I do now, that so great a King resided in the little palace of my soul,¹⁰ I should not have left Him alone so often, but should have stayed with Him sometimes and not have left His mansion so untidy.

<margin>¹⁰ “Remember that the Word, the Son of God, together with the Father and the Holy Spirit, is hidden in essence and in presence in the inmost being of the soul. <note>original: "in essentia et in praesentia"</note>. O thou soul, most beautiful of creatures, who so earnestly longest to know the place where thy Beloved is, that thou mayest seek Him and be united to Him! Thou art thyself that very <term>tabernacle</term> <note>Literally a "tent" or "dwelling place"; in a religious context, the container for the Eucharist or the presence of God.</note> where He dwells, the secret chamber of His retreat where He is hidden. As Christ does not force our will, He only takes what we give Him, but He does not give Himself entirely until He sees that we yield ourselves entirely to Him. Do not imagine that heaven is like this world, where, if a <term>prince</term> or <term>prelate</term> <note>A high-ranking church official, such as a bishop.</note> shows partiality for any one for some special reason, or out of friendship, other people at once feel jealous and abuse the poor man who has never injured them, so that the favours he receives cost him dear.

CHAPTER XXIX.] INDIFFERENCE.

# CHAPTER XXIX.<gloss>Valladolid edition, chapter 31; Escorial edition, continuation of chapter 48.</gloss>

->Continues the instruction on the <term>prayer of recollection</term> <note>A form of quiet meditation where the soul gathers its faculties inward to be with God.</note>.<-
->That it is of little consequence whether or no the<-
-><term>Prioress</term> <note>The nun in charge of the local convent.</note> likes us.<-

1. *We should be indifferent to the partiality of superiors. 2. Human favours incompatible with divine consolations. 3. How to become recollected. 4. We must realise God's presence. 5. How recollection aids our vocal prayers. 6. Perseverance needed to acquire it.*

1. FOR the love of God, sisters, do not wish to be the favourite of your <term>Superior</term> <note>The person in authority over the religious community.</note>. Let each one do her duty, and if the Prioress is not pleased with her, you may be sure our Lord will repay her and be satisfied with her. We did not come here to be rewarded in this life: let us keep our minds fixed on eternity and make no account of this world's matters, which do not even last our lifetime. To-day, another nun is the favourite—to-morrow, if she sees some greater virtue in you, the Superior will like you best—if not, it is of little consequence. Never give way to such thoughts, which sometimes rise from some trifling matter and may worry you a great deal. Check them at once by reflecting that your "kingdom is not of this world"<gloss>St. John 18:36: "My kingdom is not of this world." <note>original Latin: "Regnum meum non est de hoc mundo."</note></gloss> and that everything will come to an end, for there is nothing here that does not change.<gloss>Escorial edition, chapter 49. Continues the same subject. This chapter is very useful.</gloss>

<summary>Teresa begins Chapter 29 by advising her sisters to remain indifferent to the Prioress's favor, arguing that seeking human approval is a worldly distraction that fades with time. She emphasizes that the soul's true reward is eternal and that one must focus on pleasing God rather than earthly superiors.</summary>

2. O my God! If only we really knew Thee, all things would be indifferent to us, for Thou givest in abundance to all who truly trust in Thee. Believe me, my friends, the grasp of this truth helps us immensely to see the deception of all worldly favours that prevent the <term>soul</term> <note>The inner spiritual self that Teresa teaches must be entered through prayer.</note> from entering into itself. God have mercy on me! Who could make people understand this? Not I, for certain, for, although no one has better reason to say so than myself, yet I never realise it as I should do.

# [CHAP. XXIX.] GOD’S PRESENCE.

...how the <term>soul</term> <note>The inner spiritual self that Teresa teaches must be entered through prayer.</note> holds communion with this Companion, the <term>Holy of Holies</term> <note>A reference to the innermost sanctuary of the Temple in Jerusalem; here it signifies the deepest part of the soul where God dwells.</note>, with nothing to intrude on the solitude of itself and its <term>Spouse</term> <note>A common mystical metaphor where the soul is the "bride" and Christ is the "bridegroom."</note>, whenever it seeks to retire within itself with its God into this "heaven," shutting the door against all the world. I say "the soul seeks," because you must understand that this is not a <term>supernatural state</term> <note>Teresa distinguishes between "supernatural" prayer (gifts directly from God like ecstasy) and "acquired" prayer (states we can reach through our own effort and God's ordinary help).</note>, but something which, with the <term>grace of God</term> <note>The divine help and influence necessary for any spiritual progress.</note>, we can desire and obtain for ourselves. This "grace" is always implied whenever I say, in this book, that we are able to do anything, for without it we can do nothing—nothing—nor could we, by any strength of our own, think a single good thought.

<margin>See *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 1, chapter 1, paragraph 9.</margin>

4. This is not what is called <term>silence of the powers</term> <note>A high state of contemplation where the mental faculties (intellect, memory, and will) are suspended or stilled by God's action.</note>; it is a <term>recollection</term> <note>The "Prayer of Recollection," a method where one intentionally withdraws the senses from the outside world to focus on God within.</note> of the powers within the soul itself. There are several ways of acquiring the habit. Many books advise us to cast aside all other thoughts in order to approach God, Who dwells in our souls, and tell us, that even in the midst of our occupations, we should occasionally withdraw into ourselves, if only for a moment. It is very profitable for us to remember Who resides within our hearts. All I desire is, that we should realize to Whom our prayers are addressed, and should remain in His presence and not turn our back on Him, as we appear to do if, while we are speaking to God, we occupy our thoughts with a thousand <term>vanities</term> <note>Worthless or trivial worldly distractions.</note>. All this evil comes from our not really understanding that

<meta>This page continues Teresa’s instruction on the practice of internal prayer, moving from the definition of "recollection" to the practical necessity of recognizing God's immediate presence within the soul rather than searching for Him in the distance.</meta>
