# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXIX.]

God is near us, but we often imagine that He is far away—and how very far away, if we think we must go to heaven to find Him! And dost Thou not deserve a glance from us, O Lord, since Thou art so near us? Unless the person we are speaking to looks at us, we think he is not listening: shall we then close our eyes so that we cannot see whether Thou dost attend to us or not? How could we tell whether our words were heard?

5. I want to teach you that, in order to accustom ourselves to quiet our mind with facility, so that we may understand what we are saying and Whom we are addressing, we must withdraw our senses from outward things and keep them occupied within our souls. Then we shall possess heaven within us, since the <term>King of heaven</term> <note>Teresa’s characteristic way of describing God’s royal and sovereign presence residing in the "castle" of the human soul.</note> dwells there. Let us accustom ourselves to the fact that we need not call loudly to make God hear us: <term>His Majesty</term> <note>A respectful title Teresa habitually uses for God, mirroring the courtly etiquette of 16th-century Spain.</note> will make us feel that He is there. Thus we shall be able to recite, in great peace, such a prayer as the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"; the central prayer of the Christian tradition.</note> or any other that we select. We shall save ourselves a great deal of trouble, for God will aid us lest we grow weary. In return for the short time spent in forcing ourselves to keep near Him, He will make us understand, by certain signs, that He is listening.⁷ Thus, if we have to recite the **Our Father** several times, He will show us that He heard us sufficiently the first time.

***

<margin>⁷ Escorial edition, chapter 50. Explains the great advantages of this mode of prayer.</margin> <note>The "Escorial edition" refers to one of the original manuscripts of Teresa's work, which contains variations from other versions.</note>

## [CHAP. XXIX.] RECOLLECTION.

...will understand it the first time we said it, for He dearly loves to save us trouble. We need not repeat it more than once in a whole hour, if we only apprehend that we are in His presence and know for what we are asking Him, and believe that He is willing to grant it, like a tender Father Who loves to be with us and to enjoy our company. He does not want us to make our heads ache by much talking, and to those who do not know it He will teach this way of prayer. I beg of you to practise it, although at first you may find it difficult, as it is harder for those unaccustomed to it. I assure you, however, that before long you will find that you need not tire yourselves by seeking the holy Father to Whom you pray, since He resides in your soul. For my part, I own that I never knew what it was to pray in peace, until God taught me this way. The great benefits I have reaped from the habit of <term>interior recollection</term> <note>interior recollection: A deeper state of prayer where the soul treats itself as an inner temple, finding God dwelling in its very center.</note> have made me write about it here at such length. Perhaps all of you know this already: however, in the future some nun might not know; so you must not be annoyed at my having discussed it here.

When accustomed to it, she will benefit greatly, sooner or later; when once God has bestowed this grace on her, she would not exchange it for any earthly treasure. 

Nothing can be learnt without a certain amount of trouble. For the love of God, sisters, reckon your time well spent in acquiring this habit. I know that, with His help, if you practise it for a year, or perhaps for only six months, you will gain it. Think what a short time that is for so great an advantage as laying this firm foundation, so that if our Lord wishes to raise you to a high <term>degree of prayer</term> <note>degree of prayer: Teresa views prayer as a journey with different stages, from simple vocal prayer to "contemplation" and eventually "union" with God.</note>, He will find you prepared for it, since you keep close to Him. ## CHAPTER XXX. <note>In the Valladolid edition, this is Chapter XXXII; in the Escorial manuscript, it is Chapter LI.</note>

Of what the happiness of the heavenly kingdom consists. 5. This kingdom is in our souls. 6. The <term>prayer of quiet</term> <note>prayer of quiet: A state of contemplative prayer where the soul experiences a deep sense of peace and the presence of God, with the will being "captured" by divine love while the other faculties (like reason) may still be active.</note>. 7. further words seem useless. O eternal Wisdom! This alone would have sufficed between Thee and Thy Father. Thus didst Thou address Him in the garden <note>A reference to the Garden of Gethsemane, where Jesus prayed before His arrest.</note>: Thou didst show Him Thy will and Thy dread, and didst submit Thyself to Him. But Thou knowest, O my God, that we are not as resigned as Thou wert to the will of Thy Father—there was need to name each thing we pray for, that we might decide whether it was what we wanted; if not, we would not ask it of Thee. Having free will, we should not receive God's gift, unless we had first chosen it, although it might be best for us—for we never think we are rich unless we see the money in our hands.

2. Alas, O God! what is it that paralyses our faith so that we cannot see how inevitably we shall some day be either punished or rewarded? This is, daughters, why you ought to understand what you beg for in the <term>Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note></term>, so that if God bestows it, you may not cast it back at Him. Always think first carefully over what you ask, and whether it would be well for it to be granted. If not, do not make the petition, but implore <term>His Majesty <note>Teresa’s characteristic way of referring to God, emphasizing His royal dignity and power.</note></term> to give you light, for we are both blind and fastidious: we do not relish the food that nourishes us, but prefer that which causes death—and what a death—full of horror, and lasting to eternity.<note>Escorial edition, ch. lii. Comments on the words: “Hallowed be Thy name; Thy kingdom come.” Commences the explanation of the <term>prayer of quiet <note>A stage of contemplative prayer where the soul experiences a deep, peaceful rest in the divine presence, and the will is absorbed in God’s love.</note></term>.</note>

<summary>Teresa explains that humans specify their requests in prayer because, unlike Christ in Gethsemane, they lack perfect resignation to God's will. She warns that we must understand the petitions of the Our Father to avoid asking for things that lead to spiritual death.</summary>

->### CHAP. XXX.] " THY KINGDOM COME. "<-

3. The good Jesus bids us say these words, which ask that this kingdom may come in us—" <term>Hallowed be Thy name <note>original: "Sanctificetur nomen tuum". This is the first petition of the Lord’s Prayer.</note></term>; <term>Thy kingdom come <note>original: "Adveniat regnum tuum". Teresa interprets this kingdom not as a distant place, but as a state of soul.</note></term>. " How great is the wisdom of our <term>Master <note>Refers to Christ as the ultimate teacher of the soul.</note></term> and our <term>Spouse <note>A key concept in Teresian spirituality where the soul is "married" to Christ in a deep, mystical union.</note></term>! It is well that we should all learn what we ask for here, when praying for this kingdom. <term>His Majesty <note>Teresa’s preferred way of referring to God, highlighting His royal dignity and power.</note></term> knew that, unless He enabled us to do so by giving us His kingdom here on earth, our natural defects would render us unfit either to hallow, praise, magnify, glorify, or extol the holy name of the eternal Father. The good Jesus therefore placed the two petitions close together. I will tell you what I understand about the matter, that you may realise what you are praying for, how eager we should be to gain it, and how we should strive to please Him Who can give it us. If this subject does not please you, meditate on some other: God permits you to do so, as long as you submit in all things to the <term>teaching of the Church <note>Teresa was always careful to emphasize her orthodoxy to avoid conflict with the Spanish Inquisition.</note></term>, as I always do myself. I will not give you this book until it has been read by <term>competent judges <note>She refers to her confessors and theologians who censored her work to ensure it contained no heresy.</note></term>: if it contains errors, they come from ignorance, and not from malice.

4. It lives in perfect peace and feels supreme satisfaction in seeing that all those around it honour and praise

### “THY KINGDOM COME.”

1. **NEVERTHELESS**, my daughters, I will try to tell you about the <term>prayer of quiet <note>A state of mystical prayer where the soul experiences an intense inner peace and a sense of being in God's presence, though the mind may still be aware of its surroundings.</note></term>, as I have heard it explained or as our Lord has been pleased to make me understand it—perhaps in order that I might teach you. ->CHAP. XXXI.]  PRAYER OF QUIET.  197<-

<margin>² See *The Book of Her Life*, chapters 14 and 15; *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 4; *The Interior Castle*, Mansion IV, chapter 1, sections 4 and 5; chapter 2, sections 3–8; chapter 3, section 7.</margin>
...said, I have written elsewhere about the <term>prayer of quiet <note>A state of deep contemplation where the soul experiences a profound sense of peace and God's presence, requiring no mental effort to sustain.</note></term>,² so I will do little but touch on the subject here. This prayer is a <term>supernatural state <note>A spiritual experience that is given as a gift from God and cannot be achieved through human effort or techniques alone.</note></term> to which no effort of our own can raise us, because here the soul rests in peace—or rather, our Lord gives it peace by His presence, as He did to the righteous man, Simeon <note>A devout man mentioned in the Gospel of Luke who was promised he would not die until he had seen the Messiah; he recognized the infant Jesus in the Temple.</note>. Thus all the <term>faculties <note>The powers of the soul: the memory, the understanding (reason), and the will.</note></term> are calmed, and in some manner, in no way connected with the exterior senses, the spirit realizes that it is close to its God, and that if it drew but a little nearer to Him, it would become one with Him by <term>union <note>The highest state of prayer where the soul's will is completely merged with the will of God.</note></term>. This is not because such a person sees Him, either with the <term>corporal <note>Physical sight using the eyes.</note></term> or spiritual sight. Thus the holy Simeon, while bearing in his arms the glorious Infant, poor and wrapped in swaddling-clothes and followed by a procession of only two attendants, might more easily have taken Christ for the child of <term>indigent <note>Very poor or needy.</note></term> parents than for the Son of the heavenly Father. But the Babe Himself gave the old man light to recognize Him, as He enlightens the soul to recognize Him during the prayer of quiet. It cannot tell how it knows Him, yet it feels sure it is in that “kingdom,” or at least, near the King from Whom the kingdom is to come. So reverential is the awe felt by such a soul that it dares ask nothing of God.

2. This state resembles a <term>swoon <note>A fainting-like state of spiritual ecstasy where the physical senses become dulled as the soul focuses entirely on God.</note></term>, both exterior and interior, so that the exterior man (or as I will call it,

She describes this state as a supernatural "swoon" where the soul feels the immediate but invisible presence of the Divine King.</summary>

"the body" (I say this lest <note>Saint Teresa often uses self-deprecating or blunt language to ensure her sisters, who may not have had formal theological training, understand her metaphors.</note> some simpleton among you may say she does not know what "outer" and "inner" mean) does not wish to move, but rests like a traveler who, having nearly come to the journey's end, stops so that they may start again refreshed; for the strength of the soul is now double what it was. The body feels a sense of enjoyment while the spirit is supremely satisfied and so delighted at finding itself near the fountain <note>A recurring image in Teresa's writing, representing God as the source of "living water" that satisfies the soul’s deepest needs.</note> that, even before tasting the water, its thirst is quenched, and there seems to be nothing left to desire. Two of them are free; the <term>will <note>The part of the soul that chooses and loves. In this state, it is "captured" or fully occupied by the love of God.</note></term> alone is captive, and, if it were capable of feeling pain at this time, it could only do so at the thought that it might regain its liberty. The mind centers itself on one thing only and works very little, and the memory tries to remember nothing else, for both see that this is "the one thing needful" <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 10:42, where Jesus tells Martha that her sister Mary, who is sitting quietly at His feet, has chosen "the one thing necessary."</note> and that anything else disturbs them. At such a time, people wish the body to remain motionless; they think its movement would destroy their repose, therefore they dare not stir. Speaking troubles them: they may spend an hour in saying one <term>Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster" (Latin)</note></term>. Being very close to God, they know that He understands them by signs. They are in the palace, and near their King, and they...

# CHAPTER XXXI. DISTRACTIONS.

...cause the dawn to break, nor can we stop night from coming on. This prayer is no work of ours: it is supernatural and utterly beyond our control. The surest way to prolong it is to recognize that we can neither diminish nor add to it; and, unworthy as we are, we can only receive this grace with thanksgiving. We do this not by daring to utter many words, but, like the **publican** <note>In the Gospel of Luke, the publican (a tax collector) is the model of humble prayer, standing at a distance and barely raising his eyes to heaven.</note>, by simply raising our eyes.

<margin>⁶ The Interior Castle, Mansion 4, chapter 1, sections 8-13. The Life, chapter 15, section 10; chapter 30, section 19.</margin>
6. It is well to seek solitude, so as to give place to our Lord and to allow Him to do His work. We may occasionally make a gentle <term>aspiration <note>A short, spontaneous prayer or "sigh" of the soul toward God, intended to keep the heart focused without intense mental effort.</note></term>, just as one blows on a candle that is going out in order to rekindle it; though, if it had been burning brightly, our breath would only have extinguished it. I think we should ignite it gently, for by straining our minds to compose long sentences, the **will** might be disturbed. 

Pay great attention to the following piece of advice. You will often find that you cannot help yourselves with either of the other <term>powers <note>Teresa refers to the three faculties of the soul: the memory, the understanding (intellect), and the will.</note></term>: while the soul is immersed in peace, the imagination is so distracted that it seems to take no part in what is passing in the house, but to be merely lodging in it as a stranger. Being unhappy at home, it looks for some other dwelling, not knowing how to enjoy this repose. Perhaps this only happens in my case, and other people do not suffer in the same way.⁶ Sometimes—I am only speaking of myself—I long to die, so hopeless is it to attempt to control my thoughts. At other times,

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXI.<-

the mind seems settled at home and stays with the **will**. When the three <term>faculties <note>According to St. Teresa, the soul consists of three "powers" or faculties: the memory, the understanding (intellect), and the will. In deep prayer, these three ideally work in harmony.</note></term> concur, this state resembles heaven. It may be compared to a loving married couple who are happy together and who both wish for the same things, but a bad husband makes his wife very wretched.

7. Mark well what I say, for it is most important. When the **will** enjoys this <term>quiet <note>The "Prayer of Quiet" is a specific state of contemplation where the soul experiences a deep, peaceful, and supernatural sense of God's presence.</note></term>, it should take no more notice of the <term>understanding <note>The faculty of the soul that reasons, analyzes, and often wanders. Teresa suggests that trying to "think" or "reason" during this peace actually disrupts the experience.</note></term> than it would of an idiot. If it tries to force the **imagination** to keep it company, it will perforce be preoccupied and disturbed, and in a state of painful struggle: thus, instead of profiting, the soul will lose what God was giving it without its having made any effort. Think well over the comparison which I am about to make—our Lord suggested it to me in this very state of prayer and it explains my meaning very clearly. The soul is here like a babe at the breast of its mother, who, to please it, feeds it without its moving its lips. <margin>⁷ See Teresa's other works: *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 4, section 6; and *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 4, chapter 3, section 9.</margin> Thus it is now, for the soul loves without using the **understanding**. Our Lord wishes it to realise, without reasoning about the matter, that it is in His company. He desires that it should drink the milk He gives, and enjoy its sweetness, while acknowledging that it is receiving a <term>divine favour <note>In this context, a "favour" is a specific spiritual gift or grace granted by God during prayer that the person did not earn through their own work.</note></term>, and that it should delight in its own happiness. He does not require the soul to know *how* it enjoys this, nor *what* it is enjoying, but to forget itself. He Who is beside it will care for its highest interests.

<summary>St. Teresa advises that during the Prayer of Quiet, the soul should remain still and ignore the distractions of the intellect. She uses the metaphor of a nursing child to illustrate how God nourishes the soul with grace without the soul needing to exert effort.</summary>
<keywords>Prayer of Quiet, the Will, the Understanding, the Imagination, nursing infant metaphor, divine favor, contemplative prayer, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

--- Page 245 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 31 of St. Teresa’s "The Way of Perfection," moving from the analogy of a nursing infant to the technical distinctions between different states of mystical prayer, specifically comparing "Quiet" and "Union."</meta>

->### CHAP. XXXI. DIFFERENCES. 203<-

Any effort made to constrain the mind to take part in what is passing will result in failure and the soul will be forced to lose the milk—that is, the <term>divine nourishment <note>A metaphor for the spiritual grace or "food" God provides directly to the soul during deep prayer.</note></term>.

8. The <term>prayer of quiet <note>A state of contemplative prayer where the soul experiences a deep, peaceful awareness of God's presence, though the mind may still wander slightly.</note></term> differs from that of <term>union <note>A more advanced state of prayer where the soul is fully absorbed in God, and the internal faculties—memory, reason, and will—are entirely captured by the divine experience.</note></term>,<gloss>This refers to St. Teresa's autobiography, "The Life of St. Teresa of Jesus," chapter 17, sections 5 and 6.</gloss> in which the spirit is entirely united to God. In the latter, the soul does not even swallow the nourishment, which, without its knowledge, God Himself places within it. During the former prayer, He appears to wish the soul to work a little, although with so much ease as hardly to be conscious of any labour. My meaning will be clear to any one who has enjoyed this degree of prayer, if she reads this attentively: what I say is important, although to others it may seem only jargon <note>Teresa often used the word "jargon" or "gibberish" to describe her attempts to explain complex spiritual experiences in everyday language.</note>. In this state the mind disturbs the soul, which is not the case when there is union of the <term>three faculties <note>According to 16th-century psychology, the soul functioned through three "powers": the Memory, the Understanding (intellect), and the Will. In "Union," all three are suspended or occupied by God.</note></term>. Their Creator then suspends them, the delight He bestows on them keeping them occupied without their being able to understand why. Therefore during this prayer of union, which is a peaceful and supreme content of the **will**, together with a feeling of repose, the soul cannot decide with certainty what it enjoys, although recognising the immense difference between this and all earthly pleasures. To possess the whole world, with all its delights, would not bring such happiness to the <term>interior of the will</term>, for, as it appears to me, all the joys of this life only reach the exterior of the will, or its rind, as we may call it.

<summary>St. Teresa distinguishes the "Prayer of Quiet" from the "Prayer of Union," explaining that while the former requires some slight mental effort, the latter is a total, effortless absorption of the soul's faculties in God.</summary>
<keywords>prayer of quiet, prayer of union, three faculties, divine nourishment, will, St. Teresa of Avila, mysticism, contemplation</keywords>

9. When any of you find yourselves in the high state of the **prayer of quiet**—which, as I said, is manifestly **supernatural** <note>In Teresa's context, "supernatural" refers to a gift or experience that comes directly from God and cannot be achieved by human effort alone.</note>—if the mind, or to speak more clearly, the **imagination**, wanders about after the greatest nonsense in the world, laugh at it, treat it as a lunatic and maintain your own peace.⁹ Thoughts will come and go, but here the **will** is mistress and recalls them without your troubling yourselves in the matter. If you try to control them by force you will lose your power over them, which comes from the <term>divine nourishment <note>A metaphor for the spiritual grace God provides directly to the soul, often compared to an infant being fed.</note></term> within you, and neither the one nor the other will gain, but both will be losers. As the proverb says : “Grasp at too much and you will catch nothing,” and this seems the case here. Experience will bring my meaning home to you ; without it, what I have told you may well seem superfluous and obscure. However, a very little acquaintance with it will make my words clear ; they may help your souls, and you will thank God for having enabled me to explain the matter. We need a great deal of experience to understand this until we have read about it : afterwards we easily comprehend it. To conclude with, it may be affirmed that when the soul

10. O holy prayer! wherein, unknowingly, we crave so great a good! What a blessed petition to make! This, sisters, is the reason I want us to say the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> and other prayers with care, and to think about what we ask for in them. Most certainly, when once God has shown us this favour, we ought to forget all worldly things, for the Lord of the whole earth has come and cast them forth. I do not mean that all who have enjoyed the <term>prayer of quiet <note>A state of deep, contemplative prayer where the soul experiences an intense inner peace and the presence of God, even if the mind or imagination remains somewhat active.</note></term> must necessarily be detached from everything in this world; but I wish them to know what they ought to be, and to try to <term>mortify <note>The spiritual practice of "dying to oneself"—disciplining one's own will and worldly desires to be more open to God's will.</note></term> themselves in every way, otherwise they will stop here. They should not ask for so precious a gift as if it were worthless, and if God grants it them, let them not throw it back at Him.

11. God shows, by bestowing these pledges on the soul, that He designs it for great things. The fault will be its own if it does not make great progress. However, if He sees that, after He has set within it the kingdom of heaven, it returns to this world, not only will He desist from revealing to it the mysteries of His kingdom, but He will only show it the former favour at rare intervals and for a short time. I may be mistaken, but I have both seen and known that this occurs. I believe that the reason why so many persons fail to become thoroughly spiritual <gloss>See St. Teresa's other work, *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 4, chapter 2, paragraph 7; Mansion 5, chapter 2, paragraphs 4, 5; and chapter 4, paragraphs 2, 9.</gloss>

...is that they do not worthily respond, by their actions, to this signal <term>grace <note>A free gift from God; in this context, the supernatural assistance or favor of spiritual peace.</note></term> by preparing themselves to receive it again. They withdraw from our Lord's hands their will, which He considered His property: as they centre their affections on base things, He seeks other souls whose love for Him is so fervent that He can grant them even more sublime favours. Still, He does not altogether deprive the former persons of what He gave them, provided they keep a good conscience.

12. There are many souls (and I was among their number), whom God moves to devotion and visits with holy inspirations and light <gloss>11</gloss> to know the worthlessness of all earthly things, and on whom He finally bestows His kingdom in this <term>prayer of quiet <note>A state of deep, contemplative prayer where the soul feels a profound inner peace and the presence of God, quieting the soul's own efforts to focus on Him.</note></term>. Yet these souls close their ears against Him because they prefer to speak and to hurry through a number of <term>vocal prayers <note>Prayers that are recited using specific words, either aloud or mentally, such as the Rosary or other standard litanies.</note></term> as if a task had been set them to say a certain amount every day. Thus, when our Lord puts His kingdom into their possession, by means of the prayer of quiet and interior peace, they will not accept it, but think they can do better by reciting prayers which distract their attention. Do not imitate them, my sisters, but be attentive when God gives you this grace; think what a priceless treasure you would lose, and be assured that you had far better say one petition of the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> from time to time than repeat the whole prayer mechanically and hurriedly.

## CHAPTER XXXII.<gloss>1 <note>This is Chapter 34 in the Valladolid edition and Chapter 54 in the Escorial edition.</note></gloss>

->Explains the words of the <term>Our Father <note>original Latin: "Pater Noster"</note></term>: “Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven.” How much is effected by uttering these words with attention, and how richly God rewards us for it.<-

*1. “Thy will be done.” God requires us to give Him our will in return for all He has given us. 2. His grace enables us to do so. 3. To shrink from crosses on account of our weakness is false humility. 4. An act of resignation. 5. The vow of obedience. 6. The will of God means suffering for us. 7. The vow of obedience is the jewel that nuns offer Him. 8. An offering of the will. 10. God’s kingdom given us in return for the gift of our own will. 11. God gives us His will. 12. Humility the path to contemplation.*

### “THY WILL BE DONE ON EARTH AS IT IS IN HEAVEN.”

1. So great is the gift that our good Master has asked for us and has taught us to beg for ourselves, that it

...includes all we can desire in this life.

1. He has done us the immense favour of making us His brethren: let us now learn what Christ offers God on our behalf, and what He wishes us to give His Father in return. We must first see what our Lord requests of us, for it is only right that we should do Him some service in acknowledgment of such supreme blessings. O good Jesus! whilst demanding so much for us, how little dost Thou give in return—how little, I mean, on our part—for it is as nothing compared with the debt we owe this mighty <term>Monarch <note>Teresa frequently uses the term "His Majesty" (Su Majestad) or "Monarch" to describe God, blending a sense of His absolute sovereignty with the intimate courtesy of a royal court.</note></term>. And yet, my Lord, Thou hast not left us without means of repaying Him, for we give all we can, if, when we say the words, “I wish that, as Thy will is done in heaven, so it may be done on earth,” we yield Him our wills.²

2. Thou hast done well, O our good Master, in making this petition come last, so that we may be able to accomplish what Thou dost promise for us here. For truly, O Lord, hadst Thou not done so, our task would have seemed hopeless; yet, since Thy Father bestows His kingdom on us at Thy prayer, I know that we can fulfil Thy promise by giving what Thou didst offer in our name. For since my “earth” <note>Teresa often uses "earth" to refer to our physical nature or the soul's current state of exile, and "heaven" to refer to the soul in a state of grace or union with God.</note> is now made “heaven,” it is possible for Thy will to be done in me; otherwise, in “earth” so barren and so wretched, I know not how it could have come to pass. For Thou askest so great a thing.

### CHAP. XXXII.] ASKING FOR CROSSES.
<sig>I4</sig>

3. I wish you, daughters, to realise its importance. I am amused at the thought of people fearing to ask for <term>crosses <note>In mystical literature, "crosses" refers to the trials, illnesses, or spiritual difficulties a person undergoes to grow closer to God, mirroring the suffering of Jesus on the Cross.</note></term> from God. Some say it would be a want of <term>humility <note>Teresa often defines humility as "walking in truth"—knowing exactly who we are in relation to God. Here, she addresses the idea that asking for suffering might be seen as arrogant or overestimating one's strength.</note></term> to pray for crosses. I have met with other people who, without even this pretext, have not courage to beg for the sufferings they think would be sent at once. Persons who refrain, out of humility, from demanding them believe that they would not be able to bear such trials. For my part, I think that He Who gives the love that longs for such a hard way of proving its sincerity would also give love enough to suffer. I would ask souls who will not sue for the crosses they fancy would be sent them immediately, whether they know what they are asking for when they beg that the will of God may be done in them? Do they simply repeat the words in imitation of other people? This, my daughters, would be exceedingly wrong. The good Jesus is here our <term>Ambassador <note>Teresa uses this diplomatic term to describe Jesus as the mediator who negotiates the "treaty" between the human soul and God the Father.</note></term>, Who, at no small cost to Himself, seeks to mediate for us with His Father, and it would be unfair for us to refuse to give what He pledges on our behalf—it would be better that we should never proffer it. I will put the case in a different way. Inevitably, the will of God must be done—whether we wish it or no, it will prevail both in heaven and earth. Then take my advice; trust what I say, and make a <term>virtue of necessity <note>This phrase suggests that since God's will is ultimate and unavoidable, it is better to accept it willingly and joyfully than to submit to it with resentment.</note></term>.

4. O my God, well is it for me that Thou didst not leave such a wretch as myself at liberty to fulfil or to

These are His gifts in this world, and He grants them in proportion to His affection for us. To souls He cherishes most He gives more—and fewer to those less dear to Him, according to their courage and the love He sees they bear Him. For fervent love can suffer much for Him, while <term>tepidity <note>A state of spiritual lukewarmness or a lack of enthusiasm for one's faith.</note></term> will endure but little. For my part, I believe that our love is the measure of the cross we can bear.⁷

->CHAP. XXXII.] VOW OF OBEDIENCE.<-

way of yielding Him our will is like offering some one a jewel, begging him to accept it, and holding it fast when he puts out his hand to take it. It is shameful to trifle thus with One Who has done so much for us. Were there no other reason, it would be wrong to mock Him thus, again and again, whenever we repeat the <term>Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note></term>. Let us give Him, once for all, the gem we have so often proffered Him—although He gave us, first, what we are now tendering to His Father. Ah, how well does Jesus understand us! He does not surrender our will to God in our name, until we have already been amply repaid for this trivial service. This shows us what great benefits it will obtain for us from His Father, Who begins to recompense us for it in this life, as I will explain to you later on. People who live in the world do much if they sincerely resolve to submit their will to God, but *you*, daughters, must both say and act, must both vow and fulfil your vows, as, indeed, <term>religious <note>Those who have taken formal vows in a religious order, such as the sisters of Teresa's reformed Carmelite convents.</note></term> may truly be said to do.⁸ Yet

My whole aim in writing this book has been to incite us to yield ourselves entirely to our <term>Creator <note>God, as the one who brought all things into being.</note></term>,⁹ to submit our will to His, and to detach ourselves from all created things. As you already understand how important this is, I will say no more on the subject, but will explain to you why our good Master makes us say this petition <note>The phrase "Thy will be done" in the Lord's Prayer.</note>. He well knows how we shall benefit by accomplishing the promise made to His eternal Father. In a very short time, we shall find ourselves at the end of our journey and shall drink of the <term>fountain of living water <note>A biblical and mystical image representing the Holy Spirit and the deep satisfaction found in God's presence.</note></term> of which I spoke.¹⁰ But unless we resign and conform our will entirely to the <term>Divine will <note>The purpose and desire of God for humanity.</note></term>, we shall never obtain that water. This is the <term>perfect contemplation <note>A form of prayer where the soul is gathered into God, experiencing His presence without the need for active thought or many words.</note></term> that you wished me to write about. Here, as I have shown you, we can do nothing on our part. Here we neither work nor plan for ourselves, nor is it necessary, for everything,

9. In every way and in every matter, do **Thy will** in me, O Lord, as Thou pleasest. If Thou desirest to give me **crosses** <term>crosses <note>In Christian spiritual writing, "crosses" are a metaphor for the hardships, sufferings, or burdens a person must carry, modeled after the suffering of Jesus.</note></term>, grant me strength, and let them come: if Thou wouldst send me persecutions, shame, poverty, illness—I stand ready, nor will I turn away from them, O my **Father**! Made with full determination, it is able to draw the **Almighty** to become one with our baseness and to transform us into Himself, thus uniting the creature with its **Creator**. Are you not well repaid? See how good your **Master** is! 11. While we do not know for what more we could ask, **His Majesty** <note>Teresa consistently uses the title "His Majesty" to refer to God, emphasizing His sovereignty and royal dignity.</note> never wearies of giving us fresh favours. Not contented with having united such a soul to Himself, He begins to caress it, and reveals His secrets to it. He is pleased at its understanding what it has gained, and that it knows something of what He has in store for it. He deprives such a person of her **exterior senses** <note>The physical senses—sight, hearing, touch, etc.—which Teresa believes must be "stilled" or suspended for the soul to focus entirely on the divine presence.</note>,$^{12}$ lest they should disturb her. This produces what is called "**rapture**." <term>rapture <note>Also known as ecstasy; a state where a person is so overwhelmed by the presence of God that they lose awareness of their physical surroundings.</note></term> His friendship with her becomes so intimate, that not only does He restore her will to her, but He gives her His own as well. For, having made a close friend of her, God is pleased to take the command with her “by turns,” as we may say, and, just as she obeys His commands, so He, in return, does what she asks of Him,$^{13}$ only in a far more complete manner, for, being **almighty**, He can do what He wills and He always wills to do this, while the poor soul cannot carry out all His wishes, however strong its desire may be. Neither has it power to do anything, unless the **grace** <term>grace <note>The free and undeserved help given by God to help a person lead a holy life.</note></term> is first given it, and yet it grows richer, although the more it serves God the heavier grows its **debt**. It often becomes weary of being subject to so many...

[CHAP. XXXII.] HUMILITY. 217
<page-num>217</page-num>

drawbacks, obstacles, and bonds while imprisoned in the flesh <note>A common metaphor in spiritual writing referring to the soul being limited by the physical body and worldly desires.</note>, for it longs to pay God something of what it owes Him. 12. All else only hinders the soul that He has raised to this state: humility alone can help us here, and that not a humility won by means of our intellect, but one gained by a pure **intuition** <note>In this context, intuition refers to a direct, spiritual "seeing" or understanding granted by God, rather than a conclusion reached through thinking or logic.</note> of the truth, by which we perceive, in an instant, our own nothingness and the greatness of God with greater clearness than we could have learnt in many years by the use of our reason. But as I have already explained in another book <note>A reference to Teresa’s autobiography, *The Life of St. Teresa of Avila*, specifically chapters 18 and following, where she describes the higher stages of prayer.</note> what **contemplation** <note>A form of wordless, quiet prayer where the soul focuses entirely on God’s presence.</note> is and how the soul should conduct itself in that state, and have described in detail the spirit's experiences and the knowledge it gains of the Divinity, I will only allude to it here, so that you may learn how to recite the <term>Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster." This refers to the Lord's Prayer, which forms the structural basis for Teresa's instructions in this book.</note></term>. One piece of advice I will give you, however—do not fancy that any efforts or actions of your own will raise you to contemplation, for you would be mistaken; they would only cool any devotion you already felt—but with the simplicity and humility which obtain all things, you must simply say: “Thy will be done.”

## CHAPTER XXXIII. <note>This chapter corresponds to chapter 35 in the Valladolid edition and chapter 57 in the Escorial manuscript.</note>

### How needful it is for us that God should grant our demand in the <term>Our Father <note>original: "Pater Noster," the Latin name for the prayer taught by Jesus.</note></term>: "Give us this day our daily bread."

1. "Give us this day our daily bread." Why this "Bread" is given us. 2. Without It we could not do God's will. 3. Our Lord asked It for us. 4. Reasons why Christ remains in the <term>Blessed Sacrament <note>In Catholic theology, the bread and wine after they have been consecrated, believed to be the actual presence of Christ.</note></term>. 5. Address to God the Father respecting It. 6. The Saint <note>Teresa refers to herself here in the third person or is being described by the original editor.</note> beseeches Him to protect the Blessed Sacrament from insults. 7. Humility shown by Jesus in this petition. 8. Appeal to the Father.

->### "GIVE US THIS DAY OUR DAILY BREAD."<-

1. OUR good Jesus understood how difficult a thing He had promised on our behalf, for we are frail by nature, and often succeed in persuading ourselves that we do not know what is the will of God. We are weak and He is merciful; thus He saw that some remedy was needed, for by no means ought we to desist from giving what He offered for us, since in this consists our highest good, although it is a most difficult task for us to fulfil. For instance, if a rich man is told that he ought to moderate his table, so that those who are dying of hunger may have bread to eat, he will find a thousand excuses for not understanding this better than he chooses. If you say to a scandal-monger that he is bound to love his neighbour as himself, he will lose all patience, and nothing will convince him of the truth.

2. Declare to a <term>religious <note>A person who has taken vows in a religious order, such as a monk or nun.</note></term>, who is accustomed to liberty

and self-indulgence, that he ought to give a good example; that when he says, “Thy will be done,” <note>original: "Fiat voluntas tua"</note> it is his duty to observe these words, not only by tongue but by deed; that he has sworn and promised to do the will of God, and God’s will is that he should perform his vows: represent to such a person that if he gives scandal, although he may not absolutely break his vows, yet he infringes on them greatly—that he has taken a <term>vow of poverty <note>A solemn promise made by members of religious orders to renounce personal ownership and live simply, sharing all goods in common.</note></term> which he must in no way evade, for this is the will of God—yet you will never be able to bring such a man even to wish to do what is right. ### THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXIII.

->page 220<-

4. Consider as having very little value whatever you may have given to God when compared with this rich reward. It appears to me—although I submit my opinion to a higher judgment <note>Teresa frequently includes these disclaimers to show her submission to the Catholic Church's authority, a common practice for writers during the Spanish Inquisition.</note>—that even though the good Jesus knew what a great benefit it would be for us to surrender to His Father what He had offered on our behalf, He also recognized the obstacles that prevent us from keeping our promises. These obstacles come from our human nature, our tendency toward failure <note>original: "degradation"</note>, and our lack of love and courage. He saw that we needed help and encouragement, not just once, but day by day; therefore, He determined to remain among us.

As this was an immense <term>grace <note>A gift of divine help or favor that is unearned and freely given by God.</note></term>, He wished it to come from the hand of His eternal Father. Even though the Father and the Son are One, and He knew that whatever He did on earth God would confirm and ratify in heaven—since His will and His Father's are the same—such is the humility of the good Jesus in His human form that He appeared to ask permission for this favor. He did this even though He realized how much His Father loved and delighted in Him. Our Lord understood that we are asking for far more in this <term>petition <note>A "petition" refers to one of the specific requests found within the Lord's Prayer (the "Our Father").</note></term> than in the others, because He foresaw the death that men would inflict upon Him, and the shame and insults He would suffer.

5. O my God! what father could be found who, after giving us his son—and such a son!—would allow him to remain among us after we had treated Him so poorly?

How is it that He is mute, and knows not how to speak for Himself, but only pleads for *us*? Shall no one intercede for this most meek and loving Lamb? # THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXIII.

## APPEAL TO GOD.

He puts us in the first place when appealing to His Father, as much as to say that now, having once for all given us this gift <note>The gift of Himself in the Eucharist.</note>, it is our own, and He will not take it away from us until the end of the world, but will leave it for our <term>succour</term> <note>From the Latin *succurrere*, meaning to run to the aid of; help or support given in a difficult situation.</note> every day. Let this win your hearts, my daughters, to love your <term>Spouse</term> <note>In Teresa's mystical theology, the soul's relationship with Christ is described as a "Spiritual Marriage," where Jesus is the groom.</note>, for no slave in the world would willingly call Himself by this name, yet the good Jesus seems to take it for an honour.

8. O eternal Father, how unspeakable is this humility! What treasure will suffice to purchase Your Son for us? How to sell Him we know—that was done for <term>thirty pieces of silver</term> <note>The sum paid to Judas Iscariot for betraying Jesus to the authorities.</note>, but no riches will enable us to buy Him. Being made one with us by that portion of His nature which He had assumed <note>Referring to the human nature Christ took on during the Incarnation.</note>, and being Master of His own will, He reminds His Father that, since His manhood is His own, He has the right to bestow it upon us. Therefore He says: “**Our bread**” <note>original: "Panis noster"</note>; making no distinction between Himself and us, but ranking us with Himself, so that, as He daily joins His prayer with ours, we may obtain from God that for which we ask.

<summary>Teresa reflects on Christ’s profound humility in calling himself "our bread," thereby sharing his divine inheritance and his very self with humanity. She contrasts the infinite value of Christ with the paltry price of his betrayal, emphasizing his role as our advocate and "Spouse."</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXIV.]

## CHAPTER XXXIV. <note>In the Valladolid edition, this is chapter XXXVI; in the Escorial manuscript, it is chapter LX.</note>

->Continuation of the same subject. Contains very useful advice<-
->for those who have received Holy Communion. An<-
->explanation of the word "daily."<-

*1. What "daily bread" means. 2. What "this day" signifies. 3. The "bread" of bodily sustenance, 4. For which we must trust to God, 5. As the servant trusts his master for maintenance ; 6. And must only ask Him for the "heavenly" bread. 7. Christ our food and medicine. 8. St. Teresa's communions. 9. Why Christ remains hidden in the Holy Eucharist. 10. The time after Holy Communion. 11. Holy Communion.*

1. The good Jesus, having resolved to give Himself to us, asks His Father to allow Him to remain with us "**daily**" <note>Original Latin: *quotidianum*. Teresa is reflecting on the phrase "Give us this day our daily bread" from the Lord's Prayer.</note>, which appears to mean "for ever." Yet, while writing this, I have been wondering why, having said "**daily**," He should add, "**This day**" <note>Original Latin: *hodie*.</note>. I tell you of my foolish thoughts, so that if they really are absurd, you may see what a simpleton I am—as indeed I must be, to dare to discuss such matters. Yet, as we are to think over what we are praying for, let us consider what this <term>petition</term> <note>A formal request or prayer made to God.</note> means, so that we may fulfil its obligations reasonably, and may thank our Lord for taking so much trouble to teach us. I believe that "**daily**" means that we may enjoy His presence while we dwell in this world, where He remains with us and we receive Him as our Food, and in heaven also, if we profit by His company here. His sole object in abiding with us is to aid, to incite, to strengthen us to do the

<summary>In this chapter, St. Teresa begins an in-depth reflection on the phrase "daily bread," interpreting it as a request for the constant presence of Christ in the Eucharist. She humbly offers her insights into why Jesus used the specific words "daily" and "this day" in His prayer.</summary>

# CHAP. XXXIV.] DAILY BREAD.

2. The term “this day,” seems to mean the one day during which this mortal life lasts and no more—and indeed it is but a single day for the unfortunate wretches who condemn themselves to forfeit our Lord’s presence in the next world. He has done all that He could to aid them, as His own children, while they lived on earth, dwelling with them and strengthening them, and, if they are overcome, He will not be to blame, for He never ceased to encourage them until the end of the fray. Lost souls will have no excuse to make for themselves, nor will they be able to accuse Christ’s Father of depriving them of this Bread <note>Here, "Bread" refers to the Eucharist, or Holy Communion, which Teresa views as the essential spiritual nourishment for the soul's survival.</note> in their direst need. Therefore Jesus <term>covenants</term> <note>A formal, binding agreement. In this context, it refers to a divine promise made by Jesus to God the Father on behalf of humanity.</note> with His Father that, since the world only lasts “one day,” <note>Teresa uses "one day" as a metaphor for the brevity of human life compared to eternity.</note> He may be allowed to spend it in our service. As God has given Him to us and has sent Him on earth of His own free-will, it is incredible that He would deprive us of His Son when most we want Him, for the insults men offer Him will endure but for a single day. Our Lord respects the obligation He has contracted by offering our will in conjunction with His own, which binds Him to aid us to fulfil this promise by every means in His power; He is not willing to desert us, but desires to remain with us, for the greater glory of His friends and the confusion of His enemies. He prays for nothing new, when He—

There is neither need, nor cross, nor persecution that cannot easily be borne when we once begin to share and to love those our Lord bore, and to keep them ever in our hearts.

3. As regards the other bread—I mean bodily nourishment and wants—I neither wish you to remind God of them, nor to remember them yourselves. Keep your thoughts as guarded as if you were raised to the heights of <term>contemplation</term> <note>A state of deep, silent prayer where the person experiences a direct awareness of God's presence, often losing track of physical needs.</note>, where one no more thinks of food than if one had already quitted this world. Would our Lord have laid such stress on our asking for our *meals*? It would not seem to me becoming either for Him or for us. He is here teaching us "to fix our affections on things above" ² and to pray that we may enjoy the first-fruits of them here: would He, then, bid us concern ourselves about anything so base as asking God for our sustenance? He knows perfectly well that, if we once began to concern ourselves about the needs of our bodies, we should soon forget the needs of our souls. Besides, who would carry prudence to such an excess? We are satisfied with little, and we do not beg for much;

² Colossians 3:1: "Seek the things that are above." <note>original: "Quæ sursum sunt quærite."</note>

### CHAP. XXXIV.] TRUST IN GOD.

...the more men give the more the heavenly water <note>Teresa frequently uses water as a metaphor for the divine grace and consolation received during prayer.</note> appears to fail us. Let those of you, daughters, who are most anxious about our necessities, ask for this!

4. Join our Lord, then, in praying to His Father to let you have your Spouse <note>A mystical title for Jesus Christ, reflecting the soul's intimate union with Him.</note> “this day,” that you may never be without Him in this world—your joy will be tempered by His remaining hidden beneath the <term>accidents</term> <note>A philosophical and theological term referring to the outward physical appearances of the bread and wine (such as taste, smell, and shape) which remain even after the "substance" has changed into the Body and Blood of Christ.</note> of bread and wine; which is a torture to those who can find no love or consolation elsewhere. Beg Him not to fail you, but to give you grace to receive Him worthily. Since you have completely abandoned yourselves into the hands of God, have no care for any other bread but this: I mean while you are at prayer and are asking Him for other things of far greater importance, for there are times when you ought to work to gain your living, <margin>³ Our holy Mother <note>Referring to St. Teresa herself.</note> was very anxious that her nuns should not be idle. resigning yourselves to the will of God. As for me, daughters, I assure you that if I deliberately broke this pledge, as I have often done before, I would neither ask Him to give me bread nor any other food: let Him leave me to die of hunger! For why should I seek to live, if every day I am making eternal death more inevitable? <note>original footnote 5: Escorial manuscript, chapter 61. 5. *A comparison.* If, then, you have truly surrendered yourselves to God, as you say, abandon all care of yourselves, for He cares for you and will always do so. We may be compared to a servant who enters into service with a householder: the domestic <note>The word "domestic" here refers to a household servant or staff member.</note> is obliged to do all he can to please his master, and the master is bound to maintain the servant while employing him in his house, unless prevented by poverty from feeding either himself or his dependents. Here the cases differ, for our Master is, and always will be, rich and powerful. It would seem very strange if the servant went to his employer every day to ask for his meals, knowing perfectly well that without this he would be fed and cared for. It would be waste of time, and his master might tell him to look after his own work, for if he worried himself about other people’s business <note>Meaning the management and provision of the household.</note> he would do his own badly.

->### CHAP. XXXIV.] HEAVENLY BREAD.<-

Let us speak to the purpose and beseech the Father to give us grace so to prepare ourselves for the reception of this sacred and heavenly food that, although our bodily eyes cannot rejoice in looking on Jesus, hidden as He is beneath the **sacramental veils** <term>sacramental veils: a theological term for the physical appearance of bread and wine which, in Catholic belief, conceal the actual presence of Christ</term>, yet He may reveal Himself to the sight of our soul, and may teach us that this Bread is a special kind of nutriment, which contains in itself sweetness and joy, and sustains our life. Inadvertently, we shall often desire and pray for earthly things, but we need not purposely recall such matters to our minds. Our miserable tendency to whatever is base and low, may often excite these thoughts against our will, but let us not deliberately ask for any gifts except those I have recommended to you, for if we obtain these we obtain all the rest.

7. I am sure that it is. I am acquainted with a person <note>Teresa is almost certainly referring to herself here; she often used the third person to speak of her own mystical experiences or healings to remain humble</note> subject to severe illnesses which often cause her acute pain; she was freed from them instantaneously by this Bread <gloss>See St. Teresa's autobiography, *The Life*, chapter 30, paragraph 16</gloss> and remained in perfect health. This often occurs, and people are cured of visible maladies, which I do not think could be counterfeit. 8. Although she was far from perfect, yet I know that for many years my friend endeavoured so to strengthen her faith, that whenever she received <term>Holy Communion</term> <note>The act of consuming the consecrated bread and wine during the Mass.</note>, at which time, as she believed, our Lord entered her poor little dwelling <note>A humble metaphor for the human soul or body.</note>, she might as far as possible withdraw her mind from all earthly things, and enter into herself with Him. She strove to control her senses, in order that they might comprehend the grace she was enjoying, or rather, that they might not prevent her soul from enjoying it. She imagined herself at the feet of our Lord and wept with **Magdalen**,⁷ <note>Saint Mary Magdalene. In Teresa's time, she was traditionally identified as the "sinful woman" who washed Jesus' feet with her tears at the house of a Pharisee named Simon.</note> as if she had really seen Him in the house of the Pharisee. Even if she felt no devotion, faith told her that it was well for her to be there, and she continued conversing with Him. For, unless we choose to be obtuse and to blind ourselves to the fact, we cannot suppose that Christ’s presence here is only an image of our imagination, as when we think of Him on the cross or in other phases of His <term>Passion</term> <note>The period of intense suffering undergone by Jesus, including his trial and crucifixion.</note>.

These happened in the past, but He is here with us at the present moment in very truth: we need not go far to seek Him, for we know that our good Jesus remains with us until the <term>accidents of bread</term> <note>In medieval and Renaissance theology, "accidents" refer to the physical properties of the bread—such as its taste, white color, and circular shape—which remain even after the "substance" has become the Body of Christ.</note> have been consumed by our natural heat <note>A 16th-century term for the process of digestion. Catholic teaching holds that the "Real Presence" of Christ ends once the physical bread is no longer recognizable as bread in the stomach.</note>. Let us not lose this golden opportunity, but let us stay in His company.

9. If, while Jesus lived in the world, the mere touch of His garments healed the sick, who can doubt that when He is dwelling in the very centre of our being He will work miracles on us,<gloss>8</gloss> <note>This gloss refers to the biographical note below detailing Teresa's own experiences.</note> if we have a living faith in Him? His Majesty <note>Teresa's favorite title for God, reflecting both His royal authority and His personal relationship with the soul.</note> is not a bad Paymaster for a good inn <note>Teresa uses the metaphor of an innkeeper; she suggests that God will "pay" the soul with graces in exchange for the hospitality shown to Him during Communion.</note>. Are you grieved at not seeing Him with your bodily eyes? That would not be expedient for us here. It

<note>8 Saint Teresa felt an unspeakable joy, when founding a fresh convent, at the thought that there would be one more church in which the <term>Blessed Sacrament</term> <note>The consecrated bread kept in the church tabernacle, believed to be the actual presence of Jesus.</note> would be reserved (see *Life*, chapter 36, section 5). While she was at St. Joseph's at Avila she was often enraptured after communicating and could not leave the little Communion grille <note>The window or screen through which cloistered nuns receive the bread from the priest.</note>, but had to be removed by the nuns. And if His sublime glory could be seen, how could such a sinful wretch as I am dare to draw thus near to Him, after my many offences? Beneath the <term>accidents of bread</term> <note>In the theology of the time, "accidents" refers to the physical characteristics of the bread—its taste, white color, and round shape—which remain even though the "substance" has changed into the Body of Christ.</note>, He is accessible—if the King disguises Himself, there does not seem to be the same need for ceremonies and court etiquette; indeed, He appears to have waived His claim to them by appearing *incognito* <note>original: "incógnito." When He sees that it would profit a soul, He reveals Himself to it; although unseen by the bodily eyes, He manifests Himself to it by vivid interior intuitions and by other means.

...not understand how, He will continue to teach you. But if you allow your thoughts to wander at once to other matters, and you show no more care or reverence for Him Who dwells within you than if you had not received <term>Holy Communion</term> <note>The Christian sacrament in which the faithful receive the Body and Blood of Christ under the forms of bread and wine.</note>, how can He make Himself known to you? You have no one to blame for this but yourself. This is the time for our Master to instruct, and for us to listen. I do not assert that you must use no vocal prayers, for you would say I was speaking of <term>contemplation</term> <note>A form of wordless, quiet prayer where a person simply rests in God’s presence; Teresa distinguishes this from "vocal prayer" like reciting standard verses.</note>. If our Lord does not raise you to this, recite the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster." The central prayer of Christianity, also known as the Lord's Prayer.</note>, but take care to remember how truly you are in the company of Him Who taught it you: kiss His feet for having done so, and beseech Him not to leave you. If you are accustomed to ask for graces from Christ while looking at His picture, would it not be foolish, at this time, to turn away from Him Who is now with you in person, and to look at His image? It would be the same thing as if, when a friend we dearly loved came to visit us, we refused to talk to him and would only speak to his portrait.

Do you know when the gazing on a representation of Christ is a good and holy practice, in which I take great pleasure? It is when our Lord is absent and makes us feel His loss by <term>aridities</term> <note>In spiritual life, this refers to "dryness"—a period where one feels no emotional or spiritual comfort in prayer, making it feel difficult or empty.</note>. It is a great joy to look at an image of <term>our Lady</term> <note>A traditional title for the Virgin Mary, the mother of Jesus.</note> or of any Saint for whom we have a devotion. How much more so when the likeness is that of Christ, who has given us such cause to love Him?

<summary>St. Teresa advises the soul to remain focused on Christ’s real presence within after Holy Communion, warning that distractions prevent spiritual growth. She notes that while religious images are vital during times of spiritual "dryness," they should not distract us when Christ is personally present in the soul.</summary>

## CHAP. XXXIV.] HOLY COMMUNION.

you receive <term>Holy Communion</term> <note>The central Christian act of receiving the consecrated bread and wine, believed to be the body and blood of Christ.</note>—and if you strive to keep your conscience clear, so that you may frequently enjoy this grace, His coming will not be so hidden but that, in many a way, He will reveal Himself to you in proportion to the desire you have of seeing Him. Indeed, if your longing for Him is very **vehement**,¹² He may disclose Himself entirely to you.¹³ But if we care nothing for Him Whom we have received in such intimate union, but either go to seek Him elsewhere or busy ourselves about other, and lower matters, what would we have Him do? Must He drag us by force to look at Him, and to stay with Him because He wishes to manifest Himself to us? No! for men did not treat Him too well when He showed Himself visibly among them and told them Who He was—few indeed of them believed Him. He has done us a great grace in teaching us that He is present in the <term>Blessed Sacrament</term> <note>A devotional name for the Eucharist, emphasizing the presence of Jesus in the bread and wine.</note>. But He will not show Himself openly or reveal His glories or bestow His treasures, save on souls which prove that they ardently desire Him, for these are His real friends. But let not the soul which is not of their number, which offends Him and approaches to receive Him without having prepared itself to the best of its ability—let not that soul <term>importune</term> <note>To persist in demanding or asking for something, perhaps inappropriately.</note> Him to reveal Himself to it. ## CHAPTER XXXV. <note>In the Valladolid manuscript, this is chapter 37; in the Escorial manuscript, it is chapter 62.</note>

->Continues the foregoing subject. Contains an appeal to the eternal Father. Of the <term>recollection</term> <note>A specific method of prayer where the person gathers their senses and focuses inward to find God dwelling within the soul.</note> which should be practised after Holy Communion.<-

1. ### [CHAPTER 35. THE LUTHERANS. 239]

...among the <term>Lutherans</term> <note>St. Teresa refers here to the Protestant Reformers of the 16th century. In her historical context, their rejection of Catholic doctrines regarding the Eucharist was seen as a profound spiritual crisis.</note> against the most <term>Blessed Sacrament</term> <note>The Eucharist, or Holy Communion, which Catholics believe is the real presence of Jesus Christ.</note>. I beseech You, O **eternal Father**, to extinguish this conflagration, since it is in Your power to do so.

4. Behold, Your Son remains on earth with us: in deference to Him, stop these foul and abominable outrages, for one so pure and beautiful as He is ought not to dwell amid such pollution. We do not ask this for ourselves, O God—we do not deserve it—grant it for the sake of Your Son! We dare not beg that He should stay with us no longer, for You have consented to His prayer that for "to-day," that is, as long as the world lasts, You would leave Him with us. Without His presence, what would become of us? As some redress must be found for these wrongs, may it please You to supply it, for You can do so if You will.

# CHAPTER XXXVI (continued)
## FORGIVENESS

Therefore, continuing to teach us the prayer, He says: “Forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors.” Notice, daughters, He does not say, “as we *are about* to forgive our debtors,” because we are to understand that we must have already done this, before we beg for so great a gift (as this Bread <note>Here, Teresa links the petition for forgiveness directly to the Eucharist, or "Bread of Life," discussed in the preceding chapter.</note>) and the surrender of our own will to that of God. Therefore Christ’s words are, “as we forgive our debtors.” Whoever wishes to be able to say to God in all sincerity, “Thy will be done!” must have forgiven others beforehand, at least in intention.

4. What can be more absurd than for members of a religious order to stand upon their dignity on such petty points? I am absolutely surprised at them! You know nothing about such things, sisters: I will tell you, so that you may be on your guard. The devil has not forgotten us—he has introduced the concept of "honors" into <term>religious houses</term> <note>Monasteries or convents where individuals live under religious vows.</note>—he has established the laws by which the inhabitants rise and fall in status (just as people do in the secular world), and they are jealous of their reputation in surprisingly small matters. 

Learned men must observe a certain order in their studies, which I cannot understand: he who has studied <term>theology</term> <note>The study of God and religious doctrine, considered the highest level of education at the time.</note> must not then lower himself to teach or read <term>philosophy</term> <note>In the educational system of the time, philosophy was a preliminary study to theology; returning to it was seen as a demotion in academic status.</note>. This is a <term>point of honor</term> <note>A matter of personal pride or social standing based on one's position or achievements.</note>, which consists in always moving forward and never backward. If <term>obedience</term> <note>The vow taken by nuns and monks to follow the directions of their superiors.</note> forced anyone to do the opposite, he would secretly feel it as an insult, and would find others to take his side and say he had been treated poorly. The devil would easily find reasons, even from the holy <term>Scriptures</term> <note>The Bible.</note>, to make this behavior seem justified. 

Even among nuns, she who has served as the <term>Prioress</term> <note>The woman elected to lead the convent.</note> must not afterwards hold any lower office; respect must be shown to the one highest in rank, and she makes sure we do not forget it. At times, this even seems like a virtue because the <term>Rule</term> <note>The specific set of laws and guidelines for daily life followed by the Carmelite order.</note> requires us to show respect to our elders. The whole thing is absurd, and enough to make one laugh—or rather cry, with more reason than...

## CHAP. XXXVI.] TRUE HONOUR.

...can be told. I know the <term>Rule</term> <note>The formal set of regulations governing the Carmelite Order.</note> does not forbid me to be humble: the regulation is made to maintain order, but I ought not to be so careful of my dignity as to insist on this point being obeyed as strictly as the rest. And perhaps I keep those injunctions very slackly, while I will give up no <term>jot or tittle</term> <note>A biblical expression (from Matthew 5:18) meaning the smallest possible stroke of a pen or the tiniest detail.</note> of this one. Let others see to what concerns my rank and let me take no notice of it! The fact is, we are bent on rising higher, although we shall never mount to heaven by this path, and we will not dream of descending. <note>The Saint was so averse to honors that she wished to leave the city of Avila—where she was highly respected—to live as an unknown in a distant convent. She even sought to become a <term>lay sister</term> (nuns who performed the most menial domestic work) to avoid any status, but her superiors forbade it. (References: *Life* [original: *Vida*], ch. xxxi. 16; *Ribera*, bk. iv. ch. xv.)</note>

5. O my Lord, art not Thou our **Pattern** and our **Master**? Indeed Thou art. And in what did Thine honour consist, O ever-honoured Master and King? Didst Thou lose it, in being humbled even unto death? No, Lord, Thou didst thereby gain it and didst win graces for us all. Therefore, sisters! how far we shall err from the right path if we follow this way, for it leads us wrong from the very beginning. May He grant that no soul may be lost through observing these miserable points of etiquette, without realising in what **true honour** consists. At last we come to believe...

...that we have done a great thing when we forgive some trifle <note>A "trifle" refers to a small, insignificant matter.</note> which was neither an affront nor an injury nor anything of the sort, nor gave us any just cause for resentment. Give us grace, O Lord, to know that we do not understand what we are saying, and that all such souls come to Him as empty-handed as I do myself. <note>Teresa frequently describes herself as "empty-handed" to emphasize that she has no merits of her own to offer God, only her failings.</note> Grant this for the sake of Thy loving mercy. 6. What value God places on our loving and keeping peace with one another! For when once we have given Him our will, we have given Him the right to it, and this we cannot do without love. See, sisters, what need there is for us to love and to agree with each other. The **good Jesus** <note>This was a favorite, intimate title Teresa used for Christ, reflecting her personal relationship with Him.</note> places it before anything else. He does not mention the many things we gave Him on one single occasion, nor does He offer them in our name to His Father. He might have said: "Forgive us because of our many <term>penances</term> <note>Self-imposed punishments or acts of devotion performed to show sorrow for sins.</note>, or prayers, or fasts, or because we have left all for Thee and love Thee fervently, and have suffered for Thee and long to suffer more." He never says, "Because we would lay down our lives for Thee," or recounts the many other things the soul does...

This is a central line from the Pater Noster (Our Father).</note> Perhaps this was because He knew of our attachment to this miserable “honour,” <note>In 16th-century Spain, "honra" (honor) was a rigid social code of reputation and status that often led to pride and conflict.</note> so that we will overlook no slight upon it. This being the most difficult thing for us to overcome, our Lord put it in the first place, so that, after having asked such sublime graces <note>Spiritual gifts or divine assistance given by God.</note> for us, He offers this for our repayment.⁸

7. Notice, sisters, that Christ says, “As we **have forgiven** our debtors,” to show that it is a thing we have already done, as I said. Be sure of this—when a soul, after receiving some of the special favours in prayer which I have described, and after having been raised to <term>perfect contemplation</term> <note>A deep state of wordless prayer where the soul is consciously united with God.</note>, does not come away with a firm determination to forgive others, and if occasion offers, it does not actually pardon any injury, serious as it may be—unless these fruits are left in the soul, the graces never came from God, but were illusions and delights caused by the devil, to make such a person think herself holy and therefore worthy of greater honour. I am not speaking of the trifles people call injuries, for these do not affect a soul that God raises to so high a prayer, nor does it care whether it is highly esteemed or no. I am wrong in saying that “it does not care,” for honour troubles it far more than contempt, and it dislikes

<summary>St. Teresa argues that the true measure of spiritual progress and "perfect contemplation" is the sincere ability to forgive others, especially regarding matters of social honor. She warns that spiritual ecstasies are likely demonic deceptions if they do not result in a greater capacity for forgiveness and humility.</summary>

For when God has truly given His kingdom to a person, *she* no longer desires any kingdom in this world. She understands that this is the way to reign in a far higher manner, as experience has taught her the benefits that come from it and how the soul makes rapid progress through suffering for God’s sake. Only in very exceptional cases does He grant sublime favors to souls who have not willingly endured many severe trials for Him. As I have already told you in this book, <term>contemplatives</term> <note>People who practice a form of wordless, meditative prayer focused on experiencing God’s presence directly.</note> have heavy crosses to bear,⁹ <note>Original reference: *Way of Perfection*, chapter 18, section 1. St. Teresa often uses "crosses" to mean personal hardships or spiritual burdens.</note> and so our Lord chooses hearts that have been thoroughly tested.

8. You must know, sisters, that such souls, having learned the true worth of all created things, do not pay much attention to any temporary matter. If, for a moment, some serious injury or suffering affects them, they have hardly felt it before reason comes to their aid. It is as if reason raises the banner of faith for them and overcomes their pain with the joy of seeing that God has given them an opportunity. They realize they can gain more <term>graces</term> <note>Divine assistance or spiritual "points" of favor granted by God.</note> and lasting rewards in a single day than they could have earned for themselves in ten years of their own self-chosen hard work. Such souls have no self-esteem—they are glad that their faults should be known, <note>A devout person, while talking to our holy Mother [St. Teresa], once said: "Well, Mother, you may be a saint, but you don't seem like one to me." St. Teresa was delighted and answered: "God reward you for those words. You tell the truth, and you know what I really am." If they felt any pleasure in being of noble birth, it would only be when it enabled them to render God greater service. If they are not well-born, they are distressed when people over-rate them, and, if they take no pleasure in undeceiving their friends, at any rate they feel no reluctance in doing so. This must be, because the souls on whom God has bestowed such great humility and love for Him, so entirely forget themselves in all that concerns rendering Him greater service, that they cannot believe that anyone can be troubled by certain annoyances which they themselves do not resent as injuries.

9. These last-mentioned effects are proper to persons who have arrived at a high degree of **perfection**, <note>A state of spiritual maturity and close union with God. See *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 6, chapter 8, section 5, and *The Life*, chapter 19, section 2.</note> to

For the divine kindness and mercy shown her prove the immense love felt for her by the Almighty, and she is overjoyed to have an opportunity of showing love in return.

Let no one fancy that such feelings were graces from God, for He always enriches the soul He visits. This is certain, for although the grace or <term>consolation</term> <note>A sense of spiritual peace, joy, or encouragement felt during prayer.</note> may pass away quickly, it is detected in time by the benefits it has left in the soul. The good Jesus is well aware of this and therefore deliberately assures His Father that we forgive our debtors.

## CHAPTER XXXVII.<note>Footnote 1: In the version of this text kept at Valladolid, this is Chapter 39; in the Escorial manuscript, it is a continuation of Chapter 65.</note>

### ->Of the beauties of the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> and the great consolation it brings us.<-

*1. All perfection and contemplation comprised in the Our Father. 2. It is suited to every one. 3. The reward of efforts made for God. 4. “Lead us not into temptation.”*

I. THE sublime perfection of this <term>evangelical prayer</term> <note>A prayer derived directly from the Gospels (the Evangels).</note> is marvellous and we ought to thank God fervently for it. So admirably is it composed by the good Master that everybody may apply its meaning to his own wants. I am astonished at finding all perfection and <term>contemplation</term> <note>A wordless, focused state of prayer where the soul rests in the presence of God.</note> comprised in it—

<summary>This page concludes St. Teresa's teaching on forgiveness as the proof of genuine prayer and introduces her praise for the "Our Father" as the ultimate guide to spiritual perfection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the opening of Chapter 37. St. Teresa of Avila is concluding her introductory thoughts on the "Our Father" (Pater Noster), emphasizing that this single prayer contains the entirety of the spiritual journey, from basic meditation to the highest mystical states.</meta>
