<header>THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXVII.</header>

tion comprised in it,² so that there seems no need to study any other writings. For here our Lord has unfolded to us <term>mental prayer</term> <note>Prayer practiced in the mind and heart, focusing on a personal, internal conversation with God rather than just reciting words.</note> from its very beginning, through the <term>prayer of quiet</term> <note>A state of prayer where the soul experiences a deep, supernatural peace and a sense of resting in God’s presence.</note> and <term>union</term> <note>A profound mystical state where the soul feels completely joined with the Divine.</note>, up to the most high and perfect <term>contemplation</term> <note>The highest form of prayer, characterized by a wordless, loving gaze upon God.</note>. Therefore, if I had not already written about it elsewhere, and also for the reason that I dare not enlarge upon it as the results would only be exasperating, I could, were I able to express myself, write a large book on prayer, based upon this solid foundation. Our Lord here shows us, as you have seen, the effects of prayer and contemplation when they are <term>divine graces</term> <note>Spiritual gifts or favors freely given by God to help a person grow in holiness.</note>.

2. I have sometimes wondered why <term>His Majesty</term> <note>A title St. Teresa frequently uses to refer to Jesus Christ, emphasizing His sovereign and divine nature.</note> did not speak more clearly on such sublime subjects, so that we might all understand His meaning: then I reflected that perhaps Christ left the meaning obscure because this prayer was meant for all men, and thus each one might ask for what he wanted and might feel happy in the idea that he was using the words in their right sense. Blessed be His Name to endless ages, Amen. I implore the Father, for His Son’s sake, to remit all my debts—

² Blessed Angela of Foligno <note>A 13th-century Italian mystic known for her deep spiritual writings and conversion.</note> said: “I recited the ‘<term>Our Father</term>’ <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> with so much contrition and recollection, pronouncing every word, that, though I was plunged in great anguish by the thought of my sins, I yet received immense consolation, and tasted something of the bliss God grants His beloved ones. and serious sins, for there is no reason or person that should require forgiveness from me <note>Teresa suggests that her own faults are so great that she has no right to hold a grudge against anyone else.</note>. Every day I have a fresh need for pardon, and I pray that He may grant me the grace of having something to offer Him each day as compensation for my own offenses. **Contemplatives** and very devout people who do not seek earthly goods may ask for heavenly favors, which, by the great mercy of God, can be enjoyed even while we still live in this world.

The good Jesus has taught us here a most heavenly prayer. By it, He intends that we might resemble the angels while we are still in exile <note>A traditional spiritual term referring to earthly life as a temporary state of separation from our true home in Heaven.</note> here, provided we strive with all our might to make our actions match the petitions we make. People who live in the world do right by following the duties of their station in life; they may use this prayer to ask for the bread they require to maintain their households. This is right and just, as it is for them to pray for whatever else they need. But let them take notice that two specific requirements—**yielding up our will** and **forgiving others**—apply to everyone. It is true that there are different degrees in the way of carrying them out; for example, perfect souls...

We can never succeed in deceiving Him, because He knows all things. Do not say one thing and mean another: if we treat Him with truth and candour, He will always give us more than we ask for. Our kind Master knows that those who tend to great perfection by reciting of this prayer will reach to a high degree by means of the graces the eternal Father will—

## CHAPTER XXXVIII <note>In the Valladolid manuscript, this is chapter 40; in the Escorial manuscript, it is chapter 66.</note>

Treats of the urgent need there is of begging the eternal Father to grant us the petition, “Lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.” Enumerates and explains some temptations which come from the devil.

*1. By temptation is meant, not sufferings, but the snares of the devil. 2. Wiles of the evil one. 3. Humility our safeguard. 4. Fallacious virtues. 5. Their danger for ourselves and others. 6. Our virtues are unstable. 7. Humility is our safeguard. 8. Fictitious patience. 9. Imaginary poverty of spirit. 10. True and fancied poverty of spirit. 11. Religious poverty and care for the future. 12. The guard we must keep against deceptive virtues.*

-> “AND LEAD US NOT INTO TEMPTATION, <-
-> BUT DELIVER US FROM EVIL. AMEN.” <-

1. WE must now think of and interpret these words as applying to sublime matters, since we are here praying for such graces. I feel convinced that souls which have arrived at this degree of <term>perfection in prayer</term> <note>Teresa refers to the higher states of contemplation, such as the Prayer of Quiet or Union, where the soul feels closely united with God.</note> do not ask God to deliver them from trials or temptations, nor from persecutions and combats. <note>Teresa notes similar themes in her other writings: *The Interior Castle* (Mansion 6, ch. 4; Mansion 7, ch. 3), the *Exclamations* (14.3), and her *Life* (ch. 40, 27).</note> This is another unmistakable and noteworthy effect, showing that the contemplation and favours given such people come from the <term>Holy Ghost</term> <note>The Holy Spirit. Teresa emphasizes that true divine favors lead to a desire for spiritual labor rather than just comfort.</note> and are not illusions, for, as I said just now, these souls wish for and demand such troubles and love them instead of hating them. They are like soldiers—the more they fight, the better they like it, because they hope for a more valuable share of the spoils. When there is no war, they live on their pay, but they know they will not get rich on that. <note>Teresa uses this military imagery to explain that spiritual "battles" are the means by which a soul earns "spoils" or greater degrees of glory and grace.</note>

¹ <note>original: "Valladolid edition, ch. xl. ; Escorial, ch. lxvi." These refer to the primary original manuscripts of the work.</note>
² <note>original: "Castle, M. vi. ch. iv. 19 ; M. vii. ch. iii. 4. Excl. xiv. 3. Life, ch. xl. 27." These are citations to Teresa's other major works.</note>

<summary>St. Teresa begins her commentary on the final petition of the Lord's Prayer, explaining that advanced souls do not fear trials but welcome them as spiritual battles that lead to greater growth.</summary>

for thus they hope for a richer booty. When there is no war they serve for their pay, but they know they will not grow rich on that. Believe me, sisters, the battle never comes soon enough for the <term>soldiers of Christ</term> <note>Teresa uses this military metaphor, common in 16th-century Spain, to describe those who view the spiritual life as an active, heroic struggle against evil.</note>. I allude to <term>contemplatives</term> <note>This refers to those who practice "mental prayer" or contemplation, a deep state of prayer where one listens to God rather than reciting rote formulas.</note>, and people who practise prayer. They have little fear of open enemies, knowing them well already, and they are aware that such foes have little power against the strength given them by God, through which they always gain the victory and come forth from the fray with great spoils and riches, so that they never beat a retreat. The foes they really dread—and it is well that they should dread them and should always pray God that they may be delivered from them—are those treacherous antagonists, the devils, who transform themselves into **angels of light** <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 11:14, warning that spiritual deception often disguises itself as something holy or beneficial.</note>. They come in disguise and do not let us find them out, until they have wrought great harm to the soul. They suck our very life-blood and destroy our virtues, whilst we, unwittingly, are surrounded by temptations.

What good cause has our kind Master to teach us to ask for this and to demand it Himself on our behalf ! Consider in how many ways the evil spirits injure us. You must not suppose that it is only by persuading us

<margin>³ *The Interior Castle*, Mansion 5, chapter 4, section 7. *The Life of St. Teresa*, chapter 15, sections 15, 16.</margin>
that the sweetness and consolations they give us come from God. <note>original: "consolations" — These are feelings of deep peace or emotional joy experienced during prayer.</note>³ This seems to me the least harm they can do: it may even help some souls who, lured by the devotion they feel, will spend more time in prayer. Not knowing that the devil has a hand in it, they continually praise our Lord for having granted them <term>favours</term> <note>Special spiritual gifts or experiences perceived as signs of God's grace.</note> which they did not deserve; they feel bound to serve Him more fervently, and strive to prepare themselves to receive still further graces, supposing that these have come from His hand.

3. Always try to be humble, sisters; believe that you are unworthy of these gifts and do not seek them. I am convinced that in this way the evil one loses many a soul which he thought to have ruined, and that our Lord draws good out of the harm the <term>fiend</term> <note>A common term for the devil.</note> meant to work us, for <term>His Majesty</term> <note>Teresa’s standard, respectful title for God, reflecting the courtly language of 16th-century Spain.</note> looks at our intention, which is to serve and please Him by keeping in His presence in prayer, and He is ever faithful. But we must be cautious, lest the enemy make a breach in our humility by <term>vainglory</term> <note>Inordinate pride in one's own spiritual achievements or "holiness."</note>: and we must beg God to preserve us from this. You need not fear, daughters, that He will let any one else console you much, except Himself.

-> ### CHAPTER 38. FALLACIOUS VIRTUES. ### <-

Through <term>favours</term> <note>Special spiritual gifts or experiences perceived as signs of God's grace.</note> we seem only to be recipients, and therefore feel the more strictly bound to serve God; but this delusion makes us think that we render Him some gift and service, which He is called upon to repay. By degrees, this damages us greatly, for while on the one hand it weakens our humility, on the other, we neglect to acquire the virtue we believe that we already own. Suspecting no evil (for we think we are safe), we fall into a ditch from which we cannot get out. For although there does not always appear to have been any <term>mortal sin</term> <note>In Catholic theology, a grave sin committed with full knowledge and consent that separates the soul from God's grace.</note> which would certainly drag us to hell, yet we are lamed, so that we cannot travel on the road I began to speak of (and which I have not forgotten). For how can any one walk after being plunged into a deep ditch? It would be the death of him—and it is fortunate if it does not reach down to hell. In any case, it never aids one on the way.

5. A person in this state can do nothing but mischief to himself or to anybody else. When the ditch has been dug, many passers-by may fall into it, but if the man had climbed out and filled it up with earth, it could cause no more harm either to himself or to other people. I assure you, this is a very dangerous temptation: I have had great experience of it, so that I can explain the matter, although not as well as I could wish. What remedy is there for it, sisters? That which our <term>Master</term> <note>A reference to Jesus Christ.</note> has taught us seems to me the best—to pray and to

<summary>Teresa warns that the false belief that spiritual progress is one's own merit, rather than a gift from God, destroys humility and creates a "ditch" of pride that halts spiritual growth and may even lead to spiritual death. She recommends constant prayer as the remedy for this dangerous temptation.</summary>

6. There is another temptation. When God gives us some <term>virtue</term> <note>A positive trait or moral excellence, such as patience or humility.</note>, we must understand that it is only a loan, and that He may take it away again—as indeed often happens, not without a wise <term>providence</term> <note>The protective care of God; Teresa suggests that losing our sense of virtue is a way for God to keep us humble and aware of our dependence on Him.</note>. Have you never found this out for yourselves, sisters? <note>Teresa addressed her writing to the "sisters" (nuns) of the Discalced Carmelite order.</note> I certainly have. Sometimes I imagine that I am very <term>detached</term> <note>The state of not being tethered to material things, status, or one's own desires.</note>—and in a real trial, I find that I actually am. Yet, at another time, I discover that I am so attached to things that I might have laughed at just the day before, that I hardly know myself. 

Again, I feel such courage that there is nothing I would fear to do in God’s service, and I find, when it comes to the proof, that I am indeed brave in some matters—yet, the very next day, I would not venture to "kill an ant" <note>A characteristic expression of Teresa's, used as a hyperbole to describe a lack of even the smallest amount of spiritual courage or strength.</note> for Him if I met with any opposition. Sometimes I care nothing if people talk or complain about me, and very often, indeed, it has even given me pleasure. Yet there are occasions when a single word disturbs me, and I long to leave this world, for everything in it disgusts me. I am not the only person to whom this happens, for I have noticed it in people better than myself, and I know that it is a fact.

7. If this be the case, who can say that he possesses any virtue, or that he is rich, when at the time he most needs these goods, he finds himself destitute of...

We are in the habit of saying that we want nothing and do not care for anything, yet directly something is offered us, even though it is superfluous, our poverty of spirit disappears—and much good do our professions of poverty seem to have done us ! A person who has taken this vow and who thinks that she really is **poor in spirit**, says to herself : “ I do not want anything, but I keep this because I cannot do without it ; we must live, in order to serve God, and He wishes us to maintain our bodies.” The **demon**, disguised as an **angel of light** <note>Original Latin: *angelus lucis*. <page-num>263</page-num>
-> CHAP. XXXVIII.] POVERTY OF SPIRIT. <-

he leads her to imagine that she possesses the virtue of <term>poverty of spirit</term> <note>A state of inner detachment where a person does not rely on material wealth or status for their identity or security.</note>, and that everything is accomplished.

10. Let us now come to the time of trial—for we can only test ourselves by watching our actions narrowly—and we shall soon detect signs of the devil's deceptions. For instance, let us suppose that a man possesses a larger income than he needs—I mean, than is really necessary for him, and he keeps three valets when he could manage with one. When he is sued for some part of his estate, or one of his poor tenants does not pay his rent, this person is as disturbed and worried about it as if his living depended on it.⁶ He will tell you that he cannot lose his property through neglecting it, and at once pleads this excuse. I do not say that he should neglect his business—on the contrary, he ought to attend to it; then, if it succeeds, very well; if not, never mind! One who is poor at heart cares so little for such affairs that though, for certain reasons, he attends to them, yet they give him no trouble because he never thinks he will come to want—if he should, it would not grieve him, because he considers such things secondary matters, and not the main point. His thoughts are fixed on higher objects, and he only constrains himself to attend to <term>temporal cares</term> <note>Tasks and worries related to the physical, temporary world rather than eternal spiritual life.</note> against his will.⁷

⁷ <note>The Escorial edition (a manuscript version preserved at the monastery of El Escorial), chapter 67. This note indicates that the text continues the same theme, warning against a false sense of humility that is actually a product of the devil's influence.</note>

<meta>This page continues St. Teresa’s examination of how religious individuals—monks and nuns—can fall into the trap of "false poverty." She argues that true poverty of spirit requires total reliance on God’s providence, rather than keeping material safety nets "just in case."</meta>

### -> THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAPTER 38] <-

11. A monk (or a nun) who is truly poor—or at least, who ought to be—possesses nothing. Sometimes this is simply because there is nothing to possess; but if a gift is offered to this <term>religious</term> <note>A person who has taken formal vows in a religious order, such as a monk, nun, or friar.</note>, it would be unusual for them to consider it unnecessary, for they always prefer to keep something in reserve. If they can obtain a <term>habit</term> <note>The distinctive dress or garment worn by members of a religious order.</note> made of fine material, they do not ask for a coarse one. They always keep some small item that they could pawn or sell, even if it is only a few books;⁸ they reason that if they fell ill, they would require better food than what is usually provided. What a foolish creature! Is this the <term>vow</term> <note>A solemn promise made to God; here specifically the vow of poverty, where one gives up the right to own property.</note> you made to forget yourself and to leave everything to God, no matter what might happen? If you feel you must provide for your own future, it would actually cause you far less anxiety to simply hold a steady, settled income. While keeping such reserves might not be a sin, it is important for us to recognize these <term>imperfections</term> <note>In spiritual writing, these are minor flaws or attachments that hinder a person's progress toward holiness, even if they aren't "mortal sins."</note>. By doing so, we can see how far we still have to go to achieve true **poverty of spirit**, and we can ask our Lord to grant it to us. If we mistakenly believe we already possess this virtue, we will become careless, and even worse, we will remain self-deceived on this point.

12. The same sort of thing happens in the matter of **humility**. We imagine that we do not desire honor and that we are—

The old Constitutions, which dated back to the start of the fourteenth century, allowed, for instance, a scholar at a university to sell his books—which were of much greater value then than they are today—in cases of urgent need to provide for himself. ...indifferent to everything of the kind—yet, let any one offer us even the slightest affront <note>An "affront" is a deliberate insult or an act of disrespect to one's face.</note>, and our feelings and behavior will at once betray that we are not truly humble.⁹ 

Besides, if any opportunity occurs of increasing our dignity <note>In this context, "dignity" refers to one’s social rank, reputation, or status within the community.</note> (much like the poor <term>religious</term> <note>A person who has taken formal vows in a religious order, such as a monk or nun.</note> I have just spoken of), we do not reject it, justifying it as being for the sake of a "greater good." We are so accustomed to saying that we want nothing and are indifferent to everything (which we really believe is the truth), that at last the very habit of asserting it convinces us of its truth more strongly than the reality of our actions. 

It is well to be on our guard against this temptation as well as the others I mentioned. Indeed, there are many we must be watchful about if we wish to detect them. For when God gives us any solid **virtue**, it is a well-known fact that it brings all the others in its train <note>This refers to the "connection of the virtues," a traditional theological idea that you cannot truly possess one virtue—like charity—without possessing all the others, such as humility and patience, in some degree.</note>. But I warn you again always to fear deception even when you believe that you possess such virtue. A truly humble soul mistrusts its own good qualities: it believes in them more readily and values them more highly when it finds them in another.

<summary>St. Teresa warns that true humility is often faked, as we are easily offended by insults despite our claims of indifference. She encourages a healthy self-distrust, noting that real virtue is always accompanied by other virtues and a high regard for others.</summary>

# CHAPTER 39 <note>In the Valladolid edition of the manuscript, this is Chapter 41; in the Escorial manuscript, it is a continuation of Chapter 67.</note>

## Continues the same subject. A description of different kinds of temptations and of the means of freeing the soul from them.

*1. How Satan discourages the soul. 2. Difference between humility and discouragement. 3. How to resist this temptation. 4. Indiscreet penances. 5. Temptations to presumption. 6. Humility during divine consolations. 7. A prayer against temptation. 8. How prayer protects us from the devil’s snares.*

1. **BEWARE**, daughters, of a certain kind of humility suggested by the devil, which is accompanied by great anxiety about the gravity <note>The "gravity" refers to the weight or extreme seriousness of one's previous mistakes.</note> of our past sins. <note>Teresa discusses this elsewhere in her *Life* (chapter 10, section 4 and chapter 23, sections 3 and 45) and in *The Interior Castle* (Mansion I, chapter 2, section 11).</note> He disturbs souls in many ways by this means, until at last he stops them from receiving <term>Holy Communion</term> <note>Also called the Eucharist, this is the central Christian sacrament where bread and wine are consecrated and consumed in remembrance of Jesus.</note> by doubts as to whether they are in a fit state for it, and such thoughts as: “Am I worthy of it? Am I in a good disposition? I am unfit to live in a <term>religious community</term> <note>A group of people, such as nuns or monks, who live together under a shared rule of life and religious vows.</note>.” The evil one thus hinders Christians from prayer, and when they communicate, the time during which they ought to be obtaining graces <note>In Christian theology, "graces" are free and undeserved gifts or help from God for spiritual growth.</note> is spent in wondering whether they were well prepared or no. Things come to such a pass that Satan makes the soul believe that God has forsaken it on account of its sins, so that it almost doubts His mercy. Everything such a person says seems to her on the verge of evil, and all her

<summary>In this opening to Chapter 39, St. Teresa warns that the devil uses excessive guilt and "false humility" to make individuals doubt God's mercy and avoid prayer or the sacraments. She identifies this as a strategic spiritual discouragement designed to make a person feel unworthy of their community and God’s love.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 39 of St. Teresa’s "The Way of Perfection." It follows from the previous page's discussion on how the devil uses excessive guilt over past sins to discourage a soul. ->**CHAPTER 39: DISCOURAGEMENT**<-

...actions appear fruitless, however good in themselves. She becomes discouraged, thinking that she can do nothing right, for what is good in others she fancies is wrong in herself.

2. Pay great attention, daughters <note>St. Teresa is addressing the nuns of her convent, whom she views as her spiritual daughters.</note>, to what I am about to say. At one time it may be <term>humility</term> <note>The honest recognition of one's own character and limitations before God.</note> and a virtue to think ourselves thus sinful, and at another time it is a most dangerous temptation. I know this, for I have passed through this state myself. However deep humility may be, it neither disquiets, wearies, nor disturbs the soul, but is peaceful, sweet, and serene. Although the sight of our wickedness grieves us and proves to us that we deserve to be in hell, and that in justice all mankind should hate us, so that we hardly dare to beg for mercy, yet if it is a right humility this pain is accompanied by suavity <note>Original: "suavidad." In 16th-century spiritual writing, this refers to a gentle, pleasant sweetness or internal comfort.</note>, content, and joy, and we do not wish to be without it; indeed, it ought to be prized, since it results in <term>self-knowledge</term> <note>A foundational concept for Teresa; understanding one's own soul as a "castle" or mirror.</note>. It dilates <note>To expand or open up the heart, making it feel more generous and capable of love.</note>, instead of troubling or depressing the soul, making it more capable of serving God. The other sorrow, which distresses the mind, renders it uneasy, completely subverting it and causing great pain, so that there is no possibility of calming the thoughts. You may feel certain that this is a temptation and not humility, with which it has no connection. I believe that this is a plot of the devil to make us think we are lowly, and at the same time to lead us to distrust God.

<vocab>humility, virtue, temptation, soul, self-knowledge, God, devil, distrust, discouragement</vocab>

Instead, meditate on the mercy of God, His love for us, and all the suffering He endured for our sake. 4. Something similar happens when we perform unwise <term>penances</term> <note>Acts of self-discipline or devotion performed to show sorrow for sin.</note> just to convince ourselves that we feel more sorry for our sins than others do, or to feel like we are "doing something." If we hide these <term>mortifications</term> <note>The practice of "dying to the self" through physical or spiritual disciplines, such as fasting or extra prayers.</note> from our <term>confessor</term> <note>The priest who hears a nun's confession and provides spiritual guidance.</note> or <term>Prioress</term> <note>The superior or head of the convent.</note>, or if we feel annoyed when we are forbidden from practicing them,³ and then go ahead and disobey that order—this is clearly a temptation. Strive to obey, even if you find it harder to do so, for obedience is far more perfect than self-chosen sacrifice. This advice applies to every situation, and you must take special care to remember it.⁴

5. Another very deceptive temptation is a feeling of over-confidence—the belief that we will never fall back into our old sins or care for worldly pleasures again. We might say to ourselves: “Now I truly see the world for what it is; everything in it is temporary, and I only care for the things of God.” This temptation is the most dangerous of all, especially...

->### CHAPTER XXXIX. CONSOLATIONS.<-

...at the beginning of the <term>religious life</term> <note>The formal commitment to living as a nun or monk under spiritual vows and discipline.</note>, for such souls, feeling that they are safe, do not guard themselves against occasions of sin. <margin>5 *The Interior Castle*, Mansion VI, chapter iii, 18; chapter ix, 10. *The Life of St. Teresa*, chapter xxvi, 5.</margin>

6. Whatever <term>consolations</term> <note>Spiritual feelings of joy, peace, or divine closeness experienced during prayer.</note> and signs of love our Lord may give you, avoid all occasions of evil, and never feel safe against a relapse. Be sure to mention these favours and graces to some one who is able to counsel you: hide nothing from him. Always begin and finish your prayer with the thought of your own nothingness, however sublime your <term>contemplation</term> <note>A deep, wordless form of prayer where the soul is focused entirely on God.</note> may be, and even though our Lord may impart Himself to you and offer you proofs of His love. If this contemplation comes from God, you will often find yourselves unconsciously doing this, for such prayer produces humility and leaves us with a greater knowledge of our own unworthiness. Be cautious and consult some one who understands such matters,<gloss>5</gloss> for the devil attacks us at these times in various ways. I will say no more here, as there are many books which give advice about it: I have written this because I have had much experience on the subject, which has given me a great deal of trouble. Still,

<summary>St. Teresa warns that spiritual overconfidence leads to a lack of vigilance, making the soul vulnerable to the devil's traps. She emphasizes that true divine consolations must always result in deeper humility and should be shared with a spiritual director for safety.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XXXIX.

nothing that can be said will make us perfectly secure, because we cannot understand ourselves.

7. What, O eternal Father, can we do, save to have recourse to Thee and beg Thee not to permit our enemies to lead us into temptation? Let open assaults come, and with Thine aid we can defend ourselves; but how can we detect these subtle snares? O my God! never must we cease to beg Thy help. Give us some safeguard against surprise, and show us how to understand ourselves and to feel secure. Thou knowest how few are the souls that follow the way of prayer—and if they must live thus beset with fears, their number will diminish.

8. How strange it seems! One would think the devil never tempted any one who did not pray! The world is more horrified at one person who aims at perfection falling into some deception, than at witnessing the crimes and errors of a hundred thousand souls on the road to hell, about whom there can be no question as to whether they are saints or sinners, for any one can see, a thousand leagues off, that Satan has them in his clutches. Still the world is right, for the devil entraps so very few who say the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"; the central prayer of Christianity. This book is structured as a line-by-line commentary on this prayer.</note> with devotion, that when he succeeds, men are startled and wonder at it, as at something strange and unheard of! For, as a rule, people think little of every-day sights, but anything new or uncommon strikes them with surprise. The evil spirits, too, incite them to take scandal

<summary>St. Teresa petitions God for protection against the devil's hidden traps and observes that the world is uniquely shocked when a person of prayer falls into error, while ignoring the widespread sins of others.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal Father, temptation, spiritual traps, prayer, perfection, Our Father, Satan, scandal, spiritual security</keywords>

<header>CHAPTER XL. HOLY FEAR.</header>

at this, for a single soul that reaches perfection snatches many others from their hands. And the thing itself is so strange that no wonder men marvel at it, for unless they have themselves to blame, souls travel far more safely by this way than by any other—just as those who watch the bull-fight behind the barrier do not run the same risk as others who expose themselves to the animal's horns. I heard some one make this comparison and it seems most appropriate. Do not be afraid, sisters, to walk in these paths—for there are many kinds of prayer; some minds profit by one sort and some by another, <note>Way of Perfection, chapter xvii.</note> as I told you. The way is a safe one: you will be freed more quickly from temptation if you are near our Lord than if you were far off. Beg and entreat this of Him, as you do every day, in the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note>.

## CHAPTER XL. <note>Valladolid edition, ch. xlii.; Escorial edition, ch. lxix.</note>

->**How, by always living in the love and fear of God, we shall be safe among these temptations. This chapter treats of fear.**<-

*1. Divine love and holy fear are our safeguards. 2. The love and fear of God are unmistakably shown. 3. Temptations experienced by contemplatives. 4. Apprehensions raised in their souls by Satan. 5. Weakness of human compared with divine love. 6. Signs of love for God. 7. The death of one who loves God, 8. Contrasted with that of a sinner.*

**I. GIVE** us, O our good Master, some safeguard against surprise in this most dangerous warfare. The weapons we must use, daughters—the ones that provide us with a defense—are love and fear. Love will make us quicken our steps, while fear will make us look where we are setting our feet so that we do not fall on a road where there are so many things to trip us up. If we have these two, we shall certainly not be deceived.

<summary>St. Teresa uses the metaphor of a bull-fight barrier to illustrate spiritual safety and introduces love and fear as the soul's primary defenses against temptation.</summary>
<keywords>holy fear, divine love, prayer, Our Father, spiritual warfare, St. Teresa of Avila, bull-fight metaphor, temptation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the opening of Chapter 40, moving from the introduction of the weapons of spiritual warfare to a detailed description of how love and fear manifest in the soul. St. Teresa emphasizes that while we cannot have absolute certainty of our spiritual standing, the presence of true love for God is clearly visible through one's actions and lack of interest in worldly status.</meta>

<header>THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAPTER 40]</header>

The weapons we may use, daughters, and which His Majesty <note>St. Teresa's characteristic way of referring to God.</note> has given us, are love and fear. Take this advice: it is not mine, but your Teacher's <note>Referring to Jesus Christ, particularly as the author of the Lord's Prayer.</note>. Try to keep them by you on your journey. Love will quicken your footsteps, and fear will make you look where you set your foot down, lest you should trip against the many stumbling-blocks on that road by which all men must pass in this life. Thus armed, you will be secure from pitfalls.

2. You will ask me: “Can I tell whether I possess these two *very, very*, great virtues?” You are right to ask, for there can be no absolutely certain proof of this; if we were sure we possessed charity <term>charity: from the Latin 'caritas,' meaning the highest form of love; a selfless, divine love for God and neighbor</term>, we should be sure we were in a state of grace <term>state of grace: a spiritual condition in which one is free from serious sin and in a right relationship with God</term>. But, sisters, there are some signs which are at once apparent, and which, as they say, even the blind can see. There is no secret about them; although you may not wish to hear them, they cry aloud, for few souls have these virtues in perfection: therefore they call the more for notice. The love and fear of God need not advertise themselves! They are two strong fortresses, from whence war is waged on the world and the devils. The soul that truly loves God loves all good, seeks all good, protects all good, praises all good, joins itself to good men, helps and defends them, and embraces all the virtues: it only loves what is true and worth loving. Do you think it possible that any one who loves God, cares, or can care, for vanities, or riches, or worldly things, or pleasures and honours?

<summary>St. Teresa identifies love and holy fear as the primary defenses for the soul and explains that true divine love is unmistakably revealed by a person's attraction to goodness and rejection of worldly vanity.</summary>
<keywords>love of God, holy fear, charity, state of grace, spiritual perfection, St. Teresa of Avila, spiritual warfare, vanity</keywords>

## ->CHAPTER 40: LOVE AND FEAR<-

Neither can such a soul quarrel nor feel envy, for it aims at nothing save pleasing its Beloved <note>referring to Christ as the divine spouse of the soul</note>. It dies with longing for His love and gives its life in striving how to please Him better. But a *hidden* love indeed! as if a real love for God could possibly be hidden! Look at St. Paul or the Magdalen <note>Mary Magdalene, traditionally viewed as the model of the contemplative life and passionate love for Jesus</note>—in three days he found he was sick with love, <note>original: "Acts ix. 19, 20." Referring to the account of Paul's conversion and immediate preaching in Damascus.</note> but she knew it the first day. And how sure they were of it! A love for God may be great or small: and it shows itself according to its strength. If it is weak, it is little seen, and if it is strong it appears more clearly; but small or great, love for God can never be concealed.

3. The illusions and temptations the devil plots against contemplatives <term>contemplatives: those who practice a form of prayer focused on a direct, wordless experience of God's presence</term> are numerous. <note>original reference: "Castle, M. iv. ch. iii. 10." Referring to the Fourth Mansions of St. Teresa’s work, The Interior Castle.</note> Such souls love fervently, or they would not be contemplatives, and this is plainly shown in many ways, for a large fire throws a bright and clear flame. If they are wanting in love, let them be apprehensive and think they have good cause for fear: they should try to find out what is amiss and pray fervently. They must be very humble and must ask God not to lead them into temptation, into which I fear they will fall if they have not this token. But if they are lowly and try to find out the truth—if they submit to their confessor <term>confessor: a priest who provides spiritual direction and hears a person's confession of sins</term> and are frank and outspoken with him, <note>original reference: "Castle, M. vi. ch. ix. 10." Referring to the Sixth Mansions of The Interior Castle.</note> then—God is faithful. Let them

4. But if you feel the love for God I have described, besides the fear of which I am about to speak, you may feel happy and at peace. In order to disturb the soul and to prevent it from enjoying these great graces <term>graces: divine favors or spiritual gifts bestowed by God</term>, the evil one will himself suggest, and will make other people arouse in you a thousand false fears, for if he cannot win you for himself at least he will try to snatch something from you and to cause some loss to souls who might have benefited greatly by believing that the favours shown to such a miserable wretch <note>St. Teresa frequently uses humble self-deprecation, referring to herself as a "wretch" to emphasize that God's grace is a gift, not earned by merit.</note> came from God: he will also suggest that it is impossible for such things to be, for nowadays we sometimes seem to forget His mercies in the past.

5. Do you fancy that the devil wins but little through these fears? No indeed, he gains immensely. He thus harms us in two well-known manners as well as in many other ways. First, he frightens those who listen to him from practising prayer, by making them dread deception: secondly, he deters many others from devoting themselves to God as they were inclined to...

6. You will be sure to discover this love immediately; indeed, I do not know how it could be concealed. They say it is impossible to hide a human love for some poor foolish man or woman, but that the more we strive to conceal it, the more clearly it is seen—although a thing so base, felt for a mere worm <note>A common 16th-century expression of humility, comparing the insignificance of a human being to the majesty of God.</note>, undeserving of any regard, does not merit the name of love, being founded upon nothingness: indeed I loathe to make the comparison. Then, could a love so strong, so just, ever growing while it lives, which never discovers any cause why it should cease, but finds so many reasons for its being, as does the love of God, built as it is on the firm foundation of a love which is returned—can such a love as this be hidden? 7. O God! what a vast contrast between the one love and the other must be felt by the soul that has tried them both! May His Majesty <note>St. Teresa's favorite title for God, reflecting His supreme authority and goodness.</note> give us grace to prove it before He takes us from this life! We may feel safe concerning the acquittal of our debts <note>Meaning the spiritual "payment" or penance required for one's sins.</note>: we shall not be going into a foreign country but into our father-land <term>father-land: a poetic term for Heaven, signifying the soul's true home and place of origin</term>, for it belongs to Him Whom we love so dearly, and Who loves us in return. For this affection excels all earthly fondness, because if we love Him, we are assured of His love for us.

## CHAPTER 40: CONTRASTS

...anguish of death, when it falls at once into the clutches of Satan <note>In the 16th century, the devil was viewed as a very literal, physical presence seeking to snatch souls at the moment of death.</note>? What a frightful fate! How it is torn and lacerated as it falls to hell! what a brood of different kinds of serpents <note>Serpents and snakes were common traditional symbols for the specific vices or demons that would torment the damned.</note> swarm around it! How appalling is the place and what a miserable reception! A self-indulgent person, such as are most of those of the lost, can hardly bear to pass the night in a bad inn <term>bad inn: a favorite metaphor of St. Teresa, representing the temporary and often uncomfortable nature of earthly life compared to the eternity of the afterlife</term>, yet here he will lodge for ever, *for ever*, to endless ages! 

What do you suppose will be the feelings of this unhappy soul? Let us not look for luxuries, daughters <note>St. Teresa is addressing her fellow nuns in the Discalced Carmelite order, whom she often calls her daughters.</note>: we are well enough off here—it is only one night in a bad inn—thank God! Let us force ourselves to do penance <term>penance: voluntary spiritual discipline or acts of devotion performed to express sorrow for sin</term> in this life. How sweet death will be to her who has expiated all her sins and who needs no purgatory <term>purgatory: in Catholic tradition, a state of final purification for those destined for heaven who still need to "pay the debt" of their sins</term>! She may begin to enjoy glory <note>A term used here for "glory" (original: "gloria"), meaning the direct experience of God's presence in Heaven.</note> even in this world, and will fear nothing, but will be in perfect peace. 

Although we should not attain to this, sisters, let us beg God, that if we must suffer pain after death, it may be where it may be endured willingly, with the hopes of deliverance, and where we shall forfeit neither His friendship nor His grace; and may He grant them us in this life, so that we may not fall into temptation unknowingly. Let us praise the Lord, and persevere in begging Him to keep us and all sinners in His hands, and not to lead us into these hidden temptations.

<summary>St. Teresa contrasts the terrifying death of a sinner with the peaceful death of a soul that has done penance, using her famous "bad inn" metaphor for earthly life.</summary>

<header>THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XLI.</header>

# CHAPTER 41<gloss>1</gloss>

->Treats of the fear of God, and that we ought to avoid venial sins.<-

*1. Fallacy of human affection. 2. Holy fear. 3. Inadvertent and deliberate sin. 4. How to obtain holy fear. 5. Our conduct when we possess it. 6. We must not repel others by our manners. 7. Disadvantages of being over-strict. 8. We must show cordiality to others. 9. Exhortations.*

1. HAVE I enlarged on this subject? Not half as much as I should like, for it is delightful to talk about the <term>love of God</term>—what will it be, then, to possess it? <note>St. Teresa often distinguishes between "talking" about spiritual matters and the "experience" of them, which she believes is a gift from God.</note> Do Thou, O Lord, bestow it on me: suffer me not to depart from this life until I care for nothing which it contains, and know what it is to love naught but Thee. Let me no longer say that I love anything else, nor give the name of love to what is nothingness, since all earthly things are false, for if the foundation shifts, how can the building stand? I do not know why we should feel surprised at people saying, “This man has treated me ungratefully,” “That person does not love me.” I laugh to myself when I hear such speeches. How else should he treat them, or why should any one love them? This will show you what the world is, for such love brings its own punishment with it, and the reason that you are tortured is that your will keenly resents your employing it in such child’s-play. <note>Teresa views human drama and the desire for social approval as "child's play" compared to the serious work of the soul's relationship with God.</note>

2. Now let us think about the <term>fear of God</term>, although

---

<gloss>1 The numbering of chapters varies between the original manuscripts; this is Chapter 43 in the Valladolid edition and Chapter 71 in the Escorial manuscript.</gloss>

<note>original: "Valladolid" and "Escorial" refer to the two primary surviving handwritten copies of this book, which Teresa revised several times.</note>

<term>love of God</term>: In Teresa’s writing, this is not just a feeling, but the "union of wills" where a person desires only what God desires.

<term>fear of God</term>: Often called "filial fear." It is not the fear of a slave for a master, but the loving anxiety of a child who does not want to hurt or offend a parent.

<term>venial sins</term>: In Catholic theology, these are "lesser" sins that do not destroy the relationship with God but weaken the soul’s fervor. Teresa warns that many small sins can lead to a great fall.

<summary>St. Teresa begins her teaching on the "fear of God," arguing that earthly disappointments are inevitable because human love is built on an unstable foundation. She encourages her readers to focus solely on divine love to avoid the emotional pain caused by the "child's play" of worldly relationships.</summary>

### CHAPTER 41 (Continued)
->**HOLY FEAR**<-

I regret that I have said nothing about the "love of this world," <note>Teresa refers here to "worldly love" or "attachments"—the preoccupation with social status, wealth, and the approval of others, which she views as the opposite of spiritual freedom.</note> for through my own failures I have learned exactly what it is, and I would have liked to explain it to you so that you might always keep yourselves free from it. However, I cannot do so now, as I would be straying too far from my main subject. 

The <term>fear of God</term> <note>In this context, "fear" does not mean being afraid of a tyrant, but rather a "holy awe" or a profound dread of hurting one's relationship with God.</note> is easily recognized by the person who possesses it and by those around her. Although at first it is not strong enough to be noticed by everyone, it develops gradually. I make an exception for certain people on whom, as I mentioned before, our Lord bestows such great favors that, in a short time, <margin>See *The Way of Perfection*, chapter 16, paragraphs 4 and 6; and *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 5, paragraph 3.</margin> they become rich in virtues and are raised to a high degree of prayer. <note>A "high degree of prayer" refers to the more advanced stages of mystical union where the soul feels a direct, felt presence of the Divine.</note> In their case, this fear of God becomes apparent immediately; otherwise, it increases day by day in stages. 

This holy fear, along with the love of God, is more clearly visible as a person reaches perfection, though the virtue of fear may be detected quite early. This is because whoever possesses it will abandon sin and the situations that lead to it, and will give up bad company. There are also other signs of it. But when souls, such as those I mentioned, are raised to <term>contemplation</term>, <note>Contemplation is a form of wordless, quiet prayer where the soul focuses entirely on God’s presence.</note> both their love and their fear of God become very evident, even in their outward behavior. 

If you watch such people closely, you will find that they are never careless. God upholds them so that, regardless of what they might gain by doing so, they would never willingly commit even a <term>venial fault</term>. <note>A venial fault or sin is considered a minor offense that weakens but does not break one’s relationship with God.</note> As for <term>mortal sin</term>, <note>A mortal sin is a grave action committed with full knowledge and intent that "kills" the life of grace in the soul.</note> they dread it like fire. These are the illusions <note>Teresa likely refers to "illusions" of safety or false spiritual security.</note> of which, sisters, I wish you to stand in fear. You...

<summary>St. Teresa describes how the "fear of God" manifests as a protective virtue that leads a person to avoid both major and minor sins. She emphasizes that while this fear grows gradually for most, it is instantly recognizable in those granted the gift of high contemplation.</summary>

...should beg God that temptation may not be strong enough to overcome you, but that He will give you the power to conquer it, for it can do you little or no harm while you keep a good conscience. <note>In St. Teresa’s writing, a "good conscience" refers to the state of being at peace with God, where one is not conscious of having committed any serious, unconfessed sins.</note> This is the important matter; this is the fear which I hope we shall never lose; this must be our defence.

3. Oh, if we have not offended God, how easy it is for us to control the minions and slaves of hell! <note>Teresa often refers to devils as "slaves," emphasizing that they have no power over a soul unless God allows it or the person consents to sin.</note>
->³ *Life*, ch. xxxi. 10.<-
<note>Teresa refers here to Chapter 31 of her autobiography, *The Book of Her Life*, where she describes her visions of demons and her realization that they are terrified of a soul that is truly surrendered to God.</note>
We must all serve Him in the end, whether we will or no—the devils by force and we with alacrity. <note>The word "alacrity" here implies a cheerful, prompt, and eager readiness to do God's will.</note> So that, if we are pleasing to Him, they will be kept within bounds and will be unable to harm us, however they may tempt and lay their snares for us; in fact, they will only leave us with more <term>merit</term> <note>In a spiritual context, "merit" refers to the spiritual growth or "reward" a person receives from God for persevering through trials or performing good deeds through His grace.</note>. Remember this caution and advice; it is most important for you not to neglect it until you have such a fixed determination not to offend God that you would rather forfeit a thousand lives than commit a <term>mortal sin</term> <note>A "mortal sin" is a grave offense against God's law that "kills" the life of grace in the soul and breaks one's relationship with Him.</note>. You must also be extremely vigilant about <term>venial faults</term> <note>"Venial faults" are smaller, less serious sins. While they do not break the relationship with God, Teresa warns they can weaken the soul's resolve and lead to larger failures.</note>, so that you would rather suffer persecution from the whole human race than fall into this offence. I am speaking about misdeeds that are committed with full consent, and which are deliberate, for who does not constantly lapse into inadvertent errors? But there is one <term>advertency</term> <note>Advertency refers to "paying attention" or acting with full deliberate awareness.</note> which is very deliberate and another so sudden, that to commit the sin and to know it seem one and the same thing, and we hardly realise what we...

<summary>Teresa explains that a soul in a state of grace has nothing to fear from the devil, as even temptations serve to increase one's spiritual merit. She urges her readers to develop a firm resolve to avoid both serious mortal sins and deliberate minor faults.</summary>

### CHAPTER XLI. DELIBERATE SINS.

...are about, although yet, to a certain extent, we are aware of it. But from wilfully committing any sin, however small, may God deliver us! I cannot think how we could dare to set ourselves against so great a **Sovereign** <note>In the 16th century, "Sovereign" referred to an absolute monarch. Teresa often uses royal imagery to remind her readers that God is the supreme King who deserves total loyalty.</note> in however small a matter, though no offence against such majesty can be called “small,” because we know that He is watching us. Such a fault seems to me thoroughly premeditated.<gloss>Teresa is referring here to her other book, *Conceptions of the Love of God*, chapter 2, paragraph 27.</gloss> It is as if we said: “Lord, although this displeases Thee, yet I shall do it. I know that Thou seest it and art angered at it: this I know, but I would rather follow my own fancies and desires than Thy will.” Is such a misdeed as this a little one? I think not; I think it is very, very serious.

4. For the love of God, sisters, always be as careful in this matter as, thank Him, you are at present. Much depends upon your cultivating the **habit of watchfulness** <note>This refers to the practice of "custody of the heart," or staying spiritually alert to one’s thoughts and motivations throughout the day.</note>. If you wish to gain this fear, it is important for you constantly to bear in mind how <term>heinous</term> <note>Heinous means shockingly evil or deeply offensive. Teresa uses strong language here to shake the reader out of complacency regarding "minor" sins.</note> a thing it is to offend God. This is a vital matter, especially if we can thus **graft** <note>A gardening term where a branch from one plant is joined to the trunk of another. Teresa suggests that we must "attach" this virtue to our souls so firmly that it becomes a natural part of our growth.</note> this virtue strongly in our souls. Until we possess it, we must act cautiously, keeping aloof from all places and persons that do not draw us to God. We must be careful to break our own will in whatever we do; we must take care that our words are **edifying** <note>From the Latin *aedificare*, meaning "to build up." Edifying conversation is talk that morally or spiritually improves the listener.</note>, and we must avoid places where the conversation is irreligious.

<summary>St. Teresa warns that choosing to commit even a small sin is a serious act of rebellion because it is done with the knowledge that God is watching. she urges her sisters to stay spiritually alert and avoid environments that might lead them away from God's will.</summary>

<keywords>deliberate sin, Sovereign, watchfulness, virtue, edifying speech, fear of God, spiritual discipline, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 281 regarding the cultivation of "holy fear." <page-num>282</page-num>

->THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XLI.<-

5. Great pains are required in order to root this **holy fear** <note>In St. Teresa’s writings, "holy fear" is not a paralyzing terror of punishment, but a profound "filial fear"—the kind of respectful awe a child has for a parent they deeply love and do not wish to disappoint.</note> deeply in the soul, although, when a genuine love is felt for God, He soon gives it, on seeing her firm determination not to commit even a **venial sin** <note>A venial sin is considered a "lesser" sin that does not break a person's relationship with God entirely, but still weakens the soul’s spiritual health.</note> for the sake of any created thing, and that not to avoid a thousand deaths would she offend Him,<gloss>5</gloss> although, in spite of this, such a one may fall into defects, for we are weak and cannot trust ourselves. The firmer are our resolutions the less ought we to confide in our own strength, for all our confidence must rest on God. When we find that we have this fixed resolve, we need not be so timid and strict with ourselves, for our Lord and the good habits we have formed will aid us not to offend Him. We may now act with a **holy liberty** <note>Holy liberty is the spiritual ease and confidence that comes when a soul is no longer plagued by "scrupulosity" (excessive anxiety about small faults). It allows the person to serve God with joy rather than fear.</note> and may associate with any one we meet. We may even prefer to be with the worldly, for, though, before we felt this genuine fear of God, they would have proved a poison destructive to our souls, yet now, when we see the contrast there is between us, their company will often strengthen our good resolutions and make us love God better and thank Him for having delivered us from danger. If, in the past, you fostered their weaknesses, now you will help them by forcing them to restrain themselves in your presence, for they will pay you this compliment unasked.

6. I often wonder why it is—and thank God for it—that the presence of a religiously minded person, even though

<summary>St. Teresa explains that once a soul is firmly committed to avoiding sin through "holy fear," it gains a spiritual freedom that allows for safe interaction with the world. This maturity turns potential worldly distractions into opportunities for gratitude and positive influence on others.</summary>

->CHAP. XLI.] COMMANDING RESPECT.<-

his silence frequently stops profanity.⁶ I suppose it must be the same as in human fellowship: people are careful not to speak ill of one who is absent when they are before those whom they know to be intimate with him. Since such a person as I speak of is in a **state of grace** <note>To be in a "state of grace" is a Catholic theological term meaning the soul is free from mortal sin and is in a living, friendly relationship with God.</note>, this must cause him to be respected, however low his station in life may be, and men will not give him the pain that they know an offence against God would cause him. To tell the truth, I do not know the exact reason, but it is generally the case. Do not be too rigid in your ways; for the soul to grow **timorous** <note>Timorous refers to a state of being overly fearful or timid; in spiritual terms, it describes an unhealthy anxiety about accidentally offending God.</note> will be a great hindrance in every way, and may sometimes lead to **scruples** <note>Scruples are a form of spiritual "OCD" where a person obsessively worries that innocent or minor actions are actually grievous sins, which "cripples" their spiritual progress.</note> which cripple it, both as regards itself and others. If things do not reach as far as this,

***

⁶ St. Teresa’s influence over the irreligious is shown by an incident which occurred when she was on her way to make the **foundation** <note>A "foundation" refers to the establishment of a new convent or monastery.</note> of Seville. She and the nuns were in the same field with some disorderly soldiers and other men who began to quarrel violently with one another. They drew their arms and began to fight. The sisters, terrified, ran to their Mother as chickens take refuge beneath their parent’s wings. The Saint said to the combatants: “My brothers, remember that you are in the presence of God, Who is to be your Judge.” Struck with horror at her words, the men ceased their conflict and took to flight. (*Ribera’s Life of St. Teresa*, book 4, chapter 21.)

When on her foundations, the holy Mother sometimes employed **mule-drivers** <note>Mule-drivers were essential for travel in 16th-century Spain, as Teresa journeyed across the country to establish her reformed convents.</note> who were given to swearing and bad language, from which they always abstained out of respect for her when she was present. They often said that nothing on earth gave them so much pleasure as listening to her conversation. (*In the same work* <note>original: "Ibid-m," an abbreviation for the Latin *ibidem*, meaning "in the same place."</note>, book 2, chapter 18.)

7. If they maintain an innocent cheerfulness, we think it is **dissipation** <note>In a spiritual context, "dissipation" refers to being scattered, unfocused, or wasting one's mental and spiritual energy on trivialities rather than on God.</note>. This is especially the case with ignorant people like ourselves who do not understand what subjects it is wrong to speak about. It is a bad frame of mind to be in, being very dangerous and a source of continual temptation, because it injures our neighbour. It is very wrong to suppose that because they are not so **scrupulous** <note>To be "scrupulous" is to suffer from "scruples"—an obsessive, unhealthy worry that one is constantly sinning in minor or even imaginary ways. Another disadvantage is that, in cases when it is our duty and our right to speak, we dare not do so, lest we should offend God, or we speak well of what we ought to hate.⁷

### CHAPTER 41: CORDIALITY

...to deal with, that they may take pleasure in your conversation and may wish to imitate your life and manners, instead of being frightened and deterred from **virtue** <note>Virtue: here meaning the habit of doing good and the pursuit of a moral, holy life.</note>. This is an important matter for nuns: the more holy they are, the more **cordial** <term>cordial: possessing a warm, friendly, and sincere heart; being approachable and pleasant rather than cold or distant.</term> they ought to be with their sisters. Although you may be pained because their conversation is not what you could wish, still, never keep aloof from them, for thus you will help them and win their love. We ought to try our best to be sociable and to humour and please those with whom we talk, especially when they are our sisters.

9. Try to realise, sisters, that God does not care for such **trifling matters** <note>This refers back to the "scruples" or obsessive worries about minor rules mentioned on the previous page. Teresa argues that God focuses on the heart rather than tiny, accidental mistakes.</note> as you suppose, and do not let these things alarm your soul and damp your courage, or you will lose greatly by it. Keep a pure intention and a firm resolve not to offend God, as I said, but do not trammel your soul, for instead of advancing in **sanctity** <note>Sanctity: the state of being holy or living in close union with God.</note> you would contract a number of imperfections which the devil would bring about in other ways, and you would not help others as you might have done. You see that, with these two things—the love and the fear of God—we can travel peacefully along the road without imagining...

<note>Historical Note 8: St. Teresa was known for her incredibly sweet disposition. She was so peaceful and pleasant that everyone who interacted with her felt drawn to her and sought her company. This was St. Teresa’s customary and respectful way of referring to Jesus Christ.</note>! <gloss>3</gloss> <note>St. Teresa elaborates on this in her other major work, *The Interior Castle*: "What must have been the feelings of our Lord Jesus Christ—and what must His life have been? She would cry to God, imploring Him to listen to her prayers, since prayer was her only resource, and beg Him to allow her to rescue at least one soul from among the many victims of the devil" (Ribera, Book 4, chapter 11).</note>

Well might He implore His Father to deliver Him from so many evils and trials and to place Him in peace in His kingdom, of which He was the rightful Heir. I think that as "Amen" is used at the end of all prayers, so our Lord means by it here, that we may be delivered from all evil for ever.

2. It would be hopeless, sisters, to think that while we are on earth we can be freed from numberless temptations, imperfections, and even sins, since Holy Scripture says: "If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves." <note>original Latin: "Si dixerimus quoniam peccatum non habemus, ipsi nos seducimus." Then, if we flee from bodily evils and sufferings—and who is without many a trial of the sort?—is it not right to ask to be delivered from the ills of the soul? Still, we must recognise that it is impossible for us to be delivered from every corporal evil or from imperfections and faults in God’s service. I am not speaking of the saints—they can "do all things in Christ," <note>original Latin: "Omnia possum in Eo qui me confortat," meaning "I can do all things in Him who strengthens me." <header>CHAPTER XLII.] PRAYER.</header>
<page-num>289</page-num>

...possess on earth, where we are destitute of all good, and absent from our Lord? Deliver me, O God, from this deadly nightmare; deliver me from the many labors, the frequent anguish, the numberless vicissitudes <note>The unpredictable changes and shifts of fortune or circumstances in life.</note>, the multitude of duties that devolve upon us in this world; from the many, many, *many* things that harass and that weary me, and that would weary any reader of this book were I to enumerate them. Life is made unbearable <gloss>7</gloss> by the loathing I feel at having led so bad a life and at the sight of its unworthiness even now, considering my indebtedness <note>The spiritual "debt" of sin or the sense of being unable to repay God's grace.</note>.

3. Therefore I beg of God to deliver me for ever from all evil since I cannot pay the score I owe, but, perhaps, only plunge deeper into debt each day. O God! unbearable are the uncertainties as to whether I love Thee or whether my desires are pleasing to Thee! My Lord and my God! deliver me from all evil and vouchsafe <note>To graciously grant or condescend to give.</note> to take me to where all good things are to be found. What do souls look for here, after Thou hast shown them in some degree the nothingness of this world, or when they have learnt it by experience, and have a lively faith in what the eternal Father is keeping in store for us because His Son asked Him to bestow it on us and has bidden us beg it for ourselves?

4. A passionate and steady longing for heaven is a certain sign for <term>contemplatives</term> <note>Those who practice deep, silent prayer focused on the direct experience of God’s presence.</note> that the <term>divine favours</term> <note>Special spiritual gifts or "touches" of grace that provide a sense of God's nearness.</note> they receive are truly from God, and that their prayer is genuine, for He is drawing their souls toward Him. Therefore, those who experience this should value it highly. But let no one suppose that *I* ask for heaven for this reason—it is only because my life has been so wicked that I am afraid of living any longer; besides, I am tired of carrying so many <term>crosses</term> <note>A common religious metaphor referring to the personal trials, sufferings, or burdens a person must endure.</note>. However, souls that receive these spiritual gifts may well desire to be in a place where they no longer taste them in mere "sips." Now that they know something of the greatness of God, they long to see it in its entirety. They do not wish to live among so many obstacles that block their enjoyment of this supreme good, but ache to be where the <term>Sun of righteousness</term> <note>original: "Sol iustitiae." A biblical title for Christ (from Malachi 4:2), describing Him as the source of all spiritual light and truth.</note> never sets. All earthly things seem dull to them from now on. I cannot understand how they can live another hour—at least, no one can ever feel content to do so once they have begun to find delight in God and have partaken in His kingdom here on earth, so that they no longer wish to do their own will, but that of their King.

5. Oh, how different must that life be, where one no longer aches for death! What a contrast between the direction of our wills and <term>God's will</term>! He wants us to love the truth, while we prefer what is false; He wants us to love what is <term>eternal</term> <note>That which exists outside of time and never ends.</note>—but we follow what is fleeting; He would have us care for the noble and <term>sublime</term> <note>Things of the highest spiritual or moral value.</note>—we only value what is lowly and earthly; He wants us to...

<summary>St. Teresa argues that a sincere desire for heaven is proof of genuine spiritual progress and describes the painful contrast between human desires and God's eternal truth.</summary>

# CHAP. XLII.] EARTH AND HEAVEN.

->291<-

should rest on what is safe, while we seek danger. All things are <term>vanity</term> <note>In this spiritual context, "vanity" refers to things that are temporary, empty, or lacking eternal value.</note>, my daughters, save to ask God to deliver us from these dangers for ever, and to deliver us from all evil. Although our wish for this may not be perfect, yet let us force ourselves to make the petition. What does it matter if we pray for great things—we are asking it of One Who is all-powerful? It would be an insult to ask a great emperor for a farthing <note>A British coin of very little value; Teresa uses this to show that we should not be afraid to ask God for "big" spiritual gifts rather than trivial earthly ones.</note>. But to make sure of obtaining our request, let us leave the choice of the gift to the will of God,<gloss>8</gloss> <note>original: "Excl. xvi." referring to Teresa’s work, *Exclamations of the Soul to God*, chapter 16.</note> since we have already yielded our will to Him. May His Name be for ever blessed, both in heaven and earth, and let His will be ever done in me! Amen.<gloss>9</gloss> <note>The Escorial manuscript version, chapter 73. This is the point where that specific version of the book concludes.</note>

6. Now you see, my friends, that to make <term>vocal prayer</term> <note>Prayers that are spoken aloud or recited using set words, such as the "Our Father," as distinguished from silent "mental prayer."</note> with perfection is to consider and to realise to Whom it is offered, who it is that makes it, and what is asked for. Do not be disheartened if people tell you that it is wrong to use any but vocal prayer. Read over very carefully all that I have written and beg God to teach you anything about the subject that you cannot understand. Nobody can hinder you from vocal prayer nor force you to say the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> hurriedly and thoughtlessly. If any one tries to prevent your prayer or advises you to give it up, do not trust what he says, but look

<summary>St. Teresa concludes her instructions on the Lord's Prayer, emphasizing that believers should boldly ask God for great spiritual deliverance while remaining submissive to His will. She defends the value of "vocal prayer," insisting it must be performed with mindfulness and focus on God.</summary>

# THE WAY OF PERFECTION. [CHAP. XLII.

->292<-

upon him as a false prophet. In these days, you cannot listen to everybody: if to-day some one tells you that you have nothing to fear, there is no knowing what he may say to-morrow. I meant to have explained how you should say the **Hail Mary** <note>original: "Ave Maria"; the central prayer to the Virgin Mary.</note>, but having enlarged my book so much, I must leave it. To know how to recite the **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> well, will show you how to say any other prayer.

<margin>^{10} *Way of Perfection, chapter 19, section 4.*</margin>

7. Now let us finish the journey which I have described. See, sisters, what trouble our Lord has saved me by teaching both you and myself "the way" I began to describe to you, by showing me how much we ask for when we say this <term>evangelical prayer</term> <note>The Lord's Prayer (Our Father), so called because it comes directly from the Gospels.</note>. May He be for ever praised, for assuredly the idea never entered my mind that it contained such sublime secrets. As you have seen, it comprises the whole **spiritual life**, from the very beginning until God absorbs the soul into Himself and gives it to drink freely of the **fountain of living water** ^{10} <note>A metaphor Teresa uses throughout her works to describe the deep, satisfying experience of God's grace in advanced prayer.</note> which I told you was to be found at the end of the **pilgrimage**. To speak the truth, after having gone through this prayer, I cannot find any more to say. Our Lord seems to have wished to teach us what great **consolation** it contains. It is most useful for those who cannot read: if they understood it, they might gather from it much spiritual instruction and **solace**. If other books are taken from us, no one can deprive

<summary>Saint Teresa concludes her commentary on the Lord's Prayer, noting that it contains all the secrets of the spiritual journey and provides a complete guide for those seeking God, even if they cannot read other books.</summary>
<keywords>Hail Mary, Our Father, evangelical prayer, spiritual life, fountain of living water, pilgrimage, consolation, prayer for the illiterate</keywords>

### CHAPTER XLII. CONCLUSION.

->293<-

us of this, which came from the lips of the very Truth, Who cannot err. And since, as I said, we recite the <term>Our Father</term> <note>original: "Pater Noster"; the central prayer of the Christian faith, taught by Jesus to his disciples.</note> very often during the day, let us take delight in it, and let us strive to learn humility from the way in which our good Master prays, besides all the other things I have explained to you.

8. Beg of Him to pardon me for having dared to speak about such high matters. <term>His Majesty</term> <note>A respectful title Saint Teresa frequently used to refer to God.</note> knows well that I should never have had the courage nor would my mind have been capable of it, unless He had taught me what to say. Now, sisters, I think our Lord does not wish me to continue, though I intended writing more. Our Lord has taught both you and me the way I have described in the book which, as I have said, I have written (the *Life* <note>Referring to *The Life of Saint Teresa of Avila by Herself*, her first major work detailing her spiritual experiences.</note>). I have told you how to journey to this <term>fount of living water</term> <note>A metaphor for the deep, refreshing experience of God’s grace and the higher stages of contemplative prayer.</note> and what the soul feels when it is attained: how God satiates the spirit, deprives it of all thirst for earthly things, and strengthens it in His service. That book will be a great help to those who feel called to such a state and will give them much light. Ask for it, for the Father-Master, <term>Dominic Bañes</term> <note>A famous Spanish Dominican theologian who served as Teresa's confessor and advisor, helping her navigate the Inquisition.</note>, who is my confessor, has it; I shall also give him this before showing it to you; should he think that you would be benefited by having it, and also give you the former, I should feel happy at having given you any comfort. If this work is unworthy of being read, you must take the will for

<summary>Saint Teresa concludes her commentary on the Lord's Prayer, emphasizing humility and directing her sisters to her previous autobiography for deeper spiritual instruction. She submits her writing to her confessor, Dominic Bañes, for approval.</summary>

...the deed, as I have done my best to obey your request.
